the inverse different of the ring of integers of
L
and
I_{+}
the standard positivity notion. To see this take
T=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/\nu])
with
\nu
as in Theorem A and consider the complement of
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
which is a finite set of rational primes. The proof of Theorem
4.1
can be found in Section
10
, and we refer to the introduction for an outline of the main ideas used in the proof.

The variety
Y(2)
.

We next give a first application of Theorem
4.1
. Consider the coarse moduli scheme
Y(2)
over
\mathbb{Z}[1/2]
of the moduli problem
\mathcal{P}(2)
of principal level 2-structures on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/2]}
. We shall deduce in Section
10.3
the following result.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
Y
be a variety over
\mathbb{Z}
. Suppose that there exists a nonempty open
T\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/2])
such that
Y_{T}\cong Y(2)_{T}
and put
U=S\cap T
. Then the following holds.

(i)

Any point
P\in Y(S)
satisfies
h_{\phi}(P)\leq c_{1}N_{U}^{e_{1}}.

(ii)

The cardinality of

Y(S)
is at most
c_{2}N_{U}^{e_{2}}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}.

In applications of Corollary
4.2
, such as for example the Corollary stated in the introduction, one usually chooses
T
such that
Y(2)
is smooth over
T
. Corollary
5.6
gives that
Y(2)
is smooth over
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2\Delta}])
, and if we take
T=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2\Delta}])
then (the proof of) Corollary
4.2
provides that each point
P\in Y(S)
satisfies

h_{\phi}(P)\leq c_{1}(\Delta N_{S})^{e_{1}}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad|Y(S)|\leq c_{2}(\Delta N_{S})^{e_{2}}.

We now consider the coarse Siegel moduli space
A_{2,2}
of principally polarized abelian surfaces with symplectic level two structure. For certain
S
, Le Fourn
[
LF17
,
LF19b
,
LF19a
]
and Box–Le Fourn
[
BLF20
]
proved strong explicit height bounds for the
S
-integral points of
A_{2,2}
outside the divisor
Z
of products of elliptic curves. Their results hold for arbitrary number fields, and their proofs use and refine Levin’s approaches via Runge’s method
[
Lev08
,
Lev18
]
or via the theory of logarithmic forms
[
BW07
,
Lev14
]
. There are many coarse Hilbert moduli schemes
Y
which admit a finite morphism
Y\to A_{2,2}
. However, usually the image of
Y\to A_{2,2}
intersects the divisor
Z
and one can not directly deduce Diophantine results for
Y
via the variety
A_{2,2}\setminus Z
. For example, for certain
Y
related to
Y(2)
, forgetting
\iota:\mathcal{O}\to\textup{End}(A)
induces a canonical finite morphism
Y\to A_{2,2}
whose image intersects the divisor
Z
. On the other hand, Corollary
4.2
gives no information for
S
-integral points of
A_{2,2}\setminus Z
defining abelian varieties
A
with
\textup{End}(A)=\mathbb{Z}
.

Section 5
Moduli problems and coarse moduli spaces

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a Hilbert moduli stack. Throughout this section we work over an arbitrary nonempty open subscheme
B\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
, and we write throughout
\mathcal{M}
for
\mathcal{M}_{B}
to simplify notation. We now begin to prove some basic properties of moduli problems on
\mathcal{M}
and their coarse moduli spaces over
B
which shall be used in our proofs below.

Products.

Let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be moduli problems on
\mathcal{M}
, and let
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}
be the product presheaf on
\mathcal{M}
. For several computations we shall need to explicitly identify
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
with
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
. On using the description of the fiber product in
[
Sta
, 0040]
, we see that one obtains an equivalence of categories fibered in groupoids over
(\textnormal{Sch}/B)

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}

(5.1)

by sending an object
(U,(x,\alpha),(y,\beta),f)
to the object
(x,\alpha,\mathcal{Q}(f)\beta)
and by sending a morphism
(a,b)
to the morphism determined by
a
.
It turns out that one obtains an inverse
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
to (
5.1
) by sending an object
(x,\alpha,\beta)
to the object
(U,(x,\alpha),(x,\beta),\textnormal{id})
and by sending a morphism
f
to the morphism determined by
f
. This inverse and (
5.1
) are both morphisms of categories over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is the first projection and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is the forgetful morphism
(x,\alpha,\beta)\mapsto(x,\alpha)
.

Group action on a moduli problem.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a moduli problem on
\mathcal{M}
and let
G
be a group. An action of
G
on
\mathcal{P}
is a morphism of presheaves
G\times\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
such that
G\times\mathcal{P}(x)\to\mathcal{P}(x)
defines a
G
-action on
\mathcal{P}(x)
for each
x\in\mathcal{M}
. Here we view
G
as a constant presheaf on
\mathcal{M}
. Suppose that
G
acts on
\mathcal{P}
. Then for each
g\in G
one obtains an automorphism
\tau_{g}
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
by sending an object
(x,\alpha)
to the object
(x,g\alpha)
and by sending a morphism
(x,\alpha)\to(x^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
to the morphism
(x,g\alpha)\to(x^{\prime},g\alpha^{\prime})
which lies over the same morphism
x\to x^{\prime}
. Furthermore, the automorphism
\tau_{g}
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is compatible with the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
and we observe that

G\to\textup{Aut}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}/\mathcal{M}),\quad g\mapsto\tau_{g},

(5.2)

is compatible with the group structures on
G
and
\textup{Aut}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}/\mathcal{M})
where
\textup{Aut}(\cdot)
is taken in the (2,1)-category of categories fibered in groupoids over
\mathcal{M}
.
We now consider the case when
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{I}
is associated to
(L,I,I_{+})
,
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}(n)
is the moduli problem of principal level
n
-structures for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and
G={\textup{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
for
\mathcal{O}
the ring of integers of
L
. In this case we obtain an action of
G
on
\mathcal{P}
by defining

G\times\mathcal{P}(x)\to\mathcal{P}(x),\quad(g,\alpha)\mapsto\alpha g^{-1}.

(5.3)

Here
x=(A,\iota,\varphi)
lies in
\mathcal{M}(S)
for some
B
-scheme
S
and
\alpha g^{-1}
denotes the composition of
\alpha:(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})^{2}_{S}\overset{\sim}{\to}A_{n}
with the automorphism of
(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})^{2}_{S}
defined by
g^{-1}\in{\textup{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
. In what follows, the action of
{\textup{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
on
\mathcal{P}(n)
always refers to (
5.3
).

Some geometric properties.

Let
Y
be a coarse moduli scheme over
B
of an arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
. Suppose that
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
is an initial morphism. Then
\pi
has the following properties:
It is a universal homeomorphism, it is proper and quasi-finite, and its formation is compatible with flat base change
Y^{\prime}\to Y
of algebraic spaces over
B
.
See for example Conrad’s approach to the Keel–Mori theorem
[
KM97
]
via stacks
[
Con05
]
. To this end, we notice that the separated finite type DM stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
over
B
satisfies all assumptions made in
[
Con05
, Thm 1.1]
: The diagonal of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
over
B
is finite since the separated stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
over
B
is quasi-DM, and therefore the inertia stack of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is finite over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
. (Here we used that a morphism of algebraic spaces is finite if it is proper and locally quasi-finite: This holds for schemes and hence for algebraic spaces by
[
Sta
, 03XX]
.)
Furthermore, as
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is separated finite type over
B
and
\pi
is surjective, it follows from
[
Con05
, Thm 1.1]
that
Y
is a variety over
B
.

Galois action on algebraic points.

Let
Y
be a coarse moduli scheme over
B
of an arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
. Then
Y
is automatically a variety over
B
. The absolute Galois group
G=\textnormal{Aut}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
acts on the
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-points of
Y
by precomposition. This induces an action of
G
on the set of isomorphism classes
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})]
via transport of structure involving the bijection
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})]\overset{\sim}{\to}Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
defined by an initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
. Then, on using the canonical identification
Y(\mathbb{Q})\overset{\sim}{\to}Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})^{G}
, we obtain

Y(\mathbb{Q})\overset{\sim}{\to}[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})]^{G}.

(5.4)

Further it turns out that
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})]^{G}
is the set of isomorphism classes of
(x,\alpha)
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
such that for each
\sigma\in G
the object
(x,\alpha)
is isomorphic to its pullback
\sigma^{*}(x,\alpha)
by the automorphism
\sigma
of
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
. Indeed this follows from a formal computation using that the bijection
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})]\overset{\sim}{\to}Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
is induced by
\pi
which is a morphism of categories over
(\textnormal{Sch}/B)
and that the pullback of an object is unique up to isomorphism.

Compatibility with base change.

The formation of the height of coarse moduli schemes is compatible with base change to a nonempty open
T\subseteq B
. More precisely let
Y
be a coarse moduli scheme over
B
of an arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
with an initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
and associated height
h_{\phi}
, and denote by
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
the restriction of
\mathcal{P}
to the open substack
\mathcal{M}_{T}
of
\mathcal{M}
. Then
Y_{T}
is a coarse moduli scheme over
T
of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with initial morphism
\pi_{T}:(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to Y_{T}
and associated height
h_{\phi^{\prime}}
such that

h_{\phi^{\prime}}=\iota^{*}h_{\phi}.

(5.5)

Here
\iota:Y_{T}\hookrightarrow Y
is the projection and we identified
(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
with
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{B}T
by sending an object
(x,\alpha)
to the object
(U,(x,\alpha),U\to T,\textnormal{id})
and by sending a morphism
f
to the morphism
(f,p(f))
where
U=p(x,\alpha)
and
p:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to B
. We now prove the statements in (
5.5
). The formation of coarse moduli spaces commutes with flat base change and
\iota
is an open immersion. Thus the base change
\pi_{T}
of
\pi
is indeed an initial morphism. Then we see that (
5.5
) follows from the (functorial) definitions of
h_{\phi}
and
h_{\phi^{\prime}}
, since the above described identification
(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{B}T
is an isomorphism of categories over
\mathcal{M}
.

Extension.

Let
T\subseteq B
be nonempty open and let
Y
be a coarse moduli scheme over
T
of an algebraic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}_{T}
with initial morphism
\pi:(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
. One can always naively extend
Y
to a coarse moduli scheme over
B
as follows: Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be the presheaf on
\mathcal{M}
which sends an object
x
to the set
\mathcal{P}(x)
if
x
lies in the open substack
\mathcal{M}_{T}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
and to the empty set otherwise. Then the composition

\pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\to}(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y

(5.6)

is initial among all morphisms to an algebraic space
Z
over
B
, since
\pi
is initial over
T
and since any morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to Z
factors uniquely through the open immersion
Z_{T}\hookrightarrow Z
. Here
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\to}(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}}
is the isomorphism of categories over
(\textnormal{Sch}/B)
given by the identity functor. In particular
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is algebraic, and
Y
is a coarse moduli scheme of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with initial morphism
\pi^{\prime}
and branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}=B_{\mathcal{P}}
. Furthermore
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is arithmetic if
\mathcal{P}
is arithmetic.

5.1
Construction of covers

We continue our notation and terminology. In particular we continue to work over an arbitrary nonempty open subscheme
B\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
and we write
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{B}
.

Let
Y
be a coarse moduli scheme over
B
of an arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
. To construct various covers, let
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
be an initial morphism and take a
B
-scheme
Z
with a surjective morphism
Z\to\mathcal{M}
. Now, we consider the algebraic stack over
B

Y^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}Z,\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y

where
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is the projection. The composition
Y^{\prime}\to Y
is surjective and
Y^{\prime}
is an algebraic space over
B
as we shall see in the proof of the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

If
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is finite, then
Y^{\prime}\to Y
is finite surjective and
Y^{\prime}
is a scheme.

Proof.

We first observe that the morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
is always surjective. Indeed it factors as
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y
where the projection
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a base change of the surjective
Z\to\mathcal{M}
and the initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
is a homeomorphism.

The forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
might not be representable in algebraic spaces. To prove that the algebraic stack
Y^{\prime}
is an algebraic space, it suffices by
[
Sta
, 04SZ]
to show that any object of
Y^{\prime}
has trivial automorphism group. This is a direct computation: Let
U
be a scheme, let
y=(U,(x,\alpha),z,f)
be an object of the fiber product
Y^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}Z
and let
g=(a,b)
be in
\textup{Aut}_{Y^{\prime}(U)}(y)
. We obtain that
b=1
since any object of
Z(U)
has trivial automorphism group. This together with
fa^{\prime}=b^{\prime}f
implies that
a^{\prime}=f^{-1}b^{\prime}f=1
, where
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
are the images of
a
and
b
under
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
and
Z\to\mathcal{M}
respectively. It follows that
a=1
since any automorphism of
(x,\alpha)
is determined by its underlying automorphism of
x
, and thus
g=(a,b)=1
. We conclude that
Y^{\prime}
is an algebraic space.

Now, we assume that the morphism
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is finite. Then its base change
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is finite. Further, the moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
is arithmetic by assumption and therefore the initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
is proper and quasi-finite. Thus the composition
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y
is separated and locally quasi-finite, and then
[
Sta
, 03XX]
implies that the algebraic space
Y^{\prime}
is a scheme since
Y
is a scheme. This completes the proof.∎

Finite étale cover of
Y\setminus B_{\mathcal{P}}
.

We now consider the open subscheme
U=Y\setminus B_{\mathcal{P}}
of
Y
where
B_{\mathcal{P}}
is the branch locus of
\mathcal{P}
. Let
S
be a connected Dedekind scheme with function field
k
. For any
P\in U(S)
we obtain cartesian squares

\textstyle{S\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y.}

If the surjective morphism
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is finite, then we get here a diagram of noetherian schemes with all vertical arrows finite surjective. This follows from Lemma
5.1
and our assumption that
\mathcal{P}
is arithmetic which assures that
Y=M_{\mathcal{P}}
is noetherian.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that the surjective morphism
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is finite étale, and let
T
be a connected component of
S\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

The morphisms
Y^{\prime}_{U}\to U
and
T\to S
are finite étale.

(ii)

The scheme
T
is a connected Dedekind scheme.

(iii)

If in addition
Z
is a moduli scheme over
B
of some moduli problem
\mathcal{Q}
on
\mathcal{M}
and
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is up to an equivalence
Z\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
the forgetful morphism, then

[k(T):k]\leq|\mathcal{Q}|_{\bar{k}}.

Proof.

The surjective morphism
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is finite by assumption. Thus, as already explained, the above displayed cartesian squares form a diagram of noetherian schemes with all vertical arrows finite surjective. In particular the scheme
S^{\prime}=S\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
is locally noetherian and hence the flat closed immersion
T\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}
, induced by the canonical scheme structure on the connected component
T
of
S^{\prime}
, is an open immersion.

We now show (i). The above discussion shows that the morphisms
Y^{\prime}_{U}\to U
and
T\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}\to S
are all finite. To see that they are étale, we denote by
f
the morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
and we let
U^{\prime}\subseteq Y^{\prime}
be the étale locus of
f
. As
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is finite étale by assumption, we can apply (
5.9
) below which gives that
B_{\mathcal{P}}
is precisely the branch locus
f(Y^{\prime}\setminus U^{\prime})
of
f
. This together with
U=Y\setminus B_{\mathcal{P}}
shows that
f^{-1}(U)\subseteq U^{\prime}
and hence
Y^{\prime}_{U}\to U
is étale. Thus the base change
S^{\prime}\to S
of
Y^{\prime}_{U}\to U
is also étale and then the composition
T\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}\to S
is étale since
T\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}
is an open immersion. This proves (i).

We next show (ii). An application of (i) gives that
T
is finite étale, and thus smooth of relative dimension zero, over the regular noetherian scheme
S
of dimension one. This implies that
T
is again a regular noetherian scheme of dimension one. In other words the connected component
T
is a connected Dedekind scheme as claimed in (ii).

To show (iii) we relate the degree of various morphisms to the number of points in the fiber
Y^{\prime}_{\bar{s}}
of the morphism
Y^{\prime}_{U}\to U
over the geometric point
u:\textnormal{Spec}(\bar{k})\to S\to^{P}U
. For this purpose we use that
Y^{\prime}_{U}\to U
and
T\to S
are finite étale by (i) and that the (locally constant) degree of a finite and locally free morphism is stable under arbitrary base change. Now, the degree
[k(T):k]
of the function field
k(T)
of
T
over the function field
k=k(S)
of
S
coincides with the degree of
T\to S
which is at most the degree of
S^{\prime}\to S
. Let
n
be the degree of
Y^{\prime}_{U}\to U
over the connected component
V
of
U
containing
u
. The morphism
P:S\to U
factors as
S\to V\hookrightarrow U
, since
S
is connected and
V
is open in the noetherian
U\subseteq Y
. Then, on using that the degree is stable under base change, we obtain that the degree of
S^{\prime}\to S
equals
n
and that
n
coincides with the degree
[Y^{\prime}_{u}:\bar{k}]
of the projection
Y^{\prime}_{u}\to\textnormal{Spec}(\bar{k})
. Combining everything leads to

[k(T):k]\leq n=[Y^{\prime}_{u}:\bar{k}].

(5.7)

We now assume in addition that
Z
is a moduli scheme over
B
of some moduli problem
\mathcal{Q}
on
\mathcal{M}
and that
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is the composition of an equivalence
Z\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
with the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}
. To bound
[Y^{\prime}_{u}:\bar{k}]
in terms of
\mathcal{Q}
, we use that the degree
[Y^{\prime}_{u}:\bar{k}]
coincides with the cardinality of the set of sections of the finite étale morphism
Y^{\prime}_{u}\to\textnormal{Spec}(\bar{k})
. This set of sections can be computed as follows. By construction the equivalence
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y^{\prime}
defined right after (
5.1
) is a morphism of categories over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
. Then, after recalling that
Y^{\prime}\to Y
factors as
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y
, we see that taking isomorphism classes of objects over
\bar{k}
gives a commutative diagram

\textstyle{[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}(\bar{k})]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \ \ \,\sim}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}(\bar{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \,\sim}
\textstyle{Y(\bar{k}).}

(5.8)

Now, the set of sections of
Y^{\prime}_{u}\to\textnormal{Spec}(\bar{k})
identifies with the fiber of
Y^{\prime}(\bar{k})\to Y(\bar{k})
over
u\in Y(\bar{k})

which in turn identifies via the above diagram with the set of isomorphism classes
[(x,\alpha,\beta)]
in
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}(\bar{k})]
such that
\pi([(x,\alpha)])=u
. The number of these isomorphism classes is at most

|\mathcal{Q}(x)|
since they are generated by
(x,\alpha,\beta)
with
\beta\in\mathcal{Q}(x)
. (Here the number of these isomorphism classes can be strictly smaller than
|\mathcal{Q}(x)|
; for example this is the case when
(x,\alpha)
has a non-trivial automorphism.) Finally, on putting everything together, we conclude that the degree
[Y^{\prime}_{u}:\bar{k}]
is at most
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}(\bar{k})}|\mathcal{Q}(x)|=|\mathcal{Q}|_{\bar{k}}
and then (
5.7
) implies (iii). This completes the proof of the lemma. ∎

Branch locus.

Suppose that the surjective morphism
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is finite étale. Then we can compute the branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}}
of
\mathcal{P}
in terms of the branch locus of
Y^{\prime}\to Y
which is defined as
B_{f}=f(Y^{\prime}\setminus U^{\prime})
, where
f
denotes the morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
and
U^{\prime}
is the union of all open subspaces of
Y^{\prime}
over which
f
is étale. It turns out that

B_{\mathcal{P}}=B_{f}.

(5.9)

To prove this equality, we denote by
U_{\mathcal{P}}
and
U
the complement in
Y
of
B_{\mathcal{P}}
and
B_{f}
respectively. The base change
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
of
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is again étale surjective, and Lemma
5.1
gives that
f
is a finite surjective morphism of schemes. In particular
U\subseteq Y
is open and
f_{U}
is étale since
f^{-1}(U)\subseteq U^{\prime}
. Therefore, as
f_{U}
factors as
Y^{\prime}_{U}\to(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}})_{U}\to^{\pi_{U}}U
with
Y^{\prime}_{U}\to(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}})_{U}
étale surjective, we obtain that
\pi_{U}
is étale and thus
U\subseteq U_{\mathcal{P}}
.
To prove the converse inclusion, we take an open
V\subseteq Y
such that
\pi_{V}
is étale. Thus
f_{V}
, which is the composition of
\pi_{V}
with the étale
Y^{\prime}_{V}\to(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}})_{V}
, is also étale and hence
f^{-1}(V)\subseteq U^{\prime}
. Then, on writing
V=Y\setminus f(Y^{\prime}\setminus f^{-1}(V))
which exploits that
f
is surjective, we deduce that
V\subseteq Y\setminus f(Y^{\prime}\setminus U^{\prime})=U
and thus
U_{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq U
. This completes the proof of (
5.9
).

Further, we remark here that the formation of the branch locus is compatible with the base change to a nonempty open subscheme
T\subseteq B
: The branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
of the coarse moduli scheme
Y_{T}
over
T
of the restriction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{M}_{T}
is given by

B_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}=(B_{\mathcal{P}})_{T}.

(5.10)

This follows for example from the arguments of the above proof of (
5.9
) after recalling from (
5.5
) that the flat base change
\pi_{T}
of
\pi
is an initial morphism
(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to Y_{T}
.

Height on
Y^{\prime}
.

Suppose that
Z
is a moduli scheme over
B
of some moduli problem
\mathcal{Q}
on
\mathcal{M}
, and assume that the surjective morphism
Z\to\mathcal{M}
is finite and is the composition of an equivalence
Z\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
with the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}
. Then Lemma
5.1
and (
5.1
) give that
Y^{\prime}
is a moduli scheme over
B
of the product moduli problem
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}
on
\mathcal{M}
. Now, let
h_{\phi^{\prime}}
be the height on
Y^{\prime}
defined in (
3.2
) with respect to the inverse of the equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
described right after (
5.1
).
A formal computation shows

h_{\phi^{\prime}}=f^{*}h_{\phi}

(5.11)

where
f
denotes the morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
and
h_{\phi}
is the height on
Y
defined in (
3.2
) with respect to
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
. Here we used that the equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
given in (
5.1
) is by construction a morphism of categories over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

5.2
Automorphism groups of objects of Hilbert moduli stacks

Let
\mathcal{M}^{I}
be the Hilbert moduli stack associated to some
(L,I,I_{+})
and let
S
be a connected scheme. We denote by
\mathcal{C}
the category of
\mathcal{O}
-abelian schemes over
S
for
\mathcal{O}
the ring of integers of the totally real field
L
. For any
\mathcal{O}
-abelian scheme
B
over
S
we write
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(B)=\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(B)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
. The goal of this section is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\sigma
be an automorphism of an object
(A,\iota,\varphi)
of
\mathcal{M}^{I}(S)
, and denote by
L(\sigma)
the
\mathbb{Q}
-subalgebra of
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
generated by
\sigma
and
\iota(\mathcal{O})
. Then
L(\sigma)
is a field which is isomorphic either to
L
or to a CM field with maximal totally real subfield
L
.

At the end of this section, we shall deduce this result from the next Lemma
5.4
. We remark that in the case when
S
has a point whose residue field is of characteristic zero, a refinement of Lemma
5.4
(ii) will be given in Lemma
8.4
below.

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that
A
and
A^{\prime}
are
\mathcal{O}
-abelian schemes over
S
which are both of relative dimension
g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

Any nonzero element of
\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,A^{\prime})
is an isogeny.

(ii)

The
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is finite and a division algebra.

In the case when
S
is in addition of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
, Lemma
5.4
(i) can be found in van der Geer’s book
[
vdG88
, X.1.6]
. His arguments rely on a trick of Drinfeld and they are completely different to our proof which exploits that
L
has a real embedding.

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

To prove (i) we take a nonzero
f\in\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,A^{\prime})
. For each point
s
of the connected scheme
S
, the base change
f_{s}:A_{s}\to A^{\prime}_{s}
is a morphism of
\mathcal{O}
-abelian varieties of dimension
g
which is again nonzero by rigidity. Thus, to show that
f
is an isogeny, we may and do assume that
S
is the spectrum of a field
k
. Then the image
B=\textnormal{Im}(f)
is an abelian subvariety of
A
. As
f
is compatible with the
\mathcal{O}
-multiplications, we obtain a ring morphism
\mathcal{O}\to\textup{End}(B)
which induces
L\hookrightarrow\textup{End}^{0}(B)
.
Next we use the following basic result
(
[
Lan83
, 1.3.1]
): (1) Let
C
be an abelian variety over
k
and let
F
be a number field contained in
\textup{End}^{0}(C)
. If
[F:\mathbb{Q}]>\dim(C)
then
F
is totally imaginary with
[F:\mathbb{Q}]=2\dim(C)
. Applying this with
C=B
and
F=L
gives that
\dim(A^{\prime})=g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
is at most
\dim(B)
since
L
is totally real. Thus the abelian subvariety
B
of
A^{\prime}
has the same dimension as
A^{\prime}
. This implies that
A^{\prime}=B=\textnormal{Im}(f)
. As
A
and
A^{\prime}
have the same dimension, it follows that
f
is an isogeny. This proves (i).

We now show (ii). To reduce to the case when
S
is the spectrum of a field, we take
s\in S
and we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{s}
the category of
\mathcal{O}
-abelian varieties over
k(s)
. Base change from our connected scheme
S
to
k(s)
induces a ring morphism
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)\to\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}_{s}}(A_{s})
which is injective by rigidity. Thus the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}_{s}}(A_{s})
are finite, since they embed into the finite
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{s})
. Next we observe: (2) If
R
is a not necessarily commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra which is finite and a division algebra, then any
\mathbb{Q}
-subalgebra
R^{\prime}
of
R
is a division algebra. (Indeed multiplication with a nonzero
r\in R^{\prime}
defines a
\mathbb{Q}
-module morphism
m_{r}:R^{\prime}\to R^{\prime}
which is injective since
R
is a division algebra and hence
m_{r}
is surjective since
R^{\prime}
is finite over
\mathbb{Q}
.) An application of (2) with
R^{\prime}
the image of
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
in
R=\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}_{s}}(A_{s})
shows that we may and do assume that
S
is the spectrum of a field
k
. Then any nonzero
f\in\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is an isogeny of abelian varieties over
k
by (i). Hence there is an isogeny
f^{\prime}:A\to A
such that
ff^{\prime}=[d]=f^{\prime}f
for
d
the degree of
f
. It follows that
f^{\prime}
lies in
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and
g=f^{\prime}\otimes d^{-1}
is an inverse of
f\otimes 1
in
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
. As any element of
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is of the form
f\otimes q
with
f\in\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, we deduce that
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is a division algebra as claimed in (ii). This completes the proof.∎

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Let
\sigma
be an automorphism of an object
(A,\iota,\varphi)
of
\mathcal{M}^{I}(S)
and recall that
L(\sigma)
denotes the
\mathbb{Q}
-subalgebra of
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
generated by
\sigma
and
\iota(\mathcal{O})
. We first show that
L(\sigma)
is a number field. Lemma
5.4
(ii) gives that the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is finite and a division algebra. Thus its
\mathbb{Q}
-subalgebra
L(\sigma)
is also a division algebra by (2) from the proof of Lemma
5.4
(ii). Furthermore, on using that
\iota(\mathcal{O})
is contained in the center of
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, we see that
L(\sigma)
is commutative. Hence
L(\sigma)
is a number field.

To compute the field
L(\sigma)
, we write
g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
and we take
s\in S
. Now we apply (1) from the proof of Lemma
5.4
(i) with
C=A_{s}
and
F
the image of
L(\sigma)\subseteq\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A)\subseteq\textup{End}^{0}(A)
inside
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{s})
. This shows that
L(\sigma)
either has degree at most
\dim(A_{s})=g
or
L(\sigma)
is totally imaginary of degree
2g
. Therefore, as
L(\sigma)
contains the totally real field
L^{\prime}=\iota(\mathcal{O})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
of degree
[L^{\prime}:\mathbb{Q}]=g
, we find that either
L(\sigma)=L^{\prime}
or
L(\sigma)
is a CM field with maximal totally real subfield
L^{\prime}
. Then we deduce Lemma
5.3
.
∎

5.3
The branch locus and automorphism groups

We now use the above covers to study automorphism groups of points in the branch locus of coarse moduli schemes. Let
\mathcal{M}
be a Hilbert moduli stack. We continue to work over an arbitrary nonempty open subscheme
B\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
and we write again
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{B}
.

Let
Y
be a coarse moduli scheme over
B
of an arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
, and let
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
be an initial morphism. We now introduce some terminology. For any point
y\in Y
, we denote by
\textup{Aut}(y)
the automorphism group algebraic space (
[
Sta
, 0DTR]
) of the point
\pi^{-1}(y)
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
and the separable rank of
\textup{Aut}(y)
is denoted by

|\textup{Aut}(y)|.

This rank coincides with the number of geometric points of
\textup{Aut}(y)
which is a finite group scheme over a field since the inertia stack of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is finite over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
. In particular
|\textup{Aut}(y)|
is an invariant of the point
\pi^{-1}(y)
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
. Further, a geometric automorphism group of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is the automorphism group of an object defined by a geometric point of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

The main goal of this subsection is to prove the following result which says that the points in the branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq Y
of
\mathcal{P}
have ‘large’ automorphism groups if a global congruence condition on the geometric automorphism groups is satisfied.

Proposition 5.5
.

Suppose that the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
is finite and that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is reduced. Further assume that each geometric automorphism group of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
has even order. Then any point
y\in B_{\mathcal{P}}
satisfies
|\textup{Aut}(y)|\geq 4
.

The first two assumptions are usually satisfied in situations of interest in arithmetic such as for example when
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
is finite étale, while the assumption on the geometric automorphism groups is more restrictive.
The proof given below shows that the assumptions can be relaxed up to a certain extent, see Remark
5.7
.

The information provided by Proposition
5.5
can be useful to compute the branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}}
. To illustrate this, we consider the coarse moduli scheme
Y(2)
over
\mathbb{Z}[1/2]
of the moduli problem
\mathcal{P}(2)
of principal level 2-structures on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/2]}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{M}^{I}
is associated to
(L,I,I_{+})
and write
\Delta
for the discriminant of
L/\mathbb{Q}
.

Corollary 5.6
.

The branch locus of
Y(2)
is empty, and
Y(2)
is smooth over
\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2\Delta}]
.

Proof.

We now take
B=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/2])
and
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}(2)
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
satisfies the first assumption in Proposition
5.5
. Indeed the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
is finite étale, which implies that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is reduced since
\mathcal{M}
is reduced. We now compute the geometric automorphism groups of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
. Let
\sigma
be an automorphism of an object
(x,\alpha)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(k)
where
\textnormal{Spec}(k)\to B
is a geometric point. After recalling that
\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{M}^{I}
is associated to
(L,I,I_{+})
, we identify
x
with a triple
(A,\iota,\varphi)\in\mathcal{M}^{I}(k)
. Then
\sigma
is induced by an automorphism of the abelian variety
A
over
k
whose restriction
\sigma_{2}
to
A_{2}
satisfies
\sigma_{2}\alpha=\alpha
as morphisms
(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})_{k}^{2}\to A_{2}
, where
\mathcal{O}
is the ring of integers of
L
. It follows that
\sigma_{2}
is the identity since
\alpha
is an isomorphism. Thus Serre’s lemma
[
GR72
, Lem 4.7.1]
shows that either
\sigma
is the identity or
\sigma
has order two. Hence Lemma
5.3
assures that
\sigma
is given by
[\pm 1]
. Thus the automorphism group of
(x,\alpha)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(k)
has order two, since
[-1]
defines an automorphism of
(x,\alpha)
. Now, as all geometric automorphism groups of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
have (even) order two, we can apply Proposition
5.5
which implies that
B_{\mathcal{P}}
is empty.

We now deduce the second part. The stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
has an étale scheme cover, the initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y(2)
is an étale cover since
B_{\mathcal{P}}
is empty, and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is smooth over
\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2\Delta}]
since the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
is étale and since
\mathcal{M}
identifies over
\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2\Delta}]
with the smooth stack of Rapoport
[
Rap78
, Thm 1.20]
. As being smooth is étale local on source and target, we then deduce that
Y(2)
is smooth over
\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2\Delta}]
. ∎

In the remaining of this section we prove Proposition
5.5
. The main ideas are as follows: After identifying
B_{\mathcal{P}}
with the branch locus of a cover
Y^{\prime}\to Y
where
Y^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}(n)}
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
, we show that
Y^{\prime}\to Y=Y^{\prime}/G
is a categorical quotient for the action of
G={\textup{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
on
Y^{\prime}
. To understand the étale locus of
Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}/G
, we then compute in (
5.13
) the stabilizer groups in terms of automorphism groups of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
and we exploit our assumption on the geometric automorphism groups, see also Remark
5.7
.

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

Suppose that the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
is finite, that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is reduced and that each geometric automorphism group of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
has even order. Then Proposition
5.5
is equivalent to the statement that any point
y\in Y
with
|\textup{Aut}(y)|\leq 3
does not lie in the branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq Y
of
\mathcal{P}
. We now prove this statement.

1.

In the first step we view
B_{\mathcal{P}}
as the branch locus of a suitable scheme cover of
Y
by applying the results of Section
5.1
. To get an étale scheme cover of
\mathcal{M}
with a simple moduli interpretation, we now reduce to the situation in which
B
is a scheme over
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
. This is possible since all involved constructions are compatible with localization on the base. The details of this reduction are as follows. There exist
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
and a point
y_{T}\in Y_{T}
which becomes
y\in Y
via the projection
Y_{T}\hookrightarrow Y
for
T=B\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
. Further the discussion surrounding (
5.5
) shows that
Y_{T}
is a coarse moduli scheme over
T
of the arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{M}_{T}
given by the restriction of
\mathcal{P}
to the open substack
\mathcal{M}_{T}
of
\mathcal{M}
. The flat base change
\pi_{T}:(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to Y_{T}
of
\pi
is again an initial morphism, where we identified
(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
with
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{B}T
. Hence we deduce that
|\textup{Aut}(y_{T})|=|\textup{Aut}(y)|
and that
(B_{\mathcal{P}})_{T}\subseteq B_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
which assures that the point
y\in Y
does not lie in
B_{\mathcal{P}}
if the point
y_{T}\in Y_{T}
does not lie in
B_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
. Therefore we may and do replace
B
by the nonempty open subscheme
T\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
which is a scheme over
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
.

Now, as
\mathcal{M}
is a stack over a
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
-scheme
B
, the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}
is a finite étale cover, where
\mathcal{Q}
is the moduli problem of principal level
n
-structures on
\mathcal{M}
. Further the algebraic stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is a scheme since
n\geq 3
. Then
Y^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is a scheme and we obtain a finite surjective morphism of schemes

f:Y^{\prime}\to Y

which factors as
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y
with
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
the projection. Indeed this follows from an application of Lemma
5.1
with the scheme
Z=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
and the forgetful morphism
Z\to\mathcal{M}
. Furthermore, as the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}
is a finite étale cover, the branch locus
B_{f}\subseteq Y
of the morphism
f
satisfies

B_{\mathcal{P}}=B_{f}
by (
5.9
).

2.

In the next step we view the morphism
f:Y^{\prime}\to Y
as a categorical quotient. The scheme
Y^{\prime}
is quasi-projective over
B
, since
Z=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is quasi-projective over
B
and since
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
is finite by assumption.
Further the projection
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a finite locally free cover since
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}
is a finite étale cover, and the fiber product
R=Y^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}}Y^{\prime}
is a scheme since it is a base change of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}
which is representable in schemes. Then it follows from
[
Con05
, Thm 3.1]
and the universal property of
\pi
that
f:Y^{\prime}\to Y
is a categorical quotient in schemes over
B
of the pre-equivalence relation

R\to Y^{\prime}\times_{B}Y^{\prime}

defined by the groupoid in algebraic spaces
(Y^{\prime},R,s,t,c)
over
B
, where
s,t:R\to Y^{\prime}
correspond to the two projections and
c:R\times_{Y^{\prime}}R\to R
corresponds to the projection which comes from the canonical equivalence
R\times_{Y^{\prime}}R\cong Y^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}}Y^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}}Y^{\prime}
.

To describe the categorical quotient
f
more explicitly, we may and do assume that the Hilbert moduli stack
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{I}
is associated to some
(L,I,I_{+})
.
Let
\mathcal{O}
be the ring of integers of
L
, and let
G
be the constant group scheme over
B
defined by the finite group
{\textup{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
which acts on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
via (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Then we obtain an action of
G
on the
B
-scheme
Y^{\prime}
by transport of structure using the equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
in (
5.1
) and the inverse defined right after (
5.1
). We now show that
f
is a categorical quotient in schemes over
B
for the induced morphism

G\times_{B}Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}\times_{B}Y^{\prime}.

(5.12)

As
f
is a categorical quotient in schemes over
B
for
R\to Y^{\prime}\times_{B}Y^{\prime}
, it suffices to prove for any morphism of
B
-schemes
Y^{\prime}\to X
that it is
R
-invariant if and only if it is
G
-invariant. It follows from (1) below that this is equivalent to the statement that for any geometric point
\textnormal{Spec}(k)\to B
the map
Y^{\prime}(k)\to X(k)
is constant on weak
R
-orbits if and only if it is constant on weak
G
-orbits. But this statement holds since the weak
R
-orbits are precisely the weak
G
-orbits by (2) below. Hence we conclude (
5.12
).

(1) Let
Y^{\prime}\to X
be a morphism of schemes over
B
. Then
Y^{\prime}\to X
is
R
-invariant (resp.
G
-invariant) if and only if for any geometric point
\textnormal{Spec}(k)\to B
the map
Y^{\prime}(k)\to X(k)
is constant on weak
R
-orbits (resp. weak
G
-orbits). This follows for example from the results in
[
Sta
, 048M]
which require that the schemes
R
and
G\times_{B}Y^{\prime}
are reduced with
R\rightrightarrows Y^{\prime}
and
G\times_{B}Y^{\prime}\rightrightarrows Y^{\prime}
locally of finite type. These conditions are satisfied. Indeed, on using that
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a base change of the finite étale
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}
and that
G
is finite étale over
B
, we see that
R
and
G\times_{B}Y^{\prime}
are both finite étale over
Y^{\prime}
which is finite étale over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
and by assumption
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is reduced and of finite type over
B
.

(2) For any geometric point
\textnormal{Spec}(k)\to B
, two
k
-points
u,u^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}(k)
are weakly
R
-equivalent if and only if they are weakly
G
-equivalent. This can be verified by a direct computation. Let
\phi:Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
be the equivalence in (
5.1
), let
\psi
be the inverse defined right after (
5.1
), and write
\phi(u)=(x,\alpha,\beta)
and
\phi(u^{\prime})=(x^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
. The following computations crucially exploit that
\phi
and
\psi
are morphisms of categories over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
. We first suppose that
u
is weakly
R
-equivalent to
u^{\prime}
. Then there exists
r\in R(k)
with
s(r)=u
and
t(r)=u^{\prime}
. Hence
r=(u,u^{\prime},\tau)
for some isomorphism
\tau:(x,\alpha)\overset{\sim}{\to}(x^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(k)
which induces an isomorphism
(x,\alpha,\beta^{*})\overset{\sim}{\to}(x^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
where
\beta^{*}\in\mathcal{Q}(x)
. Then we compute that
g\cdot u=\psi(x,\alpha,\beta^{*})=u^{\prime}
where
g\in G(k)
is defined by
(\beta^{*})^{-1}\beta
. Hence
u
is weakly
G
-equivalent to
u^{\prime}
as desired. To prove the converse, we now suppose that
u
is weakly
G
-equivalent to
u^{\prime}
. Then there exists
g\in G(k)
such that
g\cdot u=u^{\prime}
and hence
(x,\alpha,g\cdot\beta)
is isomorphic to
(x^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}(k)
. Thus there is an isomorphism
\tau:(x,\alpha)\overset{\sim}{\to}(x^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(k)
such that
r=(u,u^{\prime},\tau)\in R(k)
satisfies
s(r)=u
and
t(r)=u^{\prime}
, which means that
u
is weakly
R
-equivalent to
u^{\prime}
as desired. This completes the proof of (2).

3.

Now we are ready to show that the morphism
f
is étale over any point
y\in Y
with
|\textup{Aut}(y)|\leq 3
. For this purpose we may assume that
f
is the geometric quotient morphism
q:Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}/G
which sends a geometric point to its
G
-orbit coming from the action of
G
on the quasi-projective
B
-scheme
Y^{\prime}
. Indeed (
5.12
) combined with the universal property of categorical quotients gives a unique isomorphism
\chi:Y\overset{\sim}{\to}Y^{\prime}/G
of schemes over
B
such that
q=\chi f
. In particular
q
is étale over
\chi(y)
if and only if
f
is étale over
y
, and the separable rank
|\textup{Aut}(\chi(y))|
defined with respect to the initial morphism
\chi\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y^{\prime}/G
satisfies
|\textup{Aut}(y)|=|\textup{Aut}(\chi(y))|
. In what follows we assume that
f=q
.

Let
y\in Y
be a point with
|\textup{Aut}(y)|\leq 3
. We consider a point
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
with
f(y^{\prime})=y
and we denote by
D\subseteq{\textup{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
the stabilizer group of
y^{\prime}
with respect to the action of
{\textup{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
on
Y^{\prime}
. Then
f:Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}/G
is étale at
y^{\prime}
if and only if the geometric quotient morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}/D
is étale at
y^{\prime}
. In particular
f
is étale at
y^{\prime}
in the situation when
D
acts trivially on all points of
Y^{\prime}
. We now show that we are in this situation. To understand the action of
D
on
Y^{\prime}
, we shall construct in step 4. below a group
\textup{Aut}(y)(k)
with the following properties: Its cardinality equals
|\textup{Aut}(y)|
, it has at most one element
\sigma
of order two and there exist an isomorphism of groups

\textup{Aut}(y)(k)\overset{\sim}{\to}D

(5.13)

which sends this
\sigma
to
-1
. Then we see that
|\textup{Aut}(y)(k)|=|\textup{Aut}(y)|=2
, since by assumption each geometric automorphism group of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
has even order and
|\textup{Aut}(y)|\leq 3
.
Thus the above mentioned properties of
\textup{Aut}(y)(k)
imply that this group is generated by
\sigma
. Hence we obtain that
D=\{1,-1\}
since the isomorphism (
5.13
) sends
\sigma
to
-1
. To understand the action of
-1\in D
on the points of
Y^{\prime}
, we consider a geometric point
\textnormal{Spec}(k^{\prime})\to B
. Let
(x,\alpha)
be in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(k^{\prime})
. The forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
identifies
\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)
with a subgroup of
\textup{Aut}(x)
. Further, as the inertia stack
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is finite, the group
\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)
is finite. Hence Cauchy’s theorem and our assumption on the geometric automorphism groups of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
imply that
\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)
contains an element of order two. Then it follows from Lemma
5.3
and
\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)\subseteq\textup{Aut}(x)
that this element is given by multiplication with
[-1]
. Thus for each object
(x,\alpha,\beta)
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}(k^{\prime})
there exists an isomorphism
(x,\alpha,\beta)\overset{\sim}{\to}(x,\alpha,-1\cdot\beta)
defined by the element in
\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)
given by
[-1]
. This implies that
-1
, and hence the whole group
D=\{1,-1\}
, acts trivially on all points of
Y^{\prime}
. We conclude that
f
is étale at each
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
with
f(y^{\prime})=y
, that is
f
is étale over
y
. This shows that
y
does not lie in
B_{f}
and then the equality
B_{f}=B_{\mathcal{P}}
obtained in step 1. assures that our point
y
does not lie in
B_{\mathcal{P}}
as desired.

4.

It remains to construct the group
\textup{Aut}(y)(k)
in (
5.13
) with all the desired properties. For this purpose we use again the equivalence
\phi:Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
. Let
y^{\prime}:\textnormal{Spec}(k)\to Y^{\prime}
be a geometric point over
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
and denote by
\phi(y^{\prime})=(x,\alpha,\beta)
its image in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}(k)
. Then
(x,\alpha)
lies in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(k)
and we consider the group algebraic space over
k
given by

\textup{Aut}(y)=\textnormal{Isom}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}}((x,\alpha),(x,\alpha)).

Notice that the group
\textup{Aut}(y)(k)
identifies with
\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)
. As
f(y^{\prime})=y
and
\phi
is a morphism over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
, we find that
\pi(x,\alpha)=y
. This implies that
|\textup{Aut}(y)|=|\textup{Aut}(y)(k)|
since the separable rank
|\textup{Aut}(y)|
is an invariant of the point
\pi^{-1}(y)
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
and since
k
is an algebraically closed field. The forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
identifies
\textup{Aut}(y)(k)=\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)
with a subgroup of
\textup{Aut}(x)
, and it follows from Lemma
5.3
that any element of order two in
\textup{Aut}(x)
is given by multiplication with
[-1]:A\to A
where
x=(A,\iota,\varphi)
. Thus
\textup{Aut}(y)(k)
has at most one element of order two. Next, we construct an isomorphism
\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)\overset{\sim}{\to}D
. For each
\sigma\in\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)
we denote by
\sigma_{n}
the restriction to
A_{n}
of the automorphism of
A
underlying
\sigma
. Then we obtain a group morphism

\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)\overset{\sim}{\to}D,\quad\sigma\mapsto\beta^{-1}\sigma_{n}\beta.

Indeed
g=\beta^{-1}\sigma_{n}\beta
lies in the decomposition group
D
of
y^{\prime}
since
\sigma
defines an isomorphism
(x,\alpha,\beta)\overset{\sim}{\to}(x,\alpha,g\cdot\beta)
and hence
g\cdot y^{\prime}=y^{\prime}
, where as before we identify an automorphism of the constant group scheme
(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})^{2}_{k}
over the algebraically closed field
k
with the corresponding element in
G(k)={\textup{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
. To see that the displayed morphism is injective, we take
\lambda\in I_{+}
where
I_{+}
are the totally positive elements of the
\mathcal{O}
-submodule
I
of
L
which appears in the definition of
\mathcal{M}
. Now we consider the automorphism group
\textup{Aut}(A,\varphi(\lambda))
of the polarized abelian variety
(A,\varphi(\lambda))
over
k
. The forgetful morphisms induce an embedding
\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)\hookrightarrow\textup{Aut}(A,\varphi(\lambda))
, and the restriction morphism
\textup{Aut}(A,\varphi(\lambda))\to\textup{Aut}(A_{n})
is injective since
n\geq 3
. Therefore the composition
\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)\to\textup{Aut}(A_{n})
given by
\sigma\mapsto\sigma_{n}
is injective, which implies that the displayed morphism is injective. On the other hand, the displayed morphism is surjective since for any
g\in D
there exists an isomorphism
(x,\alpha,\beta)\overset{\sim}{\to}(x,\alpha,g\cdot\beta)
which defines an automorphism
\sigma
of
(x,\alpha)
with
g=\beta^{-1}\sigma_{n}\beta
. Further, we notice that
\beta^{-1}[-1]\beta=-1
and we recall that
\textup{Aut}(y)(k)=\textup{Aut}(x,\alpha)
. Therefore we see that the displayed group morphism, and hence the group
\textup{Aut}(y)(k)
, has all the desired properties. This completes the proof of Proposition
5.5
. ∎

We now briefly discuss where we used the assumptions appearing in Proposition
5.5
.

Remark 5.7
.

The assumption that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{M}
is finite assures in 2. that the scheme
Y^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is quasi-projective over
B
which we use to view
Y^{\prime}\to Y
as a geometric quotient
Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}/G
induced by the action of
G={\textup{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
. The assumption that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is reduced assures that
Y^{\prime}
is reduced which we use to relate
R
-invariance with
G
-invariance, see (1). Finally, the assumption on the geometric automorphism groups assures that certain decomposition groups
D\subseteq G
act trivially on all points of
Y^{\prime}
and hence
Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}/D
is étale. This assumption can be relaxed by studying the étale locus of
Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}/D
in terms of the automorphism groups of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
; such a study could be the subject of a future work.

Section 6
Morphims of abelian schemes over Dedekind schemes

Let
S
be a connected Dedekind scheme. In this section we introduce some notation and terminology, and we collect basic results for morphisms of abelian schemes over
S
which we shall use freely in what follows. We refer to
[
vKK19
,
\mathsection
5]
for detailed proofs or precise references for those statements in this section which are not further explained.

Basic properties.

Let
A
,
A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime\prime}
be abelian schemes over
S
. Composition induces a pairing
\circ
from
\textup{Hom}^{0}(A,A^{\prime})\times\textup{Hom}^{0}(A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime})
to
\textup{Hom}^{0}(A,A^{\prime\prime})
. Further
f\mapsto f\otimes 1
embeds the finitely generated free abelian group
\textup{Hom}(A,A^{\prime})
into
\textup{Hom}^{0}(A,A^{\prime})
. In what follows we shall often identify a morphism
f:A\to A^{\prime}
with its image
f\otimes 1
in
\textup{Hom}^{0}(A,A^{\prime})
. For each nonzero
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and any
f\in\textup{Hom}(A,A^{\prime})
we denote by
f/n
the element
f\otimes n^{-1}
of
\textup{Hom}^{0}(A,A^{\prime})
. Let
k
be the function field of the connected Dedekind scheme
S
. Base change from
S
to the spectrum of
k
induces canonical isomorphisms

\textup{Hom}(A,A^{\prime})\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{Hom}(A_{k},A_{k}^{\prime})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\textup{End}(A)\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}(A_{k}).

(6.1)

A direct computation shows that any element in
\textup{Hom}^{0}(A,A^{\prime})
takes the form
f\otimes q
with
f\in\textup{Hom}(A,A^{\prime})
and
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. In particular, we see that the center of the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
identifies with
Z\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
where
Z
denotes the center of the ring
\textup{End}(A)
. We say that
A
is simple if
A_{k}
is simple. Further we say that
A^{\prime}
is a factor of
A
if there exists a surjective morphism
A\to A^{\prime}
of abelian schemes over
S
. The proof of Poincaré’s reducibility theorem together with (
6.1
) shows the following: The abelian scheme
A
is simple if and only if
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
is a division algebra, and

A^{\prime}
is a factor of
A
if and only if there exists an abelian scheme
B
over
S
such that
A^{\prime}\times_{S}B
is isogenous to
A
. We say that
A
is isotypic if
A
is isogenous to a power of a simple abelian scheme over
S
.

Isogenies.

Let
\varphi\colon A\to A^{\prime}
be a morphism of abelian schemes over
S
. Then
\varphi
is an isogeny if and only if
\varphi_{k}:A_{k}\to A^{\prime}_{k}
is an isogeny of abelian varieties over
k
. Suppose now that
\varphi
is an isogeny. Then
\varphi
is flat and the rank
d=\deg(\varphi)
of its kernel
\ker(\varphi)
is constant on the connected scheme
S
. Consider the ‘inverse’ isogeny (
[
vKK19
, (5.1)]
)

\varphi^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\to A,\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad\varphi^{*}\colon\textup{End}^{0}(A^{\prime})\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}^{0}(A),\quad f\mapsto\varphi^{\prime}/d\circ f\circ\varphi.

(6.2)

Here
\varphi^{*}
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras and
[
vKK19
, Lem 5.1]
gives that
\varphi^{\prime}\varphi=[d]
and
\varphi\varphi^{\prime}=[d]
where
[n]
denotes the multiplication by
n\in\mathbb{Z}
morphism. In particular any isogeny
A\to A^{\prime}
becomes invertible inside
\textup{Hom}^{0}(A,A^{\prime})
.
If
A^{\prime}
is simple and if
A
and
A^{\prime}
are of the same relative dimension over
S
, then any nonzero element
\varphi
of
\textup{Hom}(A,A^{\prime})
is an isogeny. Indeed
\varphi
corresponds via (
6.1
) to a nonzero
\varphi_{k}:A_{k}\to A_{k}^{\prime}
which is an isogeny since
A_{k}^{\prime}
is simple and
\dim(A_{k})=\dim(A^{\prime}_{k})
.

Conjugate abelian variety.

Let
A
be an abelian variety defined over an arbitrary field
k
, let
\sigma
be an automorphism of
k
and write
\sigma^{*}A
for the base change of
A
via
\sigma:k\to k
. The abelian variety
\sigma^{*}A
is called the conjugate of
A
by
\sigma
. We denote by

\sigma^{*}:\textup{Hom}(A,A^{\prime})\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{Hom}(\sigma^{*}A,\sigma^{*}A^{\prime})

the isomorphism of abelian groups induced by base change via
\sigma
, where
A^{\prime}
is an abelian variety over
k
. We also denote by
\sigma^{*}
the isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces given by
\sigma^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
. Base change properties of (group) schemes imply the following: The map
\sigma^{*}
is a morphism of rings if
A=A^{\prime}
, and
\varphi:A\to A^{\prime}
is an isogeny if and only if
\sigma^{*}(\varphi)
is an isogeny.

Section 7
Effective Shafarevich conjecture

Let
K
be a number field. In this section we prove new cases of the effective Shafarevich conjecture for abelian varieties over
K
. In particular, we establish this conjecture for abelian varieties of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies and for CM abelian varieties.

Let
g\geq 1
be a rational integer, let
S
be a nonempty open subscheme of
\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
, and let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
. We recall that
A
is of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies if
A
is isogenous to a product
\prod A_{i}
of abelian schemes
A_{i}
over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies as in (
1.5
). A discussion of the notion of an abelian scheme over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies can be found in
\mathsection
7.1.1
below.

Height bound.

As before, we use the quantities
d=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
,
D_{K}
and
N_{S}=\prod_{v\in S^{c}}N_{v}
. We define
l=(d3^{4g^{2}})!
and we denote by
h_{F}
the stable Faltings height (
\mathsection
3.4
) of an abelian scheme over
S
. The main goal of this section is to prove the following result.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
.

(i)

If
A
is of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies then

h_{F}(A)\leq(4gl)^{144gl}\textnormal{rad}(N_{S}D_{K})^{24g}.

(ii)

If
A_{\bar{K}}
has CM then
h_{F}(A)\leq(3g)^{(5g)^{2}}\textnormal{rad}(N_{S}D_{K})^{5g}
.

The proof shows that one can replace in (i) the exponent
24g
by
\max(24,5g)
, while Proposition
7.13
and (
7.19
) provide sharper versions of (i) in relevant special situations. Further Proposition
7.14
and Lemma
7.10
contain more precise versions of (ii) in certain cases of interest, see also the discussions in
\mathsection
7.2.1
for known asymptotic versions of (ii).

Number of classes.

Another goal of this section is to prove Theorem B (ii) on the number of isomorphism classes of abelian schemes over
S
of relative dimension
g
which are of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies. In course of the proof given in Section
7.4
, we shall obtain various intermediate results which improve Theorem B (ii) in certain cases of interest; see for example Proposition
7.15
and Lemma
7.17
.

Outline of the section.

In Section
7.1
we study abelian schemes of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies. After motivating the definition (
\mathsection
7.1.1
), we discuss and prove Proposition
7.2
on the factors of such abelian schemes. This proposition involves a technical condition
(*)
which is further studied in
\mathsection
7.1.4
. We also collect in this section some basic results which are used throughout this work; in particular we consider abelian schemes of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type (Lemma
7.5
) and isogenies between conjugate abelian varieties (
\mathsection
7.1.5
).

In Section
7.2
we begin to bound the height of abelian varieties with CM. We discuss the averaged Colmez conjecture in
\mathsection
7.2.1
and we work out in
\mathsection
7.2.2
explicit analytic estimates for certain L-values attached to CM fields. Then we prove Lemma
7.12
on the ramification of endomorphism algebras of simple abelian varieties with CM.

In Sections
7.3
and
7.4
we put everything together. After reviewing known results for the Faltings height and discussing isogeny estimates (
\mathsection
7.3.1
), we prove Theorem B (ii) and Theorem
7.1
by using the strategy and the ideas explained in
\mathsection
1.2.1
.

7.1
Abelian schemes of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies

Let
k\subseteq K
be fields which are algebraic over
\mathbb{Q}
and write
G=\textup{Aut}(\bar{k}/k)
. In this section we discuss a class of abelian schemes which we call of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies. After motivating our definition, we show in Proposition
7.2
that the study of this class of abelian schemes over certain base schemes
S
with
k(S)=K
can be reduced to the study of simple abelian schemes of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type over certain base schemes
T
with
k(T)=k
.

7.1.1
The notion of abelian schemes of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies

Let
k\subseteq K
and
G
be as above. Choose
\bar{k}
with
K\subseteq\bar{k}
and let
S
be a connected Dedekind scheme with function field
K
. We say that an abelian scheme
A
over
S
of relative dimension
g
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies if the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
contains a number field
F
of degree
g
and if for each
\sigma\in G
there is an isogeny
\mu_{\sigma}:\sigma^{*}A_{\bar{k}}\to A_{\bar{k}}
such that

\mu_{\sigma}\circ\sigma^{*}(f)=f\circ\mu_{\sigma},\quad f\in F.

(7.1)

Here we identified
F
with its image in
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{\bar{k}})
. More generally, we say that an abelian scheme
A
over
S
is of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies if
A
is isogenous to a product
\prod A_{i}
such that each
A_{i}
is an abelian scheme over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies. If
A
is an abelian scheme over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies then any abelian scheme
A^{\prime}
over
S
which is isogenous to
A
is again of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies. Indeed one can take here for example
F^{\prime}=\varphi\circ F\circ\varphi^{\prime}/d
and the isogenies
\mu^{\prime}_{\sigma}=\varphi_{\bar{k}}\mu_{\sigma}\sigma^{*}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\bar{k}})
where
\varphi:A\to A^{\prime}
is an isogeny of degree
d
and
\varphi^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\to A
is an inverse as in (
6.2
).

Related notions.

The notion of abelian schemes over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies generalizes various definitions in the literature. To explain this, we take
K=\bar{k}
and we assume that
A
is an abelian variety over
K
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type. In the case
\dim(A)=1
, we observe that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies if and only if
A
is a
k
-curve
1
1

1

A
k
-curve is an elliptic curve
E
over
K
which is isogenous to
\sigma^{*}E
for all
\sigma\in G
.
in the sense of Gross
[
Gro80
]
, Ribet
[
Rib92
]
and Elkies
[
Elk04
]
. Further, Ribet
[
Rib94
]
introduced the notion of ‘
k
-HBAV’ which was generalized by Wu
[
Wu20
, Def 1.9]
. She defined
A
to be
k
-virtual if for each
\sigma\in G
there is an isogeny
\mu_{\sigma}:\sigma^{*}A\to A
such that

\mu_{\sigma}\circ\sigma^{*}(f)=f\circ\mu_{\sigma},\quad f\in\textup{End}^{0}(A).

(7.2)

On comparing (
7.2
) with (
7.1
), we see that the notion of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies generalizes the definition of
k
-virtual abelian varieties
A
over
K
and thus the notion of ‘
k
-HBAV’. The additional generality is useful in certain situations of interest in arithmetic such as for example in
\mathsection
10.1
where we can directly verify our weaker condition (
7.1
). However, if
A
has no CM then our condition (
7.1
) is in fact equivalent to the stronger
k
-virtual condition (
7.2
). To prove this claim, we suppose that
A
has no CM and that there exist
F\subseteq\textup{End}^{0}(A)
and isogenies
\mu_{\sigma}
as in (
7.1
). Then Lemma
7.5
gives that
A
is isotypic and that
F
contains the center of
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
. Thus the isogenies
\mu_{\sigma}
are center compatible, and hence
A
is
k
-virtual by Lemma
7.9
. This proves the claim.

7.1.2
Factors of abelian schemes of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies

We continue our notation. Further, we now assume that
k\subseteq K
are number fields and we suppose that
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
is open with complement
S^{c}
. Then we define

U=S\setminus p^{-1}(T^{c})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad T=p(U)

(7.3)

where
p:\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})\to\textnormal{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{k})
is the projection and
T^{c}
is the union of
p(S^{c})
with the branch locus of
p
.
Weil restriction reduces the study of some abelian schemes over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies to the study of abelian schemes over
T
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
with no CM. Suppose that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies and assume
(*)
. Then there is a simple abelian scheme
C
over
T
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type such that
A_{U}
is isogenous to a power of a simple factor of
C_{U}
.

Here condition
(*)
is as follows: The field extension
K/k
is normal, and the endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{k}}
and the isogenies
\mu_{\sigma}
in (
7.1
) are all defined over
K\subset\bar{k}
. In fact
(*)
can always be satisfied (
\mathsection
7.1.4
) when passing to a controlled field extension of
K
. The proof of Proposition
7.2
shows in addition that any simple factor
B
of
A
satisfies

\dim(C)\leq\dim(B)[K:k]

(7.4)

for
\dim
the relative dimension. Further we observe that
T
is open in
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{k})
with complement
T^{c}
,
and Dedekind’s discriminant theorem shows that
T^{c}
is the set of finite places
v
of
k
such that
v
lies under
S^{c}
or
v
divides the discriminant ideal of
K/k
.

7.1.3
Proof of Proposition
7.2

Let
k\subseteq K\subseteq\bar{k}
,
S
and
G
be as in Section
7.1.1
, and let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
. Throughout this subsection we assume that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies, that
A
has no CM and that
(*)
holds. In a first step we show the following result.

Lemma 7.3
.

There is an isogeny from
A
to a power of an abelian scheme
B
over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type such that
B_{\bar{k}}
is a simple
k
-virtual abelian variety of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with no CM.

In particular
B_{\bar{k}}
in Lemma
7.3
satisfies all assumptions in Wu
[
Wu20
, Prop 1.12]
. Hence there is a simple abelian variety
C_{k}
over
k
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type such that
B_{\bar{k}}
is a factor of
C_{\bar{k}}
. Moreover
[
vK13a
,
\mathsection
9.4]
, which crucially relies on the arguments of Ribet
[
Rib92
,
\mathsection
6]
and Wu
[
Wu20
, Prop 1.12]
, leads to the following result.

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose that
K
is a number field with
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
open, and let
B
be the abelian scheme of Lemma
7.3
. Then there is a simple abelian scheme
C
over
T
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type such that
B_{U}
is a simple factor of
C_{U}
, where
U
and
T
are as in (
7.3
).

Now, on combining this result with Lemma
7.3
we deduce Proposition
7.2
. The remaining of this section is devoted to the proofs of the above two lemmas.

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

By assumption
S
is a connected Dedekind scheme and
A
is an abelian scheme over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with no CM. Hence (
7.5
) and Lemma
7.5
give a simple abelian scheme
B
over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
A
is isogenous to
B^{n}
.

We next prove that
B_{\bar{k}}
is a simple abelian variety over
\bar{k}
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with no CM. Firstly
B_{\bar{k}}
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type, since it is a base change of
B
which is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type. Further, all endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{k}}
are defined over
K
by
(*)
. Therefore (
6.1
) gives

\textup{End}(A_{\bar{k}})\cong\textup{End}(A).

(7.5)

This shows that
A_{\bar{k}}
has no CM since
A
has no CM. Then on using that
A_{\bar{k}}
is isogenous to
B_{\bar{k}}^{n}
, we deduce that the abelian varieties
B_{\bar{k}}^{n}
and
B_{\bar{k}}
both have no CM. Poincaré reducibility shows that any factor of
A
also has property (
7.5
). Thus
\textup{End}^{0}(B_{\bar{k}})\cong\textup{End}^{0}(B)
is a division algebra since
B
is a simple factor of
A
. This implies that
B_{\bar{k}}
is simple.

To show that
B_{\bar{k}}
is
k
-virtual, we now use our assumption that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies. Hence there is a number field
F
inside
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{\bar{k}})
of degree
\dim(A_{\bar{k}})
and for each
\sigma\in G
there is an isogeny
\mu_{\sigma}:\sigma^{*}A_{\bar{k}}\to A_{\bar{k}}
satisfying (
7.1
).
As
A_{\bar{k}}
is an abelian variety over
\bar{k}
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with no CM, an application of Lemma
7.5
with
A_{\bar{k}}
gives that the center
Z^{0}
of
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{\bar{k}})
is contained in
F
. Then, on using that
\textup{Hom}(\sigma^{*}A_{\bar{k}},A_{\bar{k}})
is torsion-free and that
Z^{0}=Z\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
for
Z
the center of
\textup{End}(A_{\bar{k}})
, we see that (
7.1
) implies

\mu_{\sigma}\sigma^{*}(z)=z\mu_{\sigma},\quad z\in Z.

In other words the isogeny
\mu_{\sigma}:\sigma^{*}A_{\bar{k}}\to A_{\bar{k}}
is center compatible in the sense of
\mathsection
7.1.5
. Now, an application of Lemma
7.7
with the isotypic abelian variety
A_{\bar{k}}
and the automorphism
\sigma
of
\bar{k}
gives a center compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}B_{\bar{k}}\to B_{\bar{k}}
. Thus an application of Lemma
7.8
with the simple abelian variety
B_{\bar{k}}
provides an isogeny
\sigma^{*}B_{\bar{k}}\to B_{\bar{k}}
which (is fully compatible and hence) satisfies (
7.2
). Therefore we conclude that
B_{\bar{k}}
is a simple
k
-virtual abelian variety of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with no CM. This shows that
B
has the desired properties.
∎

In the following proof, we assume in addition that
k\subseteq K
are number fields and that
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
is open. Further, we let
p:U\to T
be as in Section
7.1.2
.

Proof of Lemma
7.4
.

Let
B
be the abelian scheme over
S
of Lemma
7.3
. Then
B_{\bar{k}}
is a simple
k
-virtual abelian variety of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with no CM. In particular, the
k
-virtual condition assures that for each
\sigma\in G
there is an isogeny
\mu_{\sigma}:\sigma^{*}B_{\bar{k}}\to B_{\bar{k}}
.

To show that each
\mu_{\sigma}
is defined over
K
, we use condition
(*)
. It assures that
K/k
is normal and the restriction of
\sigma\in G
to the subfield
K\subset\bar{k}
defines an automorphism of
K/k
which we also denote by
\sigma
. Then
\sigma^{*}B_{K}
is an abelian variety over
K
. It is simple, since it is geometrically isogenous via
\mu_{\sigma}
to the simple
B_{\bar{k}}
. Moreover, on using that
A_{K}
is isogenous to
\sigma^{*}A_{K}
by
(*)
and that
A
is isogenous to
B^{n}
, we see that
\sigma^{*}B_{K}
is a simple factor of
A_{K}
. Hence
\sigma^{*}B_{K}
has to be isogenous to the simple factor
B_{K}
, and then
\textup{Hom}^{0}(\sigma^{*}B_{K},B_{K})\cong\textup{End}^{0}(B_{K})
identifies with
\textup{Hom}^{0}(\sigma^{*}B_{\bar{k}},B_{\bar{k}})\cong\textup{End}^{0}(B_{\bar{k}})
since
B_{K}
has property (
7.5
). We conclude that each isogeny
\mu_{\sigma}
is defined over
K
.

We now go into Wu’s proof of
[
Wu20
, Prop 1.12]
. The above statements show that
B_{\bar{k}}
satisfies all assumptions of
[
Wu20
, Prop 1.12]
and that our
K
has all the properties of the field
F
appearing in Wu’s proof (
[
Wu17
,
\mathsection
1.2]
). Then Wu’s proof, which uses and generalizes Ribet’s arguments in
[
Rib92
,
\mathsection
6]
for
\mathbb{Q}
-curves, shows in addition
that the Weil restriction
D_{k}=\textnormal{Res}_{K/k}(B_{K})
of
B_{K}
has a simple factor
C_{k}
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type.

To prove that
C_{k}
extends to an abelian scheme
C
over
T
, we use our assumption that
K
is a number field. Hence Milne
[
Mil72
, Prop 1]
gives that
D_{k}
has good reduction at a finite place
v
of
k
if
v
does not divide the discriminant ideal of
K/k
and if
A_{K}
has good reduction at all points in
p^{-1}(v)
. Further the generic fiber
A_{K}
of the abelian scheme
A
over
S
has good reduction at each point in
S
, and Dedekind’s discriminant theorem shows that
v
divides the discriminant ideal of
K/k
if and only if
v
lies in the branch locus
B_{p}
of
p
. We deduce that
D_{k}
extends to an abelian scheme over
T=(p(S^{c})\cup B_{p})^{c}
, and then also the factor
C_{k}
of
D_{k}
extends to an abelian scheme
C
over
T
. Furthermore (
6.1
) gives that
C
is simple and of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type, since
C_{k}
has these properties.

We next show that
B_{U}
is a factor of
C_{U}
. The generic fiber
C_{K}
of
C_{U}
identifies with the abelian variety
C_{k}\times_{k}K
over
K
, since the composition of
\textnormal{Spec}(K)\to U
with
p:U\to T
factors as
\textnormal{Spec}(K)\to\textnormal{Spec}(k)\to T
. Further, as
C_{k}
is a factor of
D_{k}
, we obtain a surjective morphism
D_{k}\to C_{k}
of abelian varieties over
k
which induces a surjective morphism

\prod\sigma^{*}B_{K}\overset{\sim}{\to}D_{k}\times_{k}K\to C_{k}\times_{k}K\overset{\sim}{\to}C_{K}

of abelian varieties over
K

with the product taken over all
\sigma\in\textnormal{Aut}(K/k)
. Here we used that the field extension
K/k
is Galois by
(*)
which assures (
[
Wei82
, p.5]
) that the abelian variety
D_{k}\times_{k}K
over
K
is isomorphic to
\prod\sigma^{*}B_{K}
. We showed above that
B_{K}
is isogenous to each
\sigma^{*}B_{K}
. Therefore, on exploiting that the displayed morphism is surjective and hence nonzero, we see that
B_{K}
has to be a simple factor of
C_{K}
. This implies that
B_{U}
is a simple factor of
C_{U}
. We conclude that
C
has the desired properties.∎

We conclude this subsection by collecting basic results for abelian schemes of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type which are essentially due to Ribet
[
Rib75
,
Rib92
]
and Wu
[
Wu20
]
. In the above proofs we used (i) and (ii) of the following lemma, while (i) - (iii) will be used in later sections.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
S
be a connected Dedekind scheme whose function field
k
embeds into
\mathbb{C}
, and let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type such that
A
has no CM.

(i)

The abelian scheme
A
is isogenous to a power
B^{n}
of a simple abelian scheme
B
over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. In particular the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
is isomorphic to
\textnormal{M}_{n}(D)
where
D=\textup{End}^{0}(B)
is a division algebra.

(ii)

Let
F
be a subfield of the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
such that
[F:\mathbb{Q}]
equals the relative dimension of
A
over
S
. Then
F
is the centralizer of
F
in
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
.

(iii)

Either
D
identifies with a subfield
Z
of
F
with
[F:Z]=n
or
D
is a quaternion algebra whose center identifies with a subfield
Z
of
F
with
[F:Z]=2n
.

Proof.

To prove (i) we reduce to the case
S=\textnormal{Spec}(k)
which was treated in Wu
[
Wu20
, Prop 1.5 (i)]
when
k
is an arbitrary field of characteristic zero. Notice that Wu stated the result under the assumption that
A_{\bar{k}}
has no CM. However, it turns out that her proof can be copied word by word when making the weaker assumption that the abelian variety over
k
has no CM. Now, the reduction goes as follows: As
\textup{End}^{0}(A)\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}^{0}(A_{k})
by (
6.1
) we obtain that
A_{k}
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with no CM. Then an application of
[
Wu20
, Prop 1.5]
with
A_{k}
gives an isogeny
A_{k}\to B_{k}^{n}
where
B_{k}
is a simple abelian variety of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. In particular
B_{k}
is a factor of
A_{k}
which extends to the abelian scheme
A
over
S
. Thus
B_{k}
extends to an abelian scheme
B
over
S
, and then the isogeny
A_{k}\to B^{n}_{k}
extends to a morphism
A\to B^{n}
of abelian schemes over
S
which is again an isogeny; see for example
[
vKK19
,
\mathsection
5.1]
. This together with
\textup{End}^{0}(B)\cong\textup{End}^{0}(B_{k})
implies (i).

To show (ii) and (iii) we may and do assume that
S=\textnormal{Spec}(k)
since
\textup{End}^{0}(A)\cong\textup{End}^{0}(A_{k})
and
D\cong\textup{End}^{0}(B_{k})
. Our
A
is isotypic by (i) and
A
has no CM. Thus no factor of
A
has CM. Then we deduce (ii) and (iii) by using precisely the same arguments as Ribet in his proof of
[
Rib92
, Prop 5.2]
; these arguments exploit a trick (attributed by Ribet to Tunnel) which exploits that the field
k
embeds into
\mathbb{C}
and hence
[D:\mathbb{Q}]
divides
2\dim(B)
since the division algebra
D
acts
\mathbb{Q}
-linearly on the
2\dim(B)
-dimensional homology
H_{1}(B(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Q})
. This completes the proof of Lemma
7.5
.
∎

7.1.4
Condition
(*)
and controlled field extensions

In this section we investigate the technical condition
(*)
appearing in Proposition
7.2
. For this purpose we let
k\subseteq K\subset\bar{k}
,
G
,
S
and
T
be as in Section
7.1.2
.

Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
such that
A_{\bar{k}}
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies. We say that condition
(*)
holds over a number field
L\supseteq K
if
L/k
is normal and if the endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{k}}
and the isogenies
\mu_{\sigma}
in (
7.1
) are all defined over
L\subset\bar{k}
. On using Silverberg’s work
[
Sil92
]
and the criterion of Néron–Ogg–Shafarevich
[
ST68
]
, we obtain that condition
(*)
holds over a controlled field
L
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Condition
(*)
holds over a field
L\supseteq K
such that
L
is contained in the normal closure of
K(A_{n})/k
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
and such that
L/k
is unramified over
T
.

Here and in what follows, for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we denote by
K(A_{n})
the field of definition of the
n
-torsion points of
A(\bar{k})
. The degree of
K(A_{n})
over
K
is at most
n^{4g^{2}}
, and Lemma
7.6
combined with Dedekind’s discriminant theorem allows to explicitly control the discriminant of
L/k
in terms of
g
, the discriminant of
K/k
, and
N_{S}=\prod_{v\in S^{c}}N_{v}
.

Proof of Lemma
7.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and denote by
K^{\textnormal{cl}}(A_{n})
the normal closure of
K(A_{n})
over
k
.
To show that
L=\cap_{n\geq 3}K^{\textnormal{cl}}(A_{n})
has the desired properties, we take
\sigma\in G
. The field
L
is normal over
k
. Thus pulling back
A_{L}
via the restriction of
\sigma
to
L
gives an abelian variety
\sigma^{*}A_{L}
over
L
which identifies over
\bar{k}
with
\sigma^{*}A_{\bar{k}}
. We denote by
L(\sigma^{*}A_{n})
and
L(A_{n})
the field of definition of the
n
-torsion points of
\sigma^{*}A_{L}(\bar{k})
and
A_{L}(\bar{k})
respectively. On using that
\sigma
is invertible and that
L(\sigma^{*}A_{n})
and
L(A_{n})
are normal over
L
, we deduce

L(\sigma^{*}A_{n})=L(A_{n}).

Then an application of Silverberg
[
Sil92
, Thm 4.2]
with
\sigma^{*}A_{L}
and
A_{L}
implies that any morphism in
\textup{Hom}(\sigma^{*}A_{\bar{k}},A_{\bar{k}})
