is defined over the subfield
\cap_{n\geq 3}L(A_{n})
of
\bar{k}
. Furthermore we obtain that
L=\cap_{n\geq 3}L(A_{n})
, since it holds that
L(A_{n})=K(A_{n})L
and that
K(A_{n})L\subseteq K^{\textnormal{cl}}(A_{n})
for each
n\geq 3
.
Thus we deduce that condition
(*)
holds over
L
.

To prove that
L/k
is unramified over
T
, we take a rational prime
p\geq 3
and for any scheme
S^{\prime}
we write
S^{\prime}[\tfrac{1}{p}]=S^{\prime}\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{p}]
.
The abelian variety
A_{K}
has good reduction at each closed point of
S
, since
A_{K}
extends to the abelian scheme
A
over
S
. Thus
K(A_{p})/K
is unramified over
S[\tfrac{1}{p}]
by the criterion of Néron–Ogg–Shafarevich
[
ST68
, Thm 1]
. Then the construction of
T
in (
7.3
) shows that
K(A_{p})/k
is unramified over
T[\tfrac{1}{p}]
and hence its normal closure
K^{\textnormal{cl}}(A_{p})/k
is also unramified over
T[\tfrac{1}{p}]
. Therefore, for any rational prime
q\geq 3
with
q\neq p
, we deduce that the subfield
L
of
K^{\textnormal{cl}}(A_{p})\cap K^{\textnormal{cl}}(A_{q})
is unramified over
T=T[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cup T[\tfrac{1}{q}]
. Finally, as the field
L=\cap_{n\geq 3}K^{\textnormal{cl}}(A_{n})
is contained in
K^{\textnormal{cl}}(A_{n})
for each
n\geq 3
, we conclude that
L/k
has all the desired properties.
∎

7.1.5
Center compatible isogenies between conjugate abelian varieties

In this section we collect basic lemmas which we used in
\mathsection
7.1.1
and
\mathsection
7.1.3
above. These lemmas allow in situations where isogenies of conjugate abelian varieties are center compatible to reduce to the case where the isogenies are compatible with the whole endomorphism ring. The results should be all well-known. However, we are not aware of suitable references and for the sake of completeness we will thus give the proofs which use and generalize arguments of Ribet
[
Rib94
]
and Pyle
[
Pyl04
]
.

Let
A
be an abelian variety defined over an arbitrary field
k
, let
\sigma
be an automorphism of the field
k
and write
\sigma^{*}A
for the base change of
A
via
\sigma:k\to k
.

Center compatible isogenies.

We say that an isogeny
\mu:\sigma^{*}A\to A
is center compatible if any
z
in the center of
\textup{End}(A)
satisfies
\mu\sigma^{*}(z)=z\mu
. The existence of such an isogeny does not necessarily guarantee a center compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}B\to B
for the simple factors
B
of
A
. However, in the isotypic case we obtain the following result.

Lemma 7.7
.

Suppose that
A
is an isotypic abelian variety over
k
. If there exists a center compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}A\to A
, then there exists a center compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}B\to B
.

It is crucial for our applications in
\mathsection
7.1.3
that the statement of Lemma
7.7
does not depend on how
\sigma
acts on an isogeny
A\to B^{n}
or on
\textup{End}(A)
. Luckily, these dependences ‘cancel out’ as we shall see in the proof given at the end of this section.

Fully compatible isogenies.

We say that an isogeny
\mu:\sigma^{*}A\to A
is fully compatible if any
f\in\textup{End}(A)
satisfies

\mu\sigma^{*}(f)=f\mu.
In the isotypic case, one can always modify center compatible isogenies by an inner automorphism (obtained from the Skolem–Noether theorem) to get fully compatible isogenies. We first consider the simple case.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
B
be a simple abelian variety over
k
. If there exists a center compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}B\to B
, then there exists a fully compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}B\to B
.

Proof.

We may and do assume that
B\neq 0
. To modifiy a center compatible isogeny
\tau:\sigma^{*}B\to B
, we use a trick which we learned from Ribet
[
Rib92
]
and Pyle
[
Pyl04
]
. In fact their trick works exactly the same in our setting. The details are as follows:

Our
B
is simple. Thus
R=\textup{End}^{0}(B)
is a central simple algebra over its center
F
. Further
r\mapsto(\sigma^{-1})^{*}(\tau^{-1}\circ r\circ\tau)
defines an
F
-algebra morphism
\phi:R\to R
, since
\tau
is center compatible,
F=Z\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
for
Z
the center of
\textup{End}(B)
,
(\sigma^{-1})^{*}
is a ring morphism and
\circ
is a pairing. Hence Skolem–Noether gives
s\in R
such that
\phi(r)=s\circ r\circ s^{-1}
, and as
B
is simple we can take any
\mu:\sigma^{*}B\to B
with
\mu\otimes q=\tau\circ\sigma^{*}(s)
for some
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. ∎

We now use this result to strengthen the statement of Lemma
7.7
in the sense that we can moreover construct an isogeny
\sigma^{*}A\to A
which is fully compatible.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
A
be an isotypic abelian variety over
k
. If there exists a center compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}A\to A
, then there exists a fully compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}A\to A
.

Proof.

We may and do assume that
A\neq 0
is isotypic. Thus there is an isogeny
\varphi:A\to B^{n}
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
B
a simple abelian variety over
k
. Let
\varphi^{\prime}:B^{n}\to A
be its inverse isogeny as in (
6.2
). Further there is a center compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}A\to A
by assumption. Hence Lemma
7.7
provides a center compatible isogeny
\sigma^{*}B\to B
, and then Lemma
7.8
gives a fully compatible isogeny
\mu:\sigma^{*}B\to B
since
B
is simple. After identifying
\sigma^{*}B^{n}
with
(\sigma^{*}B)^{n}
, we see that
\mu
defines a fully compatible isogeny
\mu^{n}:\sigma^{*}B^{n}\to B^{n}
. Now we consider

\tau:\sigma^{*}A\to^{\sigma^{*}(\varphi)}\sigma^{*}B^{n}\to^{\mu^{n}}B^{n}\to^{\varphi^{\prime}}A.

Then
\tau
is an isogeny, since it is a composition of isogenies. Further
\varphi^{\prime}\varphi
and
\varphi\varphi^{\prime}
are both multiplication with
d=\deg(\varphi)
, and
[d]
commutes with any morphism of group schemes. Thus we deduce that
\tau\sigma^{*}(f)[d]=[d]f\tau=f\tau[d]
for all
f\in\textup{End}(A)
, since
\sigma^{*}
is a functor and since
\mu^{n}
is fully compatible. Then, on using that
\textup{Hom}(\sigma^{*}A,A)
is torsion-free, we conclude that the isogeny
\tau:\sigma^{*}A\to A
is fully compatible as desired.
∎

It remains to verify the statement of Lemma
7.7
. The following proof consists of checking various compatibilities (using standard arguments) in order to assure that a certain explicitly constructed isogeny
\tau:\sigma^{*}B\to B
is indeed center compatible.

Proof of Lemma
7.7
.

We may and do assume that
A
is nonzero. By assumption there exists a center compatible isogeny
\mu:\sigma^{*}A\to A
and there is an isogeny
\varphi:A\to B^{n}
where
B
is a simple abelian variety over
k
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Let
\varphi^{\prime}:B^{n}\to A
be the ‘inverse isogeny’ of
\varphi
as in (
6.2
) and let
\iota:B\to B^{n}
be the diagonal embedding. Now, we define

t:\sigma^{*}B\to^{\sigma^{*}(\iota)}\sigma^{*}B^{n}\to^{\sigma^{*}(\varphi^{\prime})}\sigma^{*}A\to^{\mu}A\to^{\varphi}B^{n}.

The base change
\sigma^{*}(\iota)
of
\iota
is injective, since
\iota
is universally injective. Then, on using that
\sigma^{*}(\varphi^{\prime})
,
\mu
and
\varphi
are all isogenies, we deduce that
t
has finite kernel and thus is nonzero since
\sigma^{*}B\neq 0
; here we used our assumption
A\neq 0
to assure that
B\neq 0
. Hence there is a projection
p:B^{n}\to B
such that
t
composed with
p
is a nonzero morphism

\tau:\sigma^{*}B\to B

of abelian varieties over
k
. Moreover
\tau\neq 0
is surjective since
B
is simple. Then, on using that
\sigma^{*}B
and
B
have the same dimension, we conclude that
\tau
is an isogeny.

To verify that
\tau
is center compatible, we take
z
in the center
Z
of
\textup{End}(B)
. We view
\textup{End}(B)
as a subring of
\textup{End}(B^{n})
via the diagonal embedding
\iota
and we observe that this identifies
Z
with the center of
\textup{End}(B^{n})
. A direct computation gives the identities

\varphi\circ\varphi^{*}(z)=z\circ\varphi\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad\varphi^{\prime}\circ z=\varphi^{*}(z)\circ\varphi^{\prime}

(7.6)

inside
\textup{Hom}^{0}(A,B^{n})
and inside
\textup{Hom}^{0}(B^{n},A)
respectively. Here we used the definition of the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra isomorphism
\varphi^{*}:\textup{End}^{0}(B^{n})\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}^{0}(A)
in (
6.2
). Pulling back by
\sigma
is a functor. Hence (
7.6
) shows that
\sigma^{*}(\varphi^{\prime})\circ\sigma^{*}(z)
equals
\sigma^{*}(\varphi^{*}(z))\circ\sigma^{*}(\varphi^{\prime})
, and
\sigma^{*}(\iota)
commutes with
\sigma^{*}(z)
since
\iota z=z\iota
. Therefore, on using that
\mu
is center compatible and that
\varphi^{*}(z)
lies in the center of
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
, we deduce from (
7.6
) the identity

t\circ\sigma^{*}(z)=z\circ t

inside
\textup{Hom}^{0}(\sigma^{*}B,B^{n})
. This identity then also holds inside the torsion-free abelian group
\textup{Hom}(\sigma^{*}B,B^{n})
, and we observe that
pz=zp
. It follows that
\tau\sigma^{*}(z)=z\tau
for all
z\in Z
, which means that
\tau
is center compatible. This completes the proof.
∎

7.2
Abelian varieties with CM

In this section we prove explicit upper bounds for the Faltings height of certain abelian varieties with CM. For this purpose we first recall the averaged Colmez conjecture in
\mathsection
7.2.1
. Then we work out in
\mathsection
7.2.2
explicit analytic estimates for certain L-values and we control in
\mathsection
7.2.3
the ramification of CM endomorphism algebras.

CM types and abelian varieties.

We now introduce some terminology which will be used throughout this section. A CM field is a number field which has no real embeddings but which is
quadratic over a totally real subfield. Let
E
be a CM field and let
F\subset E
be the maximal totally real subfield. A CM type
\Phi
of
E
is a subset
\Phi\subset\textup{Hom}(E,\mathbb{C})
such that
\Phi\sqcup\Phi\tau=\textup{Hom}(E,\mathbb{C})
where

\tau\in\textup{Aut}(E/F)
is not trivial. We say that a CM type of
E
is simple if it is not the full preimage under restriction of a CM type of a proper CM subfield of
E
. For any
\varphi:E\to\mathbb{C}
, we write
\mathbb{C}_{\varphi}
for
\mathbb{C}
viewed as an
E
-algebra via
\varphi
.

Let
A
be an abelian variety defined over a subfield
k
of
\mathbb{C}
. Suppose that
2\dim(A)=[E:\mathbb{Q}]
and assume that there exists a ring morphism
\iota:E\to\textup{End}^{0}(A)
. Then
\iota
induces an action of
E\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}\cong\prod_{\varphi}\mathbb{C}_{\varphi}
on the Lie algebra
\textnormal{Lie}(A_{\mathbb{C}})
of
A_{\mathbb{C}}
. This action decomposes
\textnormal{Lie}(A_{\mathbb{C}})
into a corresponding product of eigenspaces
\textnormal{Lie}(A_{\mathbb{C}})_{\varphi}
over
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\Phi_{A}
be the set of
\varphi\in\textup{Hom}(E,\mathbb{C})
such that the eigenspace
\textnormal{Lie}(A_{\mathbb{C}})_{\varphi}
has dimension one. Then
\Phi_{A}
is a CM type of
E
which is simple if and only if
A_{\mathbb{C}}
is a simple abelian variety; see for example
[
CCO14
,
\mathsection
1.5]
. We call
\Phi_{A}
the CM type of
E
associated to
(A,\iota)
.

For any CM type
\Phi
of
E
, we denote by
A_{\Phi}
the usual complex abelian variety associated to
\Phi
which is defined for example in
[
CCO14
, 1.5.3]
. The complex abelian variety
A_{\Phi}
has the following properties: There exists a ring morphism
m:\mathcal{O}_{E}\hookrightarrow\textup{End}(A_{\Phi})
such that
\Phi
is the CM type of
E
associated to
(A_{\Phi},\iota)
for
\iota=m\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and
A_{\Phi}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{E}).
Here
g=\dim(A_{\Phi})
and
\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{E})
denotes the lattice in
\mathbb{C}^{g}
given by the image of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
under the embedding
E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{g}

defined by the
g
distinct embeddings
E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
in
\Phi
.

7.2.1
Averaged Colmez conjecture

To state the averaged Colmez conjecture, let
E
be a CM field with maximal totally real subfield
F\subset E
and let
\Phi
be a CM type of
E
. We write
g=[F:\mathbb{Q}]
.

The height
h(\Phi)
.

Let
A_{\Phi}
be the complex abelian variety associated to
\Phi
. Any complex abelian variety with CM is defined over a number field and then we define

h(\Phi)=h_{F}(A_{\Phi}).

Here the invariance of the stable Faltings height
h_{F}
under geometric isomorphisms assures that
h(\Phi)
does not depend on the choice of a model of
A_{\Phi}
over a number field. Moreover, this invariance of
h_{F}
together with Colmez’
[
Col93
, Thm 0.3 (ii)]
implies that

h(\Phi)=h_{F}(A)

(7.7)

for any abelian variety
A
of dimension
g
which is defined over a number field
K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and which has the following property: There is a ring morphism
j:\mathcal{O}_{E}\to\textup{End}(A_{\mathbb{C}})
such that
\Phi
is the CM type of
E
associated to
(A_{\mathbb{C}},j\otimes\mathbb{Q})
. Yuan–Zhang obtained in
[
YZ18
,
\mathsection
2]
an alternative proof of (
7.7
) as a by-product of their height decomposition.

The formula.

Define
\mathfrak{c}=D_{E}/D_{F}
and let
L^{\prime}(s)
be the derivative of the L-function
L(s)
of the quadratic character of
\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}
corresponding to
E/F
; here
L(s)
is without the infinite part. Now we can state the averaged Colmez conjecture (
[
Col93
]
) which in the case
g=1
is a reformulation of the classical Lerch–Chowla–Selberg formula:

\tfrac{1}{2^{g}}\sum_{\Phi}h(\Phi)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{L^{\prime}(0)}{L(0)}-\tfrac{1}{4}\log\mathfrak{c}-\tfrac{g}{2}\log\pi

(7.8)

with the sum taken over all distinct CM types
\Phi
of
E
. Here the term
-\tfrac{g}{2}\log\pi
takes into account that we use Faltings’ normalization of the height
h_{F}
. Colmez
[
Col93
]
, Obus
[
Obu13
]
and Yang
[
Yan10
,
Yan13
]
proved important special cases of the exact version
[
Col93
, Conj 0.4]
of (
7.8
). In general (
7.8
) was established by Yuan–Zhang
[
YZ18
]
using the method of Yuan–Zhang–Zhang
[
YZZ13
]
and independently by Andreatta–Goren–Howard–Madapusi-Pera
[
AGHMP18
]
using the method of Yang
[
Yan10
,
Yan13
]
.

An explicit upper bound.

Colmez’ conjectures give useful bounds for the height
h(\Phi)
of any CM type
\Phi
of
E
. For example Colmez
[
Col98
,
\mathsection
3]
bounded
h(\Phi)
conditional on the exact version of (
7.8
) and other conjectures. Further Colmez
[
Col98
, p.365]
for
g=1
and Tsimerman
[
Tsi18
, Cor 3.3]
in general deduced from (
7.8
) the asymptotic bound

h(\Phi)\leq D_{E}^{o_{g}(1)}.

The proof uses analytic estimates for
L^{\prime}(0)/L(0)
via Brauer–Siegel and the lower bound
h(\Phi)\geq c_{g}
which is contained in Faltings’ work (
[
Fal83
, p.356]
,
[
Del85
, 1.22]
). In fact one can take here
c_{g}=-\tfrac{g}{2}\log(2\pi^{2})
by Bost’s lower bound (
7.10
),
and the analytic estimates (except Brauer–Siegel) applied in
[
Tsi18
, Cor 3.3]
can be worked out explicitly by using Rademacher’s arguments in
[
Rad60
]
. This leads to the following result.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
h_{E}
and
h_{F}
be the class number of
E
and
F
respectively. If
\epsilon>0
is a real number with
\epsilon\leq 1/2
, then any CM type
\Phi
of
E
satisfies

\tfrac{1}{2^{g}}h(\Phi)\leq a\tfrac{h_{F}}{h_{E}}\mathfrak{c}^{1/2}\max\bigl((\tfrac{2\pi}{2+\epsilon})^{g},\mathfrak{c}\bigl)^{\epsilon}+\tfrac{1}{4}\log\mathfrak{c}-\tfrac{\gamma}{2}g.

Here
a=\tfrac{wQ}{2\epsilon}\zeta(1+\epsilon)\tfrac{(2+\epsilon)^{g\epsilon}}{(2\pi)^{g(\epsilon+1)}}
where
\zeta(s)
is the Riemann zeta function,
\gamma
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant,
w
is the cardinality of the group
\mu
of roots of unity in
E
and
Q=[\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\times}:\mu\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}]\in\{1,2\}
is the Hasse unit index of
E/F
.

Proof.

On combining
h(\Phi)\geq-\tfrac{g}{2}\log(2\pi^{2})
in (
7.10
) with the explicit analytic estimates worked out in (the proof of) Lemma
7.11
, we see that (
7.8
) implies Lemma
7.10
.
∎

Class field theory gives that
h_{F}
divides
h_{E}

and then Lemma
7.10
provides an explicit upper bound for
h(\Phi)
in terms of
\epsilon
,
D_{E}
and
g
. This bound is sufficient for our purpose, but it is asymptotically substantially weaker than Tsimerman’s bound
h(\Phi)\leq D_{E}^{o_{g}(1)}
.

7.2.2
Explicit analytic estimates

We now work out the explicit analytic estimate which we used in Lemma
7.10
above. As before,
\zeta(s)
is the Riemann zeta function and
\gamma
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be a real number with
\epsilon\leq 1/2
, let
E
be a CM field with maximal totally real subfield
F
, and let
L^{\prime}(s)
be the derivative of the L-function
L(s)
of the character of
\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}
corresponding to
E/F
. Put
g=[F:\mathbb{Q}]
and
\mathfrak{c}=D_{E}/D_{F}
.

(i)

If the real part
\sigma
of
s\in\mathbb{C}
satisfies
-\epsilon\leq\sigma\leq 1+\epsilon
then

|L(s)|\leq\zeta(1+\epsilon)\left(\tfrac{\mathfrak{c}}{(2\pi)^{g}}|1+s|^{g}\right)^{\tfrac{1+\epsilon-\sigma}{2}}.

(ii)

Let
h_{E}
and
h_{F}
be the class number of
E
and
F
respectively, let
w
be the number of roots of unity in
E
and let
Q
be the Hasse unit index of
E/F
. It holds

\left|\tfrac{L^{\prime}(0)}{L(0)}\right|\leq 2a\tfrac{h_{F}}{h_{E}}\mathfrak{c}^{1/2}\max\bigl((\tfrac{2\pi}{2+\epsilon})^{g},\mathfrak{c}\bigl)^{\epsilon}+\log\mathfrak{c}+b,

where
a=\tfrac{wQ}{2\epsilon}\zeta(1+\epsilon)\tfrac{(2+\epsilon)^{g\epsilon}}{(2\pi)^{g(\epsilon+1)}}
and
b=g\log(2\pi e^{\gamma})
.

Here (ii) is a standard consequence of (i) and the analytic class number formula, while the bound in (i) is up to a factor
\zeta(1+\epsilon)^{g-1}
due to Rademacher
[
Rad60
, Thm 5]
. In fact we shall obtain (i) by applying his arguments with our entire Artin L-function
L(s)
.

Proof of Lemma
7.11
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be a real number with
\epsilon\leq 1/2
. We first introduce some notation. For each
s\in\mathbb{C}
we write
s=\sigma+it
with
\sigma,t\in\mathbb{R}
and we denote by
S(a,b)
the strip
a\leq\sigma\leq b
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
. Let
\chi:G\to\mathbb{C}
be the non-trivial irreducible character of
G=\textup{Aut}(E/F)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. In particular
\chi
has degree one with image
\chi(G)=\{-1,1\}
, and
L(s)
coincides with the Artin L-function of
\chi
.

We now prove (i). In the case
\sigma\geq 1+\epsilon
, it holds
\log L(s)=\sum_{p}\sum_{n\geq 1}\tfrac{1}{n}\chi(p^{n})p^{-ns}
with
\chi(p^{n})\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
|\chi(p^{n})|\leq 1
and hence
|\log L(s)|\leq\log\zeta(\sigma)
which implies

|L(s)|\leq\zeta(1+\epsilon).

(7.9)

Let
\Gamma(s)
be the Gamma function. The field
E
is CM with maximal totally real subfield
F
. Thus the conductor
\mathfrak{c}
of the non-trivial character
\chi
of
G
is given by
\mathfrak{c}=D_{E}/D_{F}
and the completed Artin L-function
\Lambda(s)
takes the following form

\Lambda(s)=\mathfrak{c}^{s/2}L_{\infty}(s)L(s),\quad L_{\infty}(s)=\pi^{-g(s+1)/2}\Gamma(\tfrac{s+1}{2})^{g}.

As the character
\chi
is fixed by complex conjugation, the functional equation becomes
\Lambda(s)=W(\chi)\Lambda(1-s)
for some
W(\chi)\in\mathbb{C}
with absolute value 1. Therefore we obtain

L(s)=W(\chi)\left(\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{\pi^{g}}\right)^{1/2-s}\left(\frac{\Gamma(1-\tfrac{s}{2})}{\Gamma(\tfrac{s+1}{2})}\right)^{g}L(1-s).

Next, an application of Rademacher’s
[
Rad60
, Lem 2]
with
Q=1
gives for all
s
in the strip
S(-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})
that
|\Gamma(1-\tfrac{s}{2})/\Gamma(\tfrac{s+1}{2})|
is at most
\left(\tfrac{1}{2}|1+s|\right)^{1/2-\sigma}
. Then, as
0<\epsilon\leq 1/2
by assumption, the above displayed expression for
L(s)
together with (
7.9
) shows

|L(-\epsilon+it)|\leq A|1-\epsilon+it|^{(1/2+\epsilon)g},\quad A=\bigl(\tfrac{\mathfrak{c}}{(2\pi)^{g}}\bigl)^{1/2+\epsilon}\zeta(1+\epsilon).

Further (
7.9
) provides
|L(1+\epsilon+it)|\leq\zeta(1+\epsilon)
, and the Artin
L
-function
L(s)
of our non-trivial
\chi
is entire since
G
is abelian. Then an application of Rademacher’s version
[
Rad60
, Thm 2]
of the Phragmén–Lindelöf theorem with
Q=1
,
\alpha=(1/2+\epsilon)g
and
\beta=0
gives for all
s
in the strip
S(-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)
an upper bound for
|L(s)|
as claimed in (i).

We now prove (ii). For this purpose we take the radius
r=\epsilon
and the entire function
f(z)=L(z)
in the basic inequality
|f^{\prime}(1)|r\leq\sup_{|z-1|=r}|f(z)|
which follows from Cauchy’s integral formula. This together with (i) leads to

|L^{\prime}(1)|\leq\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}\zeta(1+\epsilon)\max\bigl(1,\tfrac{\mathfrak{c}}{(2\pi)^{g}}(2+\epsilon)^{g}\bigl)^{\epsilon}.

We next express
L(1)
in terms of class numbers. As
G\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, the Artin L-function
L(s)
of the non-trivial
\chi
is the quotient
L(s)=\zeta_{E}(s)/\zeta_{F}(s)
of the Dedekind zeta functions of
E
and
F
. Then the analytic class number formula and
[
Was82
, Prop 4.16]
imply

L(1)=\tfrac{(2\pi)^{g}}{wQ}\tfrac{h_{E}}{h_{F}}\mathfrak{c}^{-1/2}.

To relate the values
L^{\prime}(0)/L(0)
and
L^{\prime}(1)/L(1)
, we differentiate the functional equation
\Lambda(s)=W(\chi)\Lambda(1-s)
and we use that the Digamma function
\psi(z)=\Gamma^{\prime}(z)/\Gamma(z)
has special values
\psi(1/2)=-2\log 2-\gamma
and
\psi(1)=-\gamma
respectively. Then we obtain

-\tfrac{L^{\prime}(0)}{L(0)}=\tfrac{L^{\prime}(1)}{L(1)}+\log\mathfrak{c}-g\log(2\pi e^{\gamma}).

Finally, on combining the above displayed results we deduce an upper bound for
|L^{\prime}(0)/L(0)|
as claimed in (ii). This completes the proof of Lemma
7.11
.
∎

7.2.3
Ramification of CM endomorphism algebras

The endomorphism algebra of a simple complex abelian variety with CM is a CM field whose ramification over
\mathbb{Q}
can be controlled by CM theory. The goal of this subsection is to prove the following result in which
N_{S}=\prod_{v\in S^{c}}N_{v}
is as above.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
K
be a number field, let
A
be an abelian scheme over a nonempty open subscheme
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
and let
B
be a simple factor of
A_{\bar{K}}
such that
B
has CM.

(i)

The CM field
E=\textup{End}^{0}(B)
is unramified at each rational prime
p\nmid D_{K}N_{S}
.

(ii)

If
B
and all its endomorphisms are defined over
K\subset\bar{K}
, then the CM field
E
is unramified at each rational prime
p\nmid D_{K}
.

Proof.

Let
p
be a rational prime. To show that (ii) implies (i), we assume that (ii) holds. Let
L
be the field of definition of the endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{K}}
. Poincaré’s reducibility theorem implies that
B
is isogenous to an abelian subvariety
B^{\prime}
of
A_{\bar{K}}
and that
B^{\prime}
and all its endomorphisms are defined over
L
. Further
B^{\prime}
is a simple factor of
A_{\bar{K}}
and
B^{\prime}
has CM. Then an application of (ii) with
B^{\prime}
gives that
E\cong\textup{End}^{0}(B^{\prime})
is unramified at
p
if
p\nmid D_{L}
. Further
[
Sil92
, Thm 4.1]
implies that
L/K
is unramified over
S
, since the generic fiber of the abelian scheme
A
over
S
is an abelian variety over
K
with good reduction over
S
. Thus
\textnormal{rad}(D_{L})
divides
D_{K}N_{S}
by Dedekind’s discriminant theorem and therefore we conclude that
E
is unramified at
p
if
p\nmid N_{S}D_{K}
as claimed in (i).

We now prove (ii). By assumption
B
is a simple abelian variety with CM. Hence the CM type
\Phi
of
E
associated to
(B,\textnormal{id})
is simple. The reflex field
E^{*}
of
(E,\Phi)
is defined by
E^{*}=\mathbb{Q}(\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\varphi(e);e\in E)
, and
[
Lan83
, Thm 3.1.1]
gives
E^{*}\hookrightarrow K
since
B
and all its endomorphisms are defined over
K
by assumption. Thus the Galois closure
(E^{*})^{\textnormal{cl}}
of
E^{*}
is unramified at
p
if
p\nmid D_{K}
. Further, an application of
[
Lan83
, Thm 1.5.2]
with our simple
\Phi
identifies
E
with the reflex field of
(E^{*},\Phi^{*})
for some CM type
\Phi^{*}
of
E^{*}
and thus
E\hookrightarrow(E^{*})^{\textnormal{cl}}
. Hence

E
is unramified at
p
if
p\nmid D_{K}
as claimed in (ii).∎

7.3
Proof of the effective Shafarevich conjecture

Let
K
be a number field, let
S
be a nonempty open subscheme of
\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
and let
g\geq 1
be a rational integer. Following the strategy of proof outlined in the introduction, we prove in this section the effective Shafarevich conjecture (see Theorem
7.1
) for abelian varieties over
K
which are of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies or which have CM.

No CM case.

In the first part of the proof of Theorem
7.1
, we consider abelian varieties which have no CM. In Section
7.3.2
we prove the following result by reducing the problem via Proposition
7.2
to the known case
K=\mathbb{Q}
stated in (
7.16
).

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
with no CM. Suppose that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies and assume
(*)
. Then

h_{F}(A)\leq(4gd)^{144gd}\textnormal{rad}(D_{K}N_{S})^{24}.

Here
h_{F}
is the stable Faltings height (
\mathsection
3.4
), while
D_{K}
,
d=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
and
N_{S}=\prod_{v\in S^{c}}N_{v}
are as before. Further, we recall that condition
(*)
means that
K/\mathbb{Q}
is normal and that all endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{K}}
and all isogenies
\mu_{\sigma}
in (
7.1
) are defined over
K\subset\bar{K}=\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
.

CM case.

In the second part of the proof of Theorem
7.1
, we deal with the general CM case by reducing to the situation
A=A_{\Phi}
which was treated in Lemma
7.10
. In course of the proof (
\mathsection
7.3.3
) we shall obtain the following bound which only depends on
K
and
g
.

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
such that
A_{\bar{K}}
has CM. If all endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{K}}
are defined over
K\subset\bar{K}
, then

h_{F}(A)\leq(3g)^{(5g)^{2}}\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})^{5g}.

In the remaining of this section, we first review some known results and then we give the proofs of Proposition
7.13
, Proposition
7.14
and Theorem
7.1
.

7.3.1
Properties of the Faltings height and isogeny estimates

Let
K
be a number field and let
A
be an abelian variety over
K
of dimension
g
. In this section we review properties of the stable Faltings height
h_{F}
of
A
used in our proofs. In particular, we collect effective bounds for the variation of
h_{F}
under isogenies.

We start with the following basic property: Any abelian variety
A^{\prime}
over
K
satisfies
h_{F}(A\times_{K}A^{\prime})=h_{F}(A)+h_{F}(A^{\prime})
. Further, Faltings’ work
[
Fal83
]
gives that
h_{F}(A)\geq c_{g}
for some constant
c_{g}
depending only
g
; see
[
Del85
, 1.22]
. In fact Bost
[
Bos96a
]
obtained

h_{F}(A)\geq-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi^{2})g.

(7.10)

See for example
[
GR14b
, Cor 8.4]
and notice that
h_{F}(A)=h_{B}(A)-\frac{g}{2}\log\pi
where

h_{B}
denotes the height which appears in the statement of
[
GR14b
, Cor 8.4]
.

Variation under isogenies.

We now suppose that
A^{\prime}
is an abelian variety over
K
which is isogenous to
A
. Faltings’ formula
[
Fal83
, Lem 5]
implies that

|h_{F}(A)-h_{F}(A^{\prime})|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\log\deg(\varphi)

(7.11)

for any isogeny
\varphi:A\to A^{\prime}
. Moreover, Faltings
[
Fal83
]
bounded
|h_{F}(A)-h_{F}(A^{\prime})|
in terms of
A/K
alone. On using Faltings’ arguments and some refinements of Paršin–Zarhin, Raynaud
[
Ray85
, Thm 4.4.9]
worked out an effective bound for the variation under the assumption that
A
has everywhere semi-stable reduction, that is

|h_{F}(A)-h_{F}(A^{\prime})|\leq c_{A}

(7.12)

with
c_{A}
an effective constant (simplified in
[
vK21
, Prop 6.10]
) depending only on
g
,
D_{K}
,
[K:\mathbb{Q}]
and the bad reduction places of
A
. In fact the semi-stable assumption is not required here (
[
vK21
, Lem 3.1]
). The above bounds for the variation all do not use Diophantine approximation or transcendence techniques.
Masser–Wüstholz
[
MW93b
,
MW93a
,
MW95
]
obtained bounds via a completely different approach using their isogeny estimates based on transcendence theory. These isogeny estimates are fully effective (
[
Bos96b
, p.121]
), and completely explicit constants were obtained by Gaudron–Rémond
[
GR14a
]
: Their most recent version
[
GR23
, Thm 1.9]
gives isogenies
A\to A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}\to A
of degree at most
\kappa(A)
and this combined with (
7.11
) proves

\lvert h_{F}(A)-h_{F}(A^{\prime})\rvert\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\log\kappa(A),\quad\kappa(A)=\bigl((7g)^{8g^{2}}d\max(h_{F}(A),\log d,1)\bigl)^{4g^{2}}

(7.13)

for
d=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
; one can replace here
\kappa(A)
by
\kappa(A)^{1/2}
if
\textup{End}(A)
identifies with a maximal order of
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
. We remark that (
7.12
) improves (
7.13
) in some cases, and vice versa in other cases. However, in the situations considered in this paper, it turns out that (
7.13
) leads to exponentially better bounds for the same reasons as in
[
vK21
, Rem 5.2]
.

7.3.2
Proof of Proposition
7.13

We continue our notation. Let
K
be a number field and let
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
be a nonempty open subscheme. As before we write
d=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
and
N_{S}=\prod_{v\in S^{c}}N_{v}
. Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
which has no CM. We suppose that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies and we assume that condition
(*)
is satisfied.

Proof of Proposition
7.13
.

Let
U\subseteq S
and
T\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
be the open subschemes defined in (
7.3
) with
k=\mathbb{Q}
. Our
A
satisfies all the assumptions in Proposition
7.2
. Thus this proposition gives a simple abelian scheme
C
over
T
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type such that
A_{U}
is isogenous to a power
B^{m}
of a simple factor
B
of
C_{U}
. As
B
is a factor of
C_{U}
and
U
is a connected Dedekind scheme, Poincaré’s reducibility theorem and (
6.1
) provide an abelian scheme
D
over
U
such that
B\times_{U}D
is isogenous to
C_{U}
. Now, on using isogenies

A_{U}\to B^{m}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad B\times_{U}D\to C_{U},

we can estimate the heights as in
[
vK21
,
\mathsection
5.2.2]
. For any abelian scheme
X
over an open
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
, we denote by
v_{X}
the maximal variation of
h_{F}
in the isogeny class of
X
; that is
v_{X}=\sup\lvert h_{F}(X)-h_{F}(X^{\prime})\rvert
with the supremum taken over all abelian schemes
X^{\prime}
over
S^{\prime}
which are isogenous to
X
. The abelian scheme
A^{\prime}=B^{m}
over
U
satisfies

h_{F}(A^{\prime})=mh_{F}(B)\ \ \textnormal{ and }\ \ h_{F}(A)\leq v_{A^{\prime}}+h_{F}(A^{\prime})

(7.14)

since
A_{U}
is isogenous to
A^{\prime}
. The stable height
h_{F}
is invariant under the base change
U\to T
, and
B\times_{U}D
is isogenous to
C_{U}
. Thus we obtain that
h_{F}(B)
is at most
v_{C_{U}}+h_{F}(C)-h_{F}(D)
and then we see that the lower bound for
h_{F}(D)
in (
7.10
) implies

h_{F}(B)\leq v_{C_{U}}+h_{F}(C)+\tfrac{g}{2}\log(2\pi^{2}).

(7.15)

Here we used that the relative dimension of
D
over
U
is at most
g
. The relative dimension of
B
over
U
is
g/m
. Thus the relative dimension
n
of
C
over
T
satisfies
n\leq dg/m
by (
7.4
), and applying
[
vK21
, Thm A]
with the abelian scheme
C
over
T
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type gives

h_{F}(C)\leq(3n)^{144n}N_{T}^{24}.

(7.16)

As
N_{T}=\textnormal{rad}(D_{K}N_{S})
we then
see that (
7.13
), (
7.14
) and (
7.15
) lead to an upper bound for
h_{F}(A)
as claimed in Proposition
7.13
. We now include some details of our computations described above. For example, one can use the inequalities

v_{C_{U}}\leq\tfrac{1}{4}c,\quad h_{F}(B)\leq\tfrac{3}{2}c,\quad h_{F}(A^{\prime})\leq\tfrac{3}{2}gc\ \textnormal{ and }\ v_{A^{\prime}}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}c

as intermediate steps to deduce that
h_{F}(A)\leq 2gc
where
c=(3gd)^{144gd}N_{T}^{24}
.∎

7.3.3
Proof of Proposition
7.14
and Theorem
7.1

We continue our notation. Let
K
be a number field of degree
d
over
\mathbb{Q}
, let
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
be a nonempty open subscheme with geometric generic point
\bar{s}=\textnormal{Spec}(\bar{K})

and write again
N_{S}=\prod_{v\in S^{c}}N_{v}
. Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
.

Proof of Proposition
7.14
.

As in the statement of Proposition
7.14
, we assume that
A_{\bar{s}}
has CM and that
all endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{s}}
are defined over
K\subset\bar{K}
. Then (
6.1
) gives that
\textup{End}(A)\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}(A_{\bar{s}})
. We claim that there exist simple factors
A_{i}
of
A
of relative dimension
g_{i}
, CM fields
E_{i}
of degree
2g_{i}
with
\mathcal{O}_{E_{i}}\cong\textup{End}(A_{i})
and an isogeny

A^{\prime}=\prod A_{i}^{e_{i}}\to A,\quad e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

(7.17)

To prove this claim, let
A_{i}
be a simple factor of
A
and write
g_{i}
for the relative dimension of
A_{i}
over
S
. On using that
\textup{End}(A)\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}(A_{\bar{s}})
, we see that
\textup{End}(A_{i})\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}(A_{i,\bar{s}})
. Then CM theory gives that
A_{i}
has CM since
A_{\bar{s}}
has CM by assumption. As
K
has characteristic zero, this implies that the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{i})
is a CM field
E_{i}
of degree
2g_{i}
.
The order
\mathcal{O}=\textup{End}(A_{i})
of
E
is contained in the maximal order of
E
which is
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}_{E_{i}}
. Hence an application of
[
vKK19
, Lem 5.3]
, which is based on
[
CCO14
,
\mathsection
1.7.4]
, shows that after possibly replacing
A_{i}
by an isogenous abelian scheme over
S
we may and do assume that
\mathcal{O}_{E_{i}}\cong\textup{End}(A_{i})
. Then the claim follows from Poincaré’s reducibility theorem and (
6.1
).

To estimate the heights, we choose an embedding
K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and we let
\Phi_{i}
be the CM type of
E_{i}
associated to
(A_{i,\mathbb{C}},j\otimes\mathbb{Q})
where
j
is the composition of the ring morphisms
\mathcal{O}_{E_{i}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}(A_{i})\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}(A_{i,\mathbb{C}})
. An application of (
7.7
) with
A_{i,K}
gives that
h_{F}(A_{i})=h_{F}(\Phi_{i})
. This implies that
h_{F}(A^{\prime})=\sum e_{i}h(\Phi_{i})
and then (
7.17
) shows

h_{F}(A)\leq v_{A^{\prime}}+\sum e_{i}h(\Phi_{i}),\quad g=\sum e_{i}g_{i}.

To bound
h(\Phi_{i})
in terms of
D_{K}
and
g
, we first control
D_{E_{i}}
. As
\textup{End}(A_{i})\cong\textup{End}(A_{i,\bar{s}})
we see that
A_{i,\bar{s}}
has the following properties: It has CM, it is a simple factor of
A_{\bar{s}}
, it is defined over
K\subset\bar{K}
and all its endomorphisms are defined over
K\subset\bar{K}
. Then an application of Lemma
7.12
(ii) with
B=A_{i,\bar{s}}
gives that the CM field
E_{i}\cong\textup{End}^{0}(A_{i,\bar{s}})
of degree
2g_{i}
is unramified at each rational prime
p\nmid D_{K}
. Hence
[
vK13b
, Lem 6.2]
, which is a direct consequence of Dedekind’s discriminant theorem, implies

D_{E_{i}}\leq\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})^{5g}(2g)^{24g^{2}}.

Here we used the effective version of the prime number theorem in
[
RS62
, (3.12) and (3.16)]
which gives that the number
n
of rational prime divisors of
D_{K}
satisfies
n^{n}<\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})^{3/2}
in the case when
n>12
. Next, we recall that Lemma
7.10
and the subsequent discussion give an explicit upper bound for
h(\Phi_{i})
in terms of
\epsilon
,
D_{E_{i}}
and
g_{i}
. We take
\epsilon=1/2
in this bound and then the above displayed results together with the bound for
v_{A^{\prime}}
in (
7.13
) and
d\leq 3\max(1,\log D_{K})
lead to an estimate for
h_{F}(A)
as claimed in Proposition
7.14
.

We now include some details of the explicit computations. For example, to compute the final bound one can use the following inequalities as intermediate steps:

\zeta(\tfrac{3}{2})\leq 3,\quad a_{i}\leq\tfrac{w_{i}}{2}\leq 4g_{i}^{2},\quad h_{F}(A_{i})=h(\Phi_{i})\leq(3g)^{24g^{2}}\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})^{5g}.

Here
\zeta(s)
is the Riemann zeta function and
a_{i},\omega_{i}
are as in Lemma
7.10
with
E=E_{i}
; a proof of the bound
w_{i}\leq 8g_{i}^{2}
can be found in the discussion right after (
8.4
). ∎

Proof of Theorem
7.1
(ii).

Assume that
A_{\bar{s}}
has CM and let
L
be the field of definition of the endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{s}}
. The height
h_{F}
is invariant under the base change from
S
to
S^{\prime}=S\times_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathcal{O}_{L}
. Then, on replacing in the proof of Proposition
7.14
the application of part (ii) of Lemma
7.12
by part (i), we obtain the claimed bound for
h_{F}(A)=h_{F}(A_{S^{\prime}})
.∎

Proof of Theorem
7.1
(i).

Assume first that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies such that
A_{\bar{s}}
has no CM. Then let
L\supseteq K
be the number field constructed in the proof of Lemma
7.6
and denote by
S^{\prime}
the preimage of
S
under the projection
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\to\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
.

We now verify that
A_{S^{\prime}}
satisfies all assumptions in Proposition
7.13
. Lemma
7.6
provides that
(*)
holds. In particular all endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{s}}
are defined over
L
and then base change induces an isomorphism
\textup{End}(A_{S^{\prime}})\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}(A_{\bar{s}})
by (
6.1
). This shows that
A_{S^{\prime}}
has no CM and that
A_{S^{\prime}}
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies, since
A_{\bar{s}}
has these two properties by assumption. Hence all assumptions in Proposition
7.13
are satisfied.

We next control
L
and
S^{\prime}
. Lemma
7.6
gives that the extension
L/\mathbb{Q}
is unramified over
T=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/n])
for
n=N_{S}D_{K}
and that the field
L
is contained in the Galois closure of
K(A_{3})/\mathbb{Q}
. Thus Dedekind’s discriminant theorem and basic Galois theory lead to

\textnormal{rad}(N_{S^{\prime}}D_{L})\mid\textnormal{rad}(N_{S}D_{K})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad[L:\mathbb{Q}]\leq l=(d3^{4g^{2}})!.

(7.18)

It holds that
h_{F}(A)=h_{F}(A_{S^{\prime}})
since the generic fiber of
A_{S^{\prime}}
identifies with
A_{K}\times_{K}L
. Hence (the computation used in the proof) of Proposition
7.13
provides a bound for
h_{F}(A)=h_{F}(A_{S^{\prime}})
in terms of
L
and
S^{\prime}
which together with (
7.18
) implies

h_{F}(A)\leq 2g(3gl)^{144gl}\textnormal{rad}(N_{S}D_{K})^{24}.

(7.19)

This completes the proof of Theorem
7.1
(i) in the case when the abelian scheme
A
over
S
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies such that
A_{\bar{s}}
has no CM.

More generally, we now assume that
A
is of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies. Then
A
is isogenous to a product
A^{\prime}=\prod A_{i}
of abelian schemes
A_{i}
over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies. It holds that
h_{F}(A)\leq v_{A^{\prime}}+\sum h_{F}(A_{i})
and the relative dimensions
g_{i}
of
A_{i}
over
S
satisfy
g=\sum g_{i}
. Hence, on combining the above established case and Theorem
7.1
(ii) with the bound for
v_{A^{\prime}}
provided by (
7.13
), we deduce Theorem
7.1
(i) in general. ∎

7.4
Bounding the number of isomorphism classes

Let
K
be a number field, let
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
be nonempty open and let
g\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. In this section we use the strategy outlined in the introduction to bound the number of isomorphism classes of abelian schemes over
S
of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies.

Before we give in
\mathsection
7.4.1
a proof of Theorem B (ii) in full generality, we consider a relevant special situation where we obtain a better bound which is used in Theorem
4.1
.

Proposition 7.15
.

Suppose that
K/\mathbb{Q}
is normal and let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
. Then the number of isomorphism classes of abelian schemes
A
over
S
of relative dimension
g
such that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies and
K=K(A_{n})
is at most
(9gd)^{(4g)^{5}d}\textnormal{rad}(D_{K}N_{S})^{(4g)^{4}}.

Here
d=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
,
D_{K}
and
N_{S}=\prod_{v\in S^{c}}N_{v}
are as before. We now prove some lemmas which we shall use in the proof of Proposition
7.15
given below. To control the size of each isogeny class, we apply minimal isogeny degree estimates for abelian schemes
A
over
S
which are based on transcendence (
\mathsection
7.3.1
) and we let
\kappa=\kappa(A_{K})
be as in (
7.13
).

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
. Up to isomorphisms, there exist at most
\kappa^{2g}
abelian schemes over
S
which are isogenous to
A
.

Proof.

The statement follows by using the same arguments as in step 3. of the proof of
[
vK21
, Thm 6.6]
where the exponent
2g
comes from Masser–Wüstholz
[
MW93a
, Lem 6.1]
. The details are as follows: If
A^{\prime}
is an abelian scheme over
S
which is isogenous to
A
, then there exists an isogeny
\varphi:A\to A^{\prime}
of degree at most
\kappa
by Gaudron–Rémond
[
GR23
, Thm 1.9]
and (
6.1
). As
A^{\prime}
represents the fppf quotient
A/\ker(\varphi)
,
the number of such
A^{\prime}
up to isomorphisms is thus at most the number of subgroups of
A(\bar{K})^{\textnormal{tor}}
of cardinality at most
\kappa
. Then
[
MW93a
, Lem 6.1]
and
A(\bar{K})^{\textnormal{tor}}\cong(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})^{2g}
imply Lemma
7.16
. ∎

Next, we bound the number of isogeny classes. If
A
is an abelian scheme over
S
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies satisfying condition
(*)
as in
\mathsection
7.1.2
, then any abelian scheme over
S
which is isogenous to
A
is again of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies satisfying
(*)
. Proposition
7.2
and the proof of
[
vK21
, Thm 6.6]
lead to the following result.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the set of isogeny classes of abelian schemes
A
over
S
of relative dimension
g
such that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies satisfying
(*)
and such that
A
has no CM. It holds
|\mathcal{C}|\leq 9^{9dg}\textnormal{rad}(D_{K}N_{S})^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
U\subseteq S
and
T\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
be the open subschemes defined in (
7.3
). For each isogeny class
[A]
in
\mathcal{C}
, Proposition
7.2
provides a simple abelian scheme
C
over
T
of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type such that
A_{U}
is isogenous to a power of a simple factor
B
of
C_{U}
. Moreover inequality (
7.4
) assures that the abelian scheme
C
over
T
has relative dimension at most
dg
. Therefore we see that
[A]\mapsto[B]
defines an injective map

\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}

into the set
\mathcal{D}
of isogeny classes of the simple factors of
C_{U}
where
C
is a simple abelian scheme over
T
of relative dimension at most
dg
which is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type. For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
the arguments in steps 2. and 4. of the proof of
[
vK21
, Thm 6.6]
show the following: There are at most
\tfrac{1}{24}\nu
distinct isogeny classes of simple abelian schemes over
T
of relative dimension
n
which are of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type, where
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfies
\nu\leq N_{T}^{2}(2n+1)^{6\rho}
for
\rho
the number of rational primes at most
2n+1
. This implies that
|\mathcal{D}|\leq 9^{9dg}N_{T}^{2}
since the explicit prime number theorem in
[
RS62
, (3.6)]
gives
\sum_{n\leq dg}n(2n+1)^{6\rho}\leq 9^{9dg}
. Then the inclusion
\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}
together with
N_{T}=\textnormal{rad}(D_{K}N_{S})
proves Lemma
7.17
. ∎

Next we deal with abelian varieties which have CM. To explicitly bound their isogeny classes, we reduce to the geometric situation over
\bar{K}
and then we apply CM theory which reduces the problem to count CM fields with bounded degree and discriminant.

Lemma 7.18
.

If
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
then up to isogenies there exist at most
(2g)^{(4g)^{3}}\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})^{10g^{2}}
distinct abelian varieties
A
over
K
of dimension
g
such that
A_{\bar{K}}
has CM and
K=K(A_{n})
.

Proof.

We take
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
. Let
A
be an abelian variety over
K
of dimension
g
such that
K=K(A_{n})
and
A_{\bar{s}}
has CM where
\bar{s}=\textnormal{Spec}(\bar{K})
. Silverberg
[
Sil92
, Thm 2.4]
gives that all endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{s}}
are defined over
K\subset\bar{K}
since
K=K(A_{n})
. Hence we can apply the arguments of the proof of Proposition
7.14
. They show that
A_{\bar{s}}
is isogenous to a product
\prod A_{i}^{e_{i}}
with
e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
A_{i}
simple pairwise non-isogenous abelian varieties over
\bar{K}
of dimension
g_{i}
such that
E_{i}=\textup{End}^{0}(A_{i})
is a CM field of degree
2g_{i}
with
D_{E_{i}}\leq D
for
D=\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})^{5g}(2g)^{24g^{2}}
. The number of isomorphism classes of CM fields
E_{i}
of degree
2g_{i}
and discriminant
D_{E_{i}}\leq D
is bounded by the explicit version (
7.20
) of Hermite’s theorem, and each CM field
E_{i}
has precisely
2^{g_{i}}
distinct CM types; in this proof the CM types are
\bar{K}
-valued in the sense of
[
CCO14
, p.66]
. Further CM theory (
[
CCO14
, 1.5.4.1]
) gives that the abelian variety
A_{i}
over
\bar{K}
is determined up to an
E_{i}
-compatible isogeny by the isomorphism class of the CM type associated in
[
CCO14
, p.66]
to
A_{i}
, where two CM types
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
, of CM fields
E
and
E^{\prime}
respectively, are isomorphic if there exists an isomorphism
\sigma:E\overset{\sim}{\to}E^{\prime}
of fields with
\Phi=\Phi^{\prime}\sigma
. On combining these observations, we find that up to isogenies there exist at most
m=g2^{(2g+1)^{2}}(2D)^{2g}
distinct abelian varieties
A_{i}
over
\bar{K}
as above. As
A_{\bar{s}}
is isogenous to
\prod A_{i}^{e_{i}}
and
g=\sum e_{i}g_{i}
, a combinatorial argument (
[
vK21
, p.21]
) shows that the number of isogeny classes of
A_{\bar{s}}
with
A
as above is bounded by
m^{g}
. Then the number of isogeny classes of
A
as above is at most
m^{g}
since
K=K(A_{n})
. Indeed, if
A
and
B
are abelian varieties over
K
with
K=K(A_{n})=K(B_{n})
then Silverberg
[
Sil92
, Thm 2.4]
gives that
\textup{Hom}(A,B)\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{Hom}(A_{\bar{s}},B_{\bar{s}})
and hence
A
is isogenous to
B
if and only if
A_{\bar{s}}
is isogenous to
B_{\bar{s}}
.
This completes the proof of the lemma. ∎

We point out that Orr–Skorobogatov
[
OS18
, Thm A]
obtained a uniform finiteness result for
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-isomorphism classes of abelian varieties of CM type of given dimension which can be defined over a number field of given degree; their arguments use results of Tsimerman
[
Tsi18
]
and Masser–Wüstholz
[
MW93a
]
and also Zarhin’s quaternion trick. As a (polynomial) bound in terms of
\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})
is sufficient for our purpose, we shall use Lemma
7.18
which has the advantage of providing a dependence on
g
and
K
.

We are now ready to prove Proposition
7.15
and we combine the above lemmas with the explicit height bounds obtained in Propositions
7.13
and
7.14
.

Proof of Proposition
7.15
.

Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
which is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies. Suppose that
K/\mathbb{Q}
is normal and that
K=K(A_{n})
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
. Then Lemma
7.6
assures that condition
(*)
in
\mathsection
7.1.2
is satisfied. In particular all endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{K}}
are defined over
K\subset\bar{K}
. Now, we combine Lemma
7.16
with the upper bounds for
h_{F}(A)
in Propositions
7.13
and
7.14
. This shows that the number of isomorphism classes of abelian schemes over
S
which are isogenous to our given
A
is at most
(7gd)^{608g^{5}d}\textnormal{rad}(D_{K}N_{S})^{96g^{4}}.

On combining this with the estimates for the number of isogeny classes obtained in Lemmas
7.17
and
7.18
, we deduce an upper bound for the number of isomorphism classes as claimed in Proposition
7.15
.
∎

7.4.1
Proof of Theorem B (ii)

In this section we prove Theorem B (ii) by reducing to the situation treated in Proposition
7.15
. The reduction uses two results: An explicit bound for the number of twists of an abelian variety over a number field (Lemma
7.19
), and an explicit estimate (
7.20
) for the number of number fields of given degree and bounded discriminant.

Twists.

Let
A
be an abelian variety of dimension
g\geq 1
defined over number field
K
. To bound the number of twists of the unpolarized
A
over a finite
L/K
, it seems difficult to extend the standard approach via Galois cohomology which works in the polarized case; see Remark
7.20
. Instead we exploit that each twist embeds into the Weil restriction
R_{L/K}(A_{L})
and then we apply the isogeny estimates (
7.13
) based on transcendence. This leads to the following result which holds moreover for twists up to isogenies.

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
K\subseteq L
be a finite field extension of relative degree
n
. There exist at most
(ng)^{g}
isogeny classes, and at most
(ng)^{g}\kappa(A)^{2g}
isomorphism classes, of abelian varieties
B
over
K
such that
B_{L}
is isogenous to
A_{L}
where
\kappa(A)
is as in (
7.13
).

Proof.

We define
C=R(A_{L})
where
R=R_{L/K}
denotes the Weil restriction from
L
to
K
. Let
B
be an abelian variety over
K
with an isogeny
B_{L}\to A_{L}
. Its image
R(B_{L})\to C
under the functor
R
is a morphism of abelian varieties over
K
. If
S
is a
K
-scheme then
B(S\times_{K}L)\cong R(B_{L})(S)
. On using that
\bar{K}\otimes_{K}L\cong\bar{K}^{n}
, we see that
R(B_{L})\to C
is quasi-finite and hence an isogeny. Further, the projection
S\times_{K}L\to S
induces a morphism

B\hookrightarrow R(B_{L})\to C

of abelian varieties over
K
. Here
B\hookrightarrow R(B_{L})
is a closed immersion since the identity of
B(\bar{K})
factors through
B(\bar{K})\to B(\bar{K}\otimes_{K}L)
via the decomposition
\bar{K}\to\bar{K}\otimes_{K}L\to\prod_{\varphi}\bar{K}\to\bar{K}
of the identity of
\bar{K}
, where
\prod_{\varphi}
ranges over all
K
-morphisms
\varphi:L\to\bar{K}
. Now, we can apply the arguments of steps 2 and 3 of the proof of
[
vK21
, Thm 6.6]
to bound the number of isomorphism classes of
B
as above. We first control the number of isogeny classes. Let
C_{1},\dotsc,C_{m}
be pairwise non-isogenous simple abelian varieties over
K
such that
C
is isogenous to
\prod C_{i}^{n_{i}}
with
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. As
B\hookrightarrow R(B_{L})\to C
has finite kernel, Poincaré’s reducibility theorem gives
e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
B
is isogenous to
\prod C_{i}^{e_{i}}
. Here
\prod C_{i}^{e_{i}}
has dimension
\sum e_{i}g_{i}=\dim(B)
where
g_{i}=\dim(C_{i})
, and
\dim(B)=g
since
B
is geometrically isomorphic to
A
. A combinatorial argument (
[
vK21
, p.21]
) shows that the number of
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m}
with
\sum e_{i}g_{i}=g
is at most
\tbinom{a}{g}
for
a=g+m-1
. Thus the number of distinct
\prod C_{i}^{e_{i}}
, and hence the number of isogeny classes of
B
as above, is at most

\tbinom{a}{g}\leq m^{g}.

Let
[B_{0}]
be such an isogeny class. We now bound the number of isomorphism classes of abelian schemes
B
as above which are inside
[B_{0}]
. There exists an isogeny
B_{0}\to B
of degree at most
\kappa(B)
by (
7.13
), and
\kappa(B)
equals
\kappa(A)
since the geometrically isomorphic
A
and
B
have the same stable Faltings height
h_{F}
. It follows that
[B_{0}]
contains at most
\kappa(A)^{2g}
distinct isomorphism classes of
B
as above. Here we used the arguments of step 3 in
[
vK21
, p.21]
which we applied already in the proof of Lemma
7.16
and which rely on the counting result
[
MW93a
, Lem 6.1]
of Masser–Wüstholz. Then we deduce that the number of isomorphism classes of abelian schemes
B
as above is at most
\tbinom{a}{g}\kappa(A)^{2g}
where
\tbinom{a}{g}\leq m^{g}
. This proves the lemma since
m\leq\dim(C)
and
\dim(C)=ng
. ∎

Remark 7.20
.

(i) The above proof shows that one can refine Lemma
7.19
as follows. One can replace
(ng)^{g}
by the smaller quantity
\tbinom{a}{g}
, where
a=g+m-1
and
m
is the number of isogeny classes of simple factors of the Weil restriction
R(A_{L})
. Also, if the minimal isogeny degree of abelian varieties of dimension
g
over
K
would be bounded by a constant depending only on
g
and
K
, then one could replace
\kappa(A)
by this constant. For example, one can replace
\kappa(A)^{2g}
by the number 8 if
K=\mathbb{Q}
and
g=1
. Indeed this follows by going into the step where we bound the number of isomorphism classes in
[B_{0}]
and by replacing therein (
7.13
) by Kenku’s refinement
[
Ken82
, Thm 2]
of Mazur’s
[
Maz78
, Thm 5]
.

(ii) Let
(A,\lambda)
be a polarized abelian variety over
K
, let
L
be a finite Galois extension of
K
and write
G=\textup{Aut}(L/K)
. The set of isomorphism classes of polarized abelian varieties
(A^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
over
K
such that
(A^{\prime}_{L},\lambda^{\prime}_{L})
is isomorphic to
(A_{L},\lambda_{L})
identifies with the first Galois cohomology group
H^{1}\bigl(G,\textup{Aut}(A_{L},\lambda_{L})\bigl)
.
It should be possible to explicitly bound the cardinality of the finite group
\textup{Aut}(A_{L},\lambda_{L})
and hence of
H^{1}\bigl(G,\textup{Aut}(A_{L},\lambda_{L})\bigl)
. However, the automorphism group of an unpolarized abelian variety over
K
can be infinite and quite complicated. Thus we used a different approach in Lemma
7.19
.

Number fields.

Let
d
and
D
be in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Hermite proved that the number
n
of isomorphism classes of number fields
K
of degree
[K:\mathbb{Q}]=d
and discriminant
D_{K}\leq D
is finite. On making Minkowski’s proof of this result explicit, one obtains for example

n\leq 2^{(d+1)^{2}}D^{d}.

(7.20)

Stronger bounds in terms of
D
are known, see for instance Schmidt
[
Sch95
]
, Ellenberg–Venkatesh
[
EV06
]
and the recent results of Lemke-Oliver–Thorne
[
LOT22
]
, Bhargava–Shankar–Wang
[
BSW22
]
and Anderson et al.
[
AGH+22
]
. As a polynomial bound in terms of
D
is sufficient for our purpose, we shall use (
7.20
) which has the advantage of providing also a dependence on
d
. To compute the bound (
7.20
), we go into the proof of
[
Neu99
, Thm 3.2.13]
. We find that
\textnormal{vol}(X)=\tfrac{4}{\pi}(2\pi)^{s}cD^{1/2}
and
c>\tfrac{\pi}{4}(\tfrac{2}{\pi})^{s}
. Hence, if
K
is totally complex, then it has an integral primitive element
\alpha
such that each coefficient
a
of its minimal polynomial
\prod_{\sigma}(x-\sigma(\alpha))
satisfies
|a|=|\sum\prod\sigma(\alpha)|\leq 2^{d}D
. If
K
has a real embedding
\tau_{0}
, then we take
X=\{(z_{\tau})\in K_{\mathbb{R}};\,|z_{\tau_{0}}|<cD^{1/2},|z_{\tau}|<1\textnormal{ for }\tau\neq\tau_{0}\}
with
\textnormal{vol}(X)=(2\pi)^{s}2^{r}cD^{1/2}
and
c>(\tfrac{2}{\pi})^{s}

which leads again to
|a|\leq 2^{d}D
. This implies (
7.20
).

We are now ready to prove Theorem B (ii). Recall that therein
K
is a number field of degree
d
,
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
is a nonempty open subscheme,
N_{S}=\prod_{v\in S^{c}}N_{v}
and
g\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

Proof of Theorem B (ii).

Let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
. To reduce to the situation treated in Proposition
7.15
, we make a base change to a controlled scheme
T
which is defined as follows: Choose
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
with
K\subset\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
, let
L\subset\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
be the normal closure of
K(A_{3})/\mathbb{Q}
and let
p:\textnormal{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\to\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
be the projection. Now we define

T=p^{-1}(S).

It is a connected Dedekind scheme with function field
L
of degree
[L:\mathbb{Q}]\leq l
where
l=(d3^{4g^{2}})!
. As in the proof of Lemma
7.6
, we see that the criterion of Néron–Ogg–Shafarevich
[
ST68
, Thm 1]
implies that
K(A_{3})
, and hence its normal closure
L
, is unramified over each rational prime
p\nmid 3N_{S}D_{K}
. Thus
\textnormal{rad}(N_{T}D_{L})
divides
\textnormal{rad}(3N_{S}D_{K})
and
[
vK13b
, Lem 6.2]
leads to
D_{L}\leq D
where
D=\textnormal{rad}(3N_{S}D_{K})^{3l}l^{6l^{2}}
.
Here we used the effective version of the prime number theorem in
[
RS62
, (3.12) and (3.16)]
which gives that the number
s
of rational prime divisors of
D_{L}
satisfies
s^{s}<\textnormal{rad}(D_{L})^{2}
if
s>12
.

Let
F(S)
be the set of isomorphism classes of abelian schemes over
S
of relative dimension
g
which are of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies, and let
F^{\prime}(S)
be the set of all
[A]\in F(S)
with
K(A_{3})=K
. The notion of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies is stable under the base change
T\to S
for any
T
as above. Hence
[A]\mapsto[A_{T}]
defines a map

F(S)\to\cup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}F^{\prime}(T).

Here
\mathcal{T}
is the set of all schemes
T=p^{-1}(S)
where
p:\textnormal{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\to\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
is the projection for some number field
K\subseteq L\subset\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
with the following properties:
L/\mathbb{Q}
is normal,
[L:\mathbb{Q}]\leq l
,
D_{L}\leq D
, and
\textnormal{rad}(N_{T}D_{L})
divides
\textnormal{rad}(3N_{S}D_{K})
.

To bound
|F(S)|
we now control
|\mathcal{T}|
and the degree of the map
F(S)\to\cup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}F^{\prime}(T)
. It follows from (
6.1
) that any two abelian schemes over a connected Dedekind scheme are isomorphic if and only if their generic fibers are isomorphic. Thus Lemma
7.19
gives that the fibers of the map
F(S)\to\cup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}F^{\prime}(T)
have cardinality at most

(lg)^{g}\sup\kappa([A])^{2g}

with the supremum taken over all
[A]\in F(S)
. Here
\kappa([A])=\kappa(A_{K})
and
\kappa(A_{K})
is defined in (
7.13
). Further, the construction of
\mathcal{T}
shows that
|\mathcal{T}|
is bounded by the number of subfields
L\subset\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
with
[L:\mathbb{Q}]\leq l
and
D_{L}\leq D
. Therefore (
7.20
) implies

|\mathcal{T}|\leq l^{2}2^{(l+1)^{2}}D^{l}.

If
T\in\mathcal{T}
then its function field
L=k(T)
is normal over
\mathbb{Q}
and the definition of
F^{\prime}(T)
assures that each
[A]\in F^{\prime}(T)
satisfies
L=L(A_{3})
. Therefore we can apply Proposition
7.15
which bounds
|F^{\prime}(T)|
for all
T\in\mathcal{T}
, while Theorem
7.1
bounds
\sup\kappa([A])
. On combining these bounds with the above displayed results, we deduce

|F(S)|\leq(3gl)^{(6g)^{5}l^{3}}\textnormal{rad}(N_{S}D_{K})^{4l^{2}}.

(7.21)

We now deal with the general case and we consider
A
with the following properties
(\dagger)
:
A
is an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
and
A
is of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies. Let
m
be the number of isogeny classes of abelian schemes over
S
of relative dimension at most
g
which are of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies. Suppose that
A_{1},\dotsc,A_{m}
generate these isogeny classes,
and write
g_{i}
for the relative dimension of
A_{i}
over
S
. Then for any
A
with
(\dagger)
there exists
e\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}_{\geq 0}
with
g=\sum e_{i}g_{i}
such that
A
is isogenous to
\prod A_{i}^{e_{i}}
. Thus a combinatorial argument, which we used already in the proof of Lemma
7.19
, shows that the number of isogeny classes of abelian schemes
A
with
(\dagger)
is at most
m^{g}
.
Then Lemma
7.16
gives that the number of isomorphism classes of abelian schemes
A
with
(\dagger)
is at most
\sup\kappa(A)^{2g}m^{g}
with the supremum taken over all
A
with
(\dagger)
for
\kappa(A)=\kappa(A_{K})
as in (
7.13
). Theorem
7.1
bounds
\sup\kappa(A)
, and (
7.21
) leads to

m\leq\sum c_{k}\leq\tfrac{g}{2}(g+1)c_{g}.

Here the sum is taken over all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
with
k\leq g
, and
c_{k}
denotes the expression obtained by replacing
g
by
k
in the upper bound of (
7.21
). We combine the bounds and then we deduce the estimate for the number of isomorphism classes claimed in Theorem B (ii).
∎

Section 8
Polarizations

Let
L
be a totally real number field of degree
g
over
\mathbb{Q}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, and let
I
be a nonzero finitely generated
\mathcal{O}
-submodule of
L
with a positivity notion
I_{+}
. In this section we continue our study in
[
vKK19
,
\mathsection
9]
of equivalence classes of
I
-polarizations on
\mathcal{O}
-abelian schemes of relative dimension
g
. In particular, we explicitly bound the number of such equivalence classes by using and generalizing the arguments of
[
vKK19
,
\mathsection
9]
.

Let
S
be a connected Dedekind scheme with function field of characteristic zero, let
\mathcal{C}
be the category of
\mathcal{O}
-abelian schemes over
S
and let
(A,\iota)
be an
\mathcal{O}
-abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
. We denote by
\Phi
the set of
I
-polarizations on
A
.

Equivalence relation.

Recall from Section
3.1
that an
I
-polarization on
A
is an
\mathcal{O}
-module morphism from
I
to the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,A^{\vee})^{\textnormal{sym}}
, which maps
I_{+}
to polarizations and which induces an isomorphism
I\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}A\overset{\sim}{\to}A^{\vee}
. We say that two
I
-polarizations
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\in\Phi
are equivalent, and we write
\varphi\sim\varphi^{\prime}
, if there exists an automorphism
\sigma
of the
\mathcal{O}
-abelian scheme
A
such that
\varphi(\lambda)=\sigma^{\vee}\varphi^{\prime}(\lambda)\sigma
for all
\lambda\in I
.

Equivalence classes.

To state our bound for the equivalence classes, we denote by
w
and
w^{\prime}
the number of roots of unity in
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
respectively.

Proposition 8.1
.

The number of equivalence classes satisfies

|\Phi/{\sim}|\leq\tfrac{w^{2}}{w^{\prime}}\cdot 2^{g-1}.

The number
w
is uniformly bounded: For example (
8.4
) gives that
w\leq 4g^{2}
and
\omega\ll_{\epsilon}g^{1+\epsilon}
for all real
\epsilon>0
, while Lemma
8.3
shows that
w=w^{\prime}=2
if the abelian scheme
A
over
S
has no CM. In particular we obtain that
w=w^{\prime}=2
if the function field of
S
embeds into
\mathbb{R}
, and hence
[
vKK19
, Prop 9.1]
is a special case of Proposition
8.1
. An important feature of Proposition
8.1
is that the bound
\tfrac{w^{2}}{w^{\prime}}\cdot 2^{g-1}
can be controlled only in terms of the relative dimension
g
of
A
. This is crucial for deducing the next result which explicitly controls the degree over
S
of the natural forgetful morphism
\phi_{\varphi}\colon M\to\underline{M}
defined in
[
vKK19
, (6.2)]
with respect to the Hilbert moduli stack
\mathcal{M}^{I}
associated to
(L,I,I_{+})
.

Corollary 8.2
.

The degree of
\phi_{\varphi}(S)
is at most
c\cdot 2^{g}
for some constant
c\leq 4g^{4}
.

Proof.

We go into the proof of
[
vKK19
, Cor 9.2]
and we replace therein the application of
[
vKK19
, Prop 9.1]
by Proposition
8.1
. This proves the corollary. ∎

The proof of Proposition
8.1
will be given in the remaining of this section. The strategy is as follows: After applying the formal result
[
vKK19
, Lem 9.3]
which computes the set
\Phi
in terms of certain isomorphisms
A\overset{\sim}{\to}A^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}I^{-1}
, we use and generalize the arguments of
[
vKK19
, Lem 9.4]
to construct in Section
8.1
an embedding

\Phi/{\sim}\hookrightarrow\Gamma^{\times}/U.

Here
\Gamma
is an order of a number field
F\supseteq L
which comes from Lemma
8.3
and
U
denotes a certain subgroup of the units
\Gamma^{\times}
of
\Gamma
. Then in Section
8.2
we use algebraic number theory to relate the cardinality of
\Gamma^{\times}/U
to a ratio
R_{F}/(R_{F/L}R_{L})
of regulators which can be computed by using classical results from CM theory.

8.1
Equivalence classes of polarizations and quotients of unit groups

We continue our notation introduced at the beginning of Section
8
. The goal of the current section is to view equivalence classes of
I
-polarizations on
\mathcal{O}
-abelian schemes inside finite quotients of certain unit groups of orders of number fields containing
L
.

To give a precise statement, we recall that
\Phi
denotes the set of
I
-polarizations on an arbitrary
\mathcal{O}
-abelian scheme
(A,\iota)
over
S
of relative dimension
g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
where
S
is a connected Dedekind scheme with function field of characteristic zero. Notice that
D=\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
becomes an
L
-algebra via
\iota
, since
\iota(\mathcal{O})\subseteq\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and
L=\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
.

Lemma 8.3
.

The following statements hold.

(i)

Suppose that
\dim_{L}(D)=1
. Then the ring
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
identifies with an order
\Gamma
of
L
and there exists an embedding
\Phi/{\sim}\hookrightarrow\Gamma^{\times}/(\Gamma^{\times})^{2}.

(ii)

Suppose that
\dim_{L}(D)\neq 1
. Then
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
identifies with an order
\Gamma\supset\mathcal{O}
of a CM field
F
with maximal totally real subfield
L
and there exists an embedding

\Phi/{\sim}\hookrightarrow\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times}).

Here the image
N(\Gamma^{\times})
of
\Gamma^{\times}
under the field norm
N:F\to L
is a subgroup of
\Gamma^{\times}
.

The assumption that the function field of
S
has characteristic zero is crucial for (ii) which relies on the computation of the
L
-algebra
D
in Lemma
8.4
below.

In what follows in this section we write
\otimes=\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}
for simplicity. Our proof of Lemma
8.3
uses the formal result in
[
vKK19
, Lem 9.3]
which computes
\Phi/{\sim}
in terms of equivalence classes of certain isomorphisms
A\overset{\sim}{\to}A^{\vee}\otimes I^{-1}
. To state
[
vKK19
, Lem 9.3]
, we need to introduce some notation. Firstly, we observe that multiplication defines an isomorphism
\mu:I^{-1}\otimes I\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{O}
of
\mathcal{O}
-modules since
I
is invertible. Secondly, in the category of
\mathcal{O}
-modules it holds: If
X,Y,Z
are
\mathcal{O}
-modules, then mapping any morphism
\varphi:X\to\textup{Hom}(Y,Z)
to the morphism
X\otimes Y\to Z
defined by
x\otimes y\mapsto\varphi(x)(y)
gives a bijection
\textup{Hom}(X,\textup{Hom}(Y,Z))\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{Hom}(X\otimes Y,Z)
which is functorial. Therefore, on using the isomorphism
I^{-1}\otimes I\otimes A\overset{\sim}{\to}A
induced by
\mu
and tensoring with
I^{-1}
, we obtain a bijection

\tau:\textup{Hom}(I,\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,A^{\vee}))\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,I^{-1}\otimes A^{\vee}).

Further, any
\lambda\in I
defines a morphism
m_{\lambda}:I^{-1}\otimes A^{\vee}\to A^{\vee}
of
\mathcal{O}
-abelian schemes over
S
as follows: If
T
is an
S
-scheme, then
m_{\lambda}(T)
sends
j\otimes x
in
I^{-1}\otimes A^{\vee}(T)
to the element
j\lambda\otimes x
in
\mathcal{O}\otimes A^{\vee}(T)\cong A^{\vee}(T)
.
We denote by
\Psi
the set of isomorphisms
\psi:A\overset{\sim}{\to}I^{-1}\otimes A^{\vee}
of
\mathcal{O}
-abelian schemes over
S
such that for each
\lambda\in I
the morphism
m_{\lambda}\psi:A\to A^{\vee}
is symmetric and moreover a polarization if
\lambda\in I_{+}
.
Now
[
vKK19
, Lem 9.3]
says

\tau(\Phi)=\Psi.

(8.1)

On using the canonical bijection
\tau:\Phi\overset{\sim}{\to}\Psi
and the equivalence relation on
\Phi
, we obtain an equivalence relation on
\Psi
by transport of structure. We write again
\psi\sim\psi^{\prime}
if two isomorphisms
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in\Psi
are equivalent. We are now ready to prove Lemma
8.3
.

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

To show (i) we suppose that
\dim_{L}(D)=1
. Then we are in the case when
\textup{End}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)\cong L
, and this case was treated in our proof of
[
vKK19
, Lem 9.4]
. Therefore we deduce (i) by using exactly the same arguments as in the proof of
[
vKK19
, (9.2)]
.

We next show (ii). Assume that
\dim_{L}(D)\neq 1
and notice that our abelian scheme
A
over
S
satisfies all the assumptions made in Lemma
8.4
. Therefore Lemma
8.4
gives an
L
-algebra isomorphism
D\overset{\sim}{\to}F
from
D
to a CM field
F
with maximal totally real subfield
L
. Further,
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is a subring of
\textup{End}(A)
which is a finitely generated torsion-free abelian goup. Thus
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
identifies with an order of
D
. Let
\Gamma\subset F
be the image of this order under the
L
-algebra isomorphism
D\overset{\sim}{\to}F
. Then
\Gamma
is an order of
F
, and
\Gamma
contains
\mathcal{O}
since
\iota(\mathcal{O})\subseteq\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
. This proves the first part of (ii).

It remains to construct the embedding of
\Phi/{\sim}
appearing in the second part of (ii). As the equivalence relation on
\Psi
is defined by transport of structure via (
8.1
), we see that
\tau:\Phi\overset{\sim}{\to}\Psi
induces a bijection from
\Phi/{\sim}
to the set
\Psi/{\sim}
. To embed
\Psi
into
\Gamma^{\times}
, we may and do assume that there exists an isomorphism
\psi_{0}:A\overset{\sim}{\to}I^{-1}\otimes A^{\vee}
which lies in
\Psi
. We observe that
\psi\mapsto\psi_{0}^{-1}\psi
defines an embedding
\Psi\hookrightarrow\textup{Aut}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, and the isomorphism
D\overset{\sim}{\to}F
identifies the image of
\textup{Aut}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)\hookrightarrow D
with
\Gamma^{\times}
. Therefore the composition of the maps
\Psi\hookrightarrow\textup{Aut}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)\hookrightarrow D\overset{\sim}{\to}F
gives an embedding

j\colon\Psi\hookrightarrow\Gamma^{\times}.

Further, the image
N(\Gamma^{\times})
of
\Gamma^{\times}
under the field norm
N:F^{\times}\to L^{\times}
is a subgroup of
\Gamma^{\times}
, since the order
\Gamma
is contained in the maximal order
\mathcal{O}_{F}
of
F
and since
N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})
is contained in
\mathcal{O}^{\times}\subseteq\Gamma^{\times}
. We now claim that
j:\Psi\hookrightarrow\Gamma^{\times}
induces an embedding

\Psi/{\sim}\hookrightarrow\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times}).

(8.2)

To prove this claim, we suppose that
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in\Psi
and we denote by
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\in\Phi
their preimages under
\tau
respectively. Then
\psi\sim\psi^{\prime}
if and only if there exists
\sigma\in\textup{Aut}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
with
\varphi^{\prime}=\sigma^{\vee}\varphi\sigma
. Moreover, on applying the formal arguments of the proof of
[
vKK19
, Lem 9.3]
, we compute that
\varphi^{\prime}=\sigma^{\vee}\varphi\sigma
if and only if

\psi^{\prime}=(I^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{\vee})\psi\sigma
.
The latter equality is equivalent to

\psi_{0}^{-1}\psi^{\prime}=\psi_{0}^{-1}(I^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{\vee})\psi_{0}\psi_{0}^{-1}\psi\sigma,

which in turn is equivalent to

(\psi_{0}^{-1}\psi^{\prime})=\sigma^{\star}(\psi_{0}^{-1}\psi)\sigma
. Here for any
f\in\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
the involution
⋆
is defined by
f^{\star}=\psi_{0}^{-1}(I^{-1}\otimes f^{\vee})\psi_{0}.
Thus we obtain that
\psi\sim\psi^{\prime}
if and only if there exists
\sigma\in\textup{Aut}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
with

(\psi_{0}^{-1}\psi^{\prime})=\sigma^{\star}(\psi_{0}^{-1}\psi)\sigma.

To further compute this in terms of
\Gamma^{\times}
, let
\lambda\in I_{+}
and denote by
∗
the usual Rosati involution on
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
associated to the polarization
\varphi(\lambda)
of
A
, where
\varphi=\tau^{-1}(\psi_{0})
lies in
\Phi
by (
8.1
). Then our
⋆
coincides on
\Gamma
with the restriction of the Rosati involution
∗
to
D=\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A)
. Indeed, on using that any
f
in
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is a morphism of
\mathcal{O}
-abelian schemes, we compute
f^{\vee}m_{\lambda}=m_{\lambda}(I^{-1}\otimes f^{\vee})
and then we deduce that
f^{*}=\varphi(\lambda)^{-1}f^{\vee}\varphi(\lambda)=f^{\star}
inside
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A)
since
m_{\lambda}\psi_{0}=\varphi(\lambda)
by
[
vKK19
, (9.1)]
. Moreover, as
\textup{End}^{0}(A)\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{End}^{0}(A_{k})
for
k
the function field of the connected Dedekind scheme
S
, it follows for example from
[
Lan83
, p.5]
that the positive involution

∗
on
D\subseteq\textup{End}^{0}(A)
identifies via the
L
-algebra isomorphism
D\overset{\sim}{\to}F
with the non-trivial automorphism
c
of
F/L
.
Then the above computations and
{}^{*}=\,^{\star}
on
D
show that
\psi\sim\psi^{\prime}
if and only if there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\times}
with

j(\psi^{\prime})=c(\gamma)j(\psi)\gamma=N(\gamma)j(\psi),

where the last equality exploits that
\Gamma
is commutative. Hence our claim in (
8.2
) follows and thus all statements of (ii) are proven. This completes the proof of Lemma
8.3
.∎

The above proof relies on description of the
L
-algebra
D=\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A)
in the following result which further refines Lemma
5.4
(ii) in the case when the base is a connected scheme which has a point whose residue field is of characteristic zero.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
L
be a totally real number field with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, let
S
be a connected Dedekind scheme with
k(S)
of characteristic zero, and let
A
be an
\mathcal{O}
-abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
. The
L
-algebra
D=\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A)
either has dimension one or is isomorphic to a CM field with maximal totally real subfield
L
.

Proof.

We first treat the case when the function field
k=k(S)
embeds into
\mathbb{C}
. Let
A_{\mathbb{C}}
be the base change of
A_{k}
to
\mathbb{C}
via an embedding
k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and consider the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
V
given by its first homology
H_{1}(A_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Q})
. We obtain a ring morphism

\rho:D\to\textup{End}(V)
by composing
D\subseteq\textup{End}^{0}(A)\to\textup{End}^{0}(A_{\mathbb{C}})
with the morphism
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{\mathbb{C}})\to\textup{End}(V)
induced by the functor
H_{1}(\,\ ,\mathbb{Q})
. By assumption
A=(A,\iota)
is an
\mathcal{O}
-abelian scheme over
S
. Composing
\rho
with
\iota\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}:L\to D
gives
V
an
L
-vector space structure. The
L
-dimension of
V
is two, since its
\mathbb{Q}
-dimension is
2g
and
[L:\mathbb{Q}]=g
. As the image of
L\to D
lies in the center of
D
,
we then see that
\rho
induces a morphism

D\to\textup{M}_{2}(L)

(8.3)

of
L
-algebras.
This morphism is injective since
D
is a division algebra by Lemma
5.4
.
This implies that
\dim_{L}D\leq 4
. We claim that
\dim_{L}D
can not be
3
or
4
. Indeed
\dim_{L}D=4
would imply that
D\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{M}_{2}(L)
which is not possible since
\textup{M}_{2}(L)
is not a division algebra. Further, the division algebra
D
is a central simple algebra over its center
Z
. Thus
\dim_{Z}D
is a square, while (
8.3
) and
\dim_{L}D\leq 4
assure that
\dim_{L}Z
lies in
\{1,2,4\}
. Hence we deduce that
\dim_{L}D=3
is not possible, proving our claim. Suppose now that
\dim_{L}D=2
. Then the division
L
-algebra
D
is commutative.
In particular
D
is a number field of degree
2\dim(A_{\mathbb{C}})
which embeds into
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{\mathbb{C}})
. Thus
D
is totally imaginary. As
[L:\mathbb{Q}]=g
and
\dim_{L}D=2
, we then conclude that
D
is
L
-isomorphic to a CM field with maximal totally real subfield
L
.
This proves Lemma
8.4
in the case when
k
embeds into
\mathbb{C}
.

The general situation can be reduced to the above case as follows. As
k
is of characteristic zero, we may and do choose a subfield
k^{\prime}\subseteq k
with the following properties: It is finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
,
A_{k}
descends to an abelian variety
A_{k^{\prime}}
over
k^{\prime}
and there is a ring morphism
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{k})\to\textup{End}^{0}(A_{\mathbb{C}})
where now
A_{\mathbb{C}}
is the base change of
A_{k^{\prime}}
to
\mathbb{C}
via an embedding
k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. Thus the arguments above (
8.3
) provide again a morphism
D\to\textup{M}_{2}(L)
of
L
-algebras and then the arguments below (
8.3
) imply Lemma
8.4
in general.
∎

In the next sections we bound the cardinality of
\Gamma^{\times}/(\Gamma^{\times})^{2}
and
\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})
.

8.2
Quotients of unit groups

In this section we prove two lemmas controlling the cardinality of quotient groups of the form
\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})
. For this purpose we use algebraic number theory in order to relate and compute various quotients of unit groups in extensions of number fields.

We introduce some notation. Let
F
be a number field and let
L\subseteq F
be a subfield. We denote by
N:F^{\times}\to L^{\times}
the group morphism given by the field norm, we write
R_{F/L}
for the relative regulator (
[
CF93
,
\mathsection
3]
) and we put
R_{F}=R_{F/\mathbb{Q}}
. Further for any ring
R
we denote by
\mu(R)
the group of roots of unity in
R
and we write
w_{R}=|\mu(R)|
. Our first goal is to prove the following lemma on quotients of unit groups in general number fields.

Lemma 8.5
.

The following statements hold.

(i)

If
\Gamma
is an order of
F
with
\mathcal{O}_{L}\subseteq\Gamma
, then
N(\Gamma^{\times})
is a subgroup of
\Gamma^{\times}
and

|\ker(N)\cap\Gamma^{\times}|\cdot|\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})|\leq|\ker(N)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}|\cdot|\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})|.

(ii)

The cardinality of the quotient
\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})
satisfies

|\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})|\leq w_{F}\cdot|\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/\mu(F)\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}|\cdot\tfrac{R_{F}}{R_{F/L}R_{L}}.

Proof.

To show (i) we assume that the order
\Gamma
contains
\mathcal{O}_{L}
. Any order of
F
is contained in the maximal order
\mathcal{O}_{F}
of
F
,
and
N
sends
\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
to
\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}
since the norm takes the form
x\mapsto\prod\sigma(x)
with the product taken over all
\sigma\in\textup{Hom}_{L}(F,\bar{F})
. Thus our assumption that
\Gamma
contains
\mathcal{O}_{L}
implies that the restriction of
N
to
\Gamma^{\times}
is a group morphism
N:\Gamma^{\times}\to\Gamma^{\times}
. In particular
N(\Gamma^{\times})
is a subgroup of
\Gamma^{\times}
and we obtain a diagram

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ker(N)\cap\Gamma^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ker(N)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

where
\Gamma^{\times}\to\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
is the inclusion. This is a commutative diagram of abelian groups, with exact rows and with the first three vertical morphisms injective. Therefore an application of the snake lemma shows that the kernel of
\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})\to\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})
has cardinality at most
|\ker(N)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}|/|\ker(N)\cap\Gamma^{\times}|
when
\ker(N)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
is finite. This implies the inequality in (i), which also holds when
\ker(N)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
is infinite since
\infty\leq\infty
.

We next prove (ii). Costa–Friedman observed that
|\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}/\mu(L)N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})|
can be written as the quotient of regulators
R_{F}/(R_{F/L}R_{L})
, see
[
CF93
, (3.1)]
. Then, on using that the index of subgroups is multiplicative and that
N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})
is a subgroup of
\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}
, we compute

|\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})|=|\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}|\cdot|\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}/N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})|=|\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/\mu(F)\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}|\cdot n\cdot\tfrac{R_{F}}{R_{F/L}R_{L}}\cdot m.

Here
n=|\mu(F)\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}|
and
m=|\mu(L)N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})/N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})|
. The indices
n
and
m
are both finite, and they satisfy
n\leq w_{F}/w_{L}
and
m\leq w_{L}
. Therefore the displayed formula implies the upper bound for
|\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/N(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})|
stated in (ii). This completes the proof. ∎

We now apply the above lemma in the case of CM fields. In this case CM theory allows to compute the quantities appearing in Lemma
8.5
leading to the following result.

Lemma 8.6
.

Suppose that
F
is a CM field with maximal totally real subfield
L
and write
g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
. Then any order
\Gamma
of
F
with
\mathcal{O}_{L}\subseteq\Gamma
satisfies

|\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})|\leq\tfrac{w_{F}^{2}}{w_{\Gamma}}\cdot 2^{g-1}.

Proof.

We assume that
F
,
L
and
\Gamma
are as in the statement. Then the claimed upper bound for
|\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})|
is in fact contained in the statements of Lemma
8.5
. To see this we now compute the quantities appearing in Lemma
8.5
. We first show that

\ker(N)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}=\mu(F)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mu(\Gamma)=\ker(N)\cap\Gamma^{\times}.

For this purpose we may and do assume that the CM field
F\subset\mathbb{C}
. Then the non-trivial automorphism of the quadratic field extension
F/L
