is given by complex conjugation
\bar{}\,
and we obtain that
\ker(N)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}=\{|x|=1\}
where we write
\{|x|=1\}
for
\{x\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times};\ x\bar{x}=1\}.
Further
\bar{}\,
commutes with any embedding
F\to\mathbb{C}
since
F
is a CM field. Thus, if
x\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
satisfies
x\bar{x}=1
, then for each embedding
\sigma:F\to\mathbb{C}
the complex absolute value of
\sigma(x)
is 1 and hence
x
has to be a root of unity. This shows that
\{|x|=1\}
is contained in
\mu(F)
which in turn is contained in
\{|x|=1\}
. Thus we obtain that
\ker(N)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}=\{|x|=1\}=\mu(F)
, which implies that
\ker(N)\cap\Gamma^{\times}=\mu(\Gamma)
since
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Next, we compute that

|\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/\mu(F)\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}|\cdot\tfrac{R_{F}}{R_{F/L}R_{L}}=2^{g-1}.

Indeed this is a consequence of
R_{F/L}=1
and the standard formula for the regulator ratio
R_{F}/R_{L}
which can be found for example in
[
Was82
, Prop 4.16]
. Here
R_{F/L}=1
follows directly from the definition of the relative regulator (
[
CF93
,
\mathsection
3]
), since
\{|x|=1\}=\mu(F)
assures that the relative units of
F/L
have (free) rank zero. Now, we insert the displayed computations into the inequalities of Lemma
8.5
and then we deduce Lemma
8.6
.∎

8.3
Proof of Proposition
8.1

In this section we prove Proposition
8.1
by combining the results collected in previous sections. We continue the notation introduced at the beginning of Section
8
.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

Recall that the set
\Phi
in Proposition
8.1
denotes the set of
I
-polarizations on an arbitrary
\mathcal{O}
-abelian scheme
(A,\iota)
over
S
of relative dimension
g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
, where
S
is a connected Dedekind scheme with function field of characteristic zero. Hence we can apply Lemma
8.3
and we consider again the
L
-algebra
D=\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A)
.

We first assume that
\dim_{L}(D)=1
. Then Lemma
8.3
(i) gives an order
\Gamma
of
L
with
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)\cong\Gamma
and an embedding
\Phi/{\sim}\hookrightarrow\Gamma^{\times}/(\Gamma^{\times})^{2}
. In particular it holds

|\Phi/{\sim}|\leq|\Gamma^{\times}/(\Gamma^{\times})^{2}|.

Further, Dirichlet’s unit theorem
[
Neu99
, p.81]
provides that the free part of
\Gamma^{\times}
has rank
g-1
, since
L
is totally real. Thus
|\Gamma^{\times}/(\Gamma^{\times})^{2}|
is at most
w_{L}\cdot 2^{g-1}
, where we recall that
w_{R}
denotes the cardinality of the group
\mu(R)
of roots of unity in a ring
R
. Notice that
\mu(L)=\{1,-1\}
are the only roots of unity in the totally real field
L
, the ring
D
is isomorphic to
L
since
\dim_{L}(D)=1
, and the ring
\Gamma\cong\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
contains
\{1,-1\}
. Hence we obtain that
w=w_{L}=w_{\Gamma}=w^{\prime}
and it follows that
|\Gamma^{\times}/(\Gamma^{\times})^{2}|\leq\tfrac{w^{2}}{w^{\prime}}\cdot 2^{g-1}
. Therefore the displayed inequality proves Proposition
8.1
in the case when
\dim_{L}(D)=1
.

We now assume that
\dim_{L}(D)\neq 1
. Then an application of Lemma
8.3
(ii) gives the following: The ring
\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
identifies with an order
\Gamma\supset\mathcal{O}
of a CM field
F
with maximal totally real subfield
L
and there exists an embedding
\Phi/{\sim}\hookrightarrow\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})
where we recall that
N:F^{\times}\to L^{\times}
denotes the field norm. It follows that

|\Phi/{\sim}|\leq|\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})|.

Next, we observe that the field extension
F/L
and the order
\Gamma\supset\mathcal{O}
of
F
satisfy all assumptions of Lemma
8.6
. Hence this lemma provides that
|\Gamma^{\times}/N(\Gamma^{\times})|\leq\tfrac{w_{F}^{2}}{w_{\Gamma}}\cdot 2^{g-1}
. Moreover, in this bound we can replace
w_{\Gamma}
by
w^{\prime}
and
w_{F}
by
w
, since
\Gamma\cong\textup{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and thus
F=\Gamma\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}\cong D
. Therefore the displayed inequality proves Proposition
8.1
in the case when
\dim_{L}(D)\neq 1
. This completes the proof of Proposition
8.1
. ∎

The above proof shows that the number
w
appearing in Proposition
8.1
can be bounded only in terms of the relative dimension
g
of
A
over
S
. For example, one obtains an explicit bound and an inexplicit but asymptotically sharper bound for each real
\epsilon>0
,

w\leq 4g^{2}\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad w\ll_{\epsilon}g^{1+\epsilon},

(8.4)

by controlling
w_{F}
as follows: Suppose that
\zeta\in F
generates the group of roots of unity in
F
and write
n=w_{F}
. Then we deduce (
8.4
) by combining
\varphi(n)=[\mathbb{Q}(\zeta):\mathbb{Q}]\leq 2g

with the elementary bound
n^{1/2}\leq\varphi(n)
which holds when
n>6
and the asymptotically sharper bound
n^{1-\epsilon}\ll_{\epsilon}\varphi(n)
which follows from
[
RS62
, Thm 15]
for
\varphi
the Euler totient function.

Section 9
Endomorphism structures

Let
\Gamma
be an order of an arbitrary number field
L
of degree
g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
, let
S
be a connected Dedekind scheme whose function field is a number field
K
, and let
A
be an abelian scheme over
S
of relative dimension
g
. The goal of this section is to show the following result, which was proven in
[
vKK19
,
\mathsection
10]
when
K=\mathbb{Q}
.

Theorem 9.1
.

Suppose that
A
has no CM or that
L
is totally real.

(i)

Then the number
n_{A}
of isomorphism classes of
\Gamma
-module structures on
A
satisfies

n_{A}\leq c(h(A)|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma|)^{e}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}

where
h(A)=d\max(h_{F}(A),1)
,
e=(2g)^{9}
and
c=(3g)^{(3g)^{11}}
.

(ii)

Suppose that
S
is an open subscheme of
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
, and define
k=3^{3^{a}}
for
a=7^{4g^{2}}d^{2}
. If
A
is of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies, then

n_{A}\leq k|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma|^{e}\textnormal{rad}(N_{S}D_{K})^{24ge}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}.

Here the quantities
d=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
,
D_{K}
and
N_{S}=\prod_{v\in S^{c}}N_{v}
are as before, while
h_{F}
denotes the stable Faltings height (
\mathsection
3.4
). Further we write
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{L}
and
\Delta=D_{L}
.

In the case when
A
has no CM, we obtain in (
9.1
) and (
9.2
) more precise bounds which show that Theorem
9.1
holds in this case with
\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}
replaced by the class number of
L
. If
A
has CM then a slightly sharper version of (i) can be found in (
9.4
).

Notice that (i) and Theorem
7.1
directly imply the bound in (ii) which is uniform in
K
,
g
and
S
for all abelian schemes
A
of product
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies. This uniformity is crucial for the proof of Theorem
4.1
(ii) given in Section
10
.

9.1
Proof of Theorem
9.1

We continue our notation. To prove Theorem
9.1
we use and generalize the arguments of
[
vKK19
,
\mathsection
10]
.
As already mentioned, (ii) follows directly from (i) and Theorem
7.1
.

We now prove (i). To bound the number
n_{A}
of isomorphism classes of
\Gamma
-module structures on
A
, we assume that there exists a ring morphism
\iota:\Gamma\to\textup{End}(A)
.
Hence
A
is of
{\textup{GL}}_{2}
-type. In steps 1-4 below we prove Theorem
9.1
under the assumption that
A
has no CM and we distinguish two subcases (a) and (b). In step 5 we deal with the CM case.

1.

Suppose that
A
has no CM. As
A
is of
{\textup{GL}}_{2}
-type, Lemma
7.5
(i) and Yu
[
Yu11
, Thm 1.3]
then imply that
A
is isogenous to a power
A^{\prime}=B^{n}
of a simple abelian scheme
B
over
S
such that
\textup{End}(B)
identifies with a maximal order of the division algebra
D=\textup{End}^{0}(B)
. Furthermore, Lemma
7.5
(iii) gives that the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
D
has the following properties:

(a)

Either
D
identifies with a subfield
Z
of
L
of relative degree
[L:Z]=n
,

(b)

or
D
is a non-split quaternion algebra and the center
Z(D)
of
D
identifies with a subfield
Z
of
L
of relative degree
[L:Z]=2n
.

Let
m
be the degree of an isogeny
A\to A^{\prime}
of minimal degree, that is any isogeny
A\to A^{\prime}
has degree at least
m
.
Write
\mathfrak{f}
for the conductor ideal of
\Gamma
.
On using precisely the same arguments as in steps 2-4 of the proof of
[
vKK19
, Thm 10.1]
, we see in case (a) that

n_{A}\leq m^{(2g+1)g}N(\mathfrak{f})^{g+1}gh,\quad h=|\textup{Pic}(\mathcal{O}_{L})|.

We have
|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma|\in\mathfrak{f}
and hence
N(\mathfrak{f})\leq|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma|^{g}
. Then the displayed bound for
n_{A}
and the (minimal isogeny degree) estimate
m^{2}\leq\kappa(A_{K})
coming from (
7.13
) lead to

n_{A}\leq(7g)^{97g^{6}}|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma|^{g(g+1)}(h(A)d)^{12g^{4}}h.

(9.1)

Thus the bound for
h
in terms of
\Delta
provided by (
9.5
) proves statement (i) in case (a). In the following steps 2-4 of the proof we assume that we are in case (b).

2.

We are going to compare
n_{A}
with the number of isomorphism classes
n_{A^{\prime}}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
-module structures on
A^{\prime}
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[m\Gamma]
denotes the subring of
\Gamma
generated by
m\Gamma
.
We write
R^{\prime}=\textup{End}(A^{\prime})
. On using precisely the same arguments as in step 2 of the proof of
[
vKK19
, Thm 10.1]
, we see that
n_{A}\leq|R^{\prime}/mR^{\prime}|n_{A^{\prime}}
. To determine
|R^{\prime}/mR^{\prime}|
, we first recall that
D=\textup{End}^{0}(B)
and
A^{\prime}=B^{n}
. Hence the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras
\textup{End}^{0}(A^{\prime})
and
\textup{M}_{n}(D)
are isomorphic. Thus the
\mathbb{Q}
-dimension of
\textup{End}^{0}(A^{\prime})
equals
n^{2}[D:\mathbb{Q}]=2ng
, since we are in case (b) and since the order
R^{\prime}=\textup{End}(A^{\prime})
of
\textup{End}^{0}(A^{\prime})
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of rank
2ng
. Therefore
|R^{\prime}/mR^{\prime}|
equals
m^{2ng}
. We conclude that
n_{A}\leq m^{2ng}n_{A^{\prime}}
.

3.

We now bound
n_{A^{\prime}}
by applying the main result of
\mathsection
9.2
below. As we are in the case (b), the center
Z(D)
of
D=\textup{End}^{0}(B)
identifies with a subfield
Z
of
L
with
[L:Z]=2n
. The ring
\textup{End}(B)
identifies with a maximal order
\mathcal{O}_{D}
of
D
and thus
\textup{End}(A^{\prime})=\textup{End}(B^{n})
is isomorphic to
\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
. Then Proposition
9.2
implies

n_{A^{\prime}}\leq\tfrac{g}{2n}\delta^{16n^{3}g^{3}}N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))^{16n^{5}g^{2}}h.

Here
\delta
is the index
|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma^{\prime}|
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{O}
and
\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D)
denotes the finite Brauer discriminant (see
\mathsection
9.2
) of the quaternion algebra
D
. In fact we applied here Proposition
9.2
with the order
\rho(\Gamma^{\prime})
of
\rho(L)
where
\rho:L\to\textup{M}_{n}(D)
is the composition of
\iota_{\mathbb{Q}}:L\to\textup{End}^{0}(A)
with
\textup{End}^{0}(A)\cong\textup{End}^{0}(A^{\prime})\cong\textup{M}_{n}(D)
; notice that
\rho(L)
automatically contains
Z(D)
by the proof of Lemma
9.3
below and
[\rho(L):Z(D)]=2n
since
Z(D)\cong Z
.

4.

In this step we bound the index
\delta
and the discriminant
N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))
and then we complete the proof of (i) in the case when
A
has no CM. We first relate
N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))
to a covolume of
\textup{End}(B_{K})
. Write
Z
for
Z(D)
.
It holds that
\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D)^{2}=d(\mathcal{O}_{D}/\mathcal{O}_{Z})
by (
9.9
), and
N(d(\mathcal{O}_{D}/\mathcal{O}_{Z}))
divides
d(\mathcal{O}_{D}/\mathbb{Z})
; note
\mathcal{O}_{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}_{D}
(As
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
lies in the center of
D
, we see that
\mathcal{O}_{D}\mathcal{O}_{Z}
is an order of
D
. Hence the maximal order
\mathcal{O}_{D}
of
D
contains the commutative ring
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
since maximal orders of
D
are unique up to conjugation.). Here
d(\cdot)
denotes the discriminant ideal defined in
[
Rei75
, p. 218]
using the reduced trace
tr
. Notice that
2\textnormal{tr}_{D/\mathbb{Q}}
equals the trace
T_{D/\mathbb{Q}}
of the representation induced by left multiplication, see
[
Rei75
, (9.14) and (9.32)]
. As
B_{K}
is simple and
\textup{End}(B_{K})
has
\mathbb{Z}
-rank
2g/n
, we find that
nT_{D/\mathbb{Q}}
coincides on
\mathcal{O}_{D}=\textup{End}(B)\cong\textup{End}(B_{K})
with the trace
Tr
in
[
GR14a
, Def 2.1]
. In the proof of
[
GR14a
, Prop 2.9]
, Gaudron–Rémond showed that
|\textnormal{det}(\textnormal{Tr}(\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}))|=\textnormal{vol}(\textup{End}(B_{K}))^{2}
where
\textnormal{vol}(\cdot)
is the covolume defined in
[
GR23
, p. 2067]
using the Rosati metric and
(\varphi_{i})
is a basis of the free
\mathbb{Z}
-module
\textup{End}(B_{K})
. It follows that

N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))\leq\textnormal{vol}(\textup{End}(B_{K}))\leq\kappa(B_{K})^{1/4}.

Here
\kappa(B_{K})
is defined in (
7.13
) and the upper bound for
\textnormal{vol}(\textup{End}(B_{K}))
is due to Gaudron–Rémond
[
GR23
, Thm 1.9 (3)]
. To estimate the index
\delta
of
\Gamma^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[m\Gamma]
in
\mathcal{O}
, we observe that
m\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
and that
\Gamma/m\Gamma\cong(\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z})^{g}
. Hence we obtain

\delta\leq|\mathcal{O}/m\Gamma|=|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma|m^{g}.

Now we combine the results of steps 2 and 3 with the displayed bounds for
N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))
and
\delta
and with the estimate
m^{2}\leq\kappa(A_{K})
provided by (
7.13
). Then, after bounding
h_{F}(B)
in terms of
h(A)
and
g
via (
7.10
), (
7.13
) and Rémond’s
[
Rém19
, Cor 1.2]
, we deduce

n_{A}\leq(3g)^{(2g)^{11}}|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma|^{16g^{6}}(h(A)d)^{88g^{9}}h.

(9.2)

Thus the bound for
h
in (
9.5
) proves (i) in the case when
A
has no CM. We now include some details of our computations described above.
To bound
h_{F}(B)
in terms of
h(A)
,
g
and
d
, we used the inequality
h_{F}(B)\leq h_{F}(A)+\tfrac{g}{2}\log(2\pi^{2})+h_{F}(B)-h_{F}(C)
which follows from (
7.10
) and
[
Rém19
, Cor 1.2]
, and then we bounded
h_{F}(B)-h_{F}(C)
via (
7.13
) where
C
is an abelian subvariety of
A_{K}
which is isogenous to the factor
B_{K}
of
A_{K}
.
This leads to

h_{F}(B)\leq\max(2h_{F}(A)+a,b,d)

(9.3)

for
a=g\log(2\pi^{2})+32g_{0}^{4}\log(7g_{0})+4g_{0}^{2}\log d
,
b=\exp(4g_{0}^{2})
and
g_{0}=\dim B_{K}=g/n
. Here we exploited that any real
x\geq b
satisfies
4g_{0}^{2}\log x\leq x
and we distinguished whether
h_{F}(B)\geq b
or not. Further, we bounded the right hand side of (
9.3
) by
2abh(A)
.

5.

From now on we assume that
A
has CM and
L
is totally real. Then
A_{K}
is isotypic since the field
L
embeds into
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{K})
and
[L:\mathbb{Q}]=\dim(A_{K})
. Indeed, otherwise Wu
[
Wu20
, Prop 1.5]
would imply that the field
L
embeds into
\textup{End}^{0}(C)
for some factor
C=A_{1}^{r_{1}}
of
A_{K}
with
2\dim(C)=\dim(A_{K})=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
and this is not possible since
L
is not totally imaginary (
[
Lan83
, Thm 3.1 (ii)]
). It follows that
A
is isogenous to a power
A^{\prime}=B^{n}
of a simple abelian scheme
B
over
S
such that
\textup{End}(B)
identifies with the maximal order
\mathcal{O}_{F}
of a CM field
F
of degree
[F:\mathbb{Q}]=2g/n
.
In particular the ring
R^{\prime}=\textup{End}(A^{\prime})
is isomorphic to
\textnormal{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
which is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of rank
2ng
. Then, as in step 2,
we have
n_{A}\leq m^{2ng}n_{A^{\prime}}
where
n_{A^{\prime}}
and
m
are defined as before.
Proposition
9.9
implies that

n_{A^{\prime}}\leq(2g)^{15g^{3}}(\delta D_{F})^{5g^{3}}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}.

Here
\delta=|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma^{\prime}|
where the order
\Gamma^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[m\Gamma]
of
L
is defined as in step 2. To estimate
D_{F}
we use that the abelian variety
B_{K}
is simple and that the free
\mathbb{Z}
-module
\textup{End}(B_{K})
has rank
2g/n
. Thus, as in step 4, we find that
nT_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=\textnormal{Tr}
on
\mathcal{O}_{F}\cong\textup{End}(B_{K})
and

D_{F}\leq\textnormal{vol}(\textup{End}(B_{K}))^{2}\leq\kappa(B_{K})^{1/2}.

In step 4 we proved that
\delta\leq|\mathcal{O}/\Gamma|m^{g}
, and (
7.13
) shows that the minimal isogeny degree
m
satisfies
m^{2}\leq\kappa(A_{K})
. Further, in (
9.3
) we estimated
h_{F}(B)
in terms of
h_{F}(A)
,
g
and
d
. Now we combine everything and then we see that (i) also holds in the remaining case when
A
has CM and
L
is totally real. Here one can use for example the inequalities

D_{F}\leq(3g)^{9g^{3}}d(h(A)d)^{2g^{2}}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad n_{A}\leq(3g)^{(3g)^{8}}\delta_{\Gamma}^{5g^{3}}(h(A)d)^{35g^{6}}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}

(9.4)

as intermediate steps. This completes the proof of Theorem
9.1
.

In the above proof we used the following estimate for the class number: Any number field
K
of degree
d
and class number
h_{K}=|\textup{Pic}(\mathcal{O}_{K})|
satisfies

h_{K}\leq a_{d}D_{K}^{1/2}\bigl(\max(1,\log(D_{K})\bigl)^{d-1},\quad a_{d}=\tfrac{d^{d-1}}{(d-1)!}.

(9.5)

For example, this estimate follows directly from Lenstra’s bound
[
Len92
, Thm 6.5]
.

9.2
The quaternion case

In this section we prove the bound for the number of conjugation classes of ring morphisms used in the proof of Theorem
9.1
in the quaternion case (b).

Let
D
be a non-split quaternion algebra with center
Z
, let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and let
L
be a number field contained in
\textup{M}_{n}(D)
with
Z\subseteq L
and
[L:Z]=2n
. Let
\Gamma
be an order of
L
and let
\mathcal{O}_{D}
be a maximal order of
D
.
Write
g
for the degree of
L/\mathbb{Q}
, let
\delta
be the index of
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{L}
and denote by
h_{L}
the class number of
L
. The finite Brauer discriminant
\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D)
is the product of all finite
Z
-places
\mathfrak{p}
where
D_{\mathfrak{p}}
is ramified.

Proposition 9.2
.

We have

|\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})|\leq\tfrac{g}{2n}\delta^{16n^{3}g^{3}}N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))^{16n^{5}g^{2}}h_{L}.

Here
{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})=\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})^{\times}
acts on the set of ring morphisms
\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
via conjugation, that is
\tau\cdot\rho=(x\mapsto\tau\rho(x)\tau^{-1})
for
\tau\in{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
and
\rho\in\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
. In Proposition
9.2
, one can replace
\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D)^{2}
by the usual discriminant
d(\mathcal{O}_{D}/\mathcal{O}_{Z})
defined for example in
[
Rei75
, p.218]
; see (
9.9
) and notice that
\mathcal{O}_{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}_{D}
.

Strategy of proof.

We first decompose

\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))

into sets of
\varphi
-compatible morphisms
\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
where
\varphi
ranges over
\textup{Hom}(Z,L)
.
Let
A=\textup{M}_{n}(D)\otimes_{\varphi}L
.
We embed each piece
\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
(as in
[
vKK19
,
\mathsection
10.4]
) into the set
X_{0}/{\sim}
of isomorphism classes of
\Gamma_{A}
-module structures on
\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
for
\Gamma_{A}=\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})\otimes_{\varphi^{-1}(\Gamma)}\Gamma
:

\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})\overset{\psi_{1}}{\hookrightarrow}X_{0}/{\sim}.

The Jordan–Zassenhaus theorem (JZ) gives that
X_{0}/{\sim}
is finite. However, it seems difficult to make the proof (see
[
Rei75
,
\mathsection
26]
) of (JZ) effective for
X_{0}/{\sim}
. Hence we use a different approach:
We first show that
A\cong\textup{M}_{2n}(L)
is split and hence acts on
L^{2n}
.
We consider a certain set
X
(resp.
Y
) of

\Gamma_{A}
-sublattices (resp.
\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
-sublattices)
\Lambda
of
L^{2n}
and use
X,Y
to construct an explicit finite map to the class group
\mathcal{I}/L^{\times}
of
L
,

X_{0}/{\sim}\overset{\psi_{2}}{\hookrightarrow}X/L^{\times}\overset{\beta}{\to}Y/L^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{I}/L^{\times}.

If
M\in X_{0}
then
\psi_{2}(M)=g(M)\subset L^{2n}
for some
A
-isomorphism
g:M\otimes\mathbb{Q}\overset{\sim}{\to}L^{2n}
. Such a
g
always exists by a dimension argument using that
A
is a simple ring with simple module
L^{2n}
, see the construction of
\psi=\psi_{2}\psi_{1}
in (
9.7
). The map
\beta:X/L^{\times}\to Y/L^{\times}
is induced by
\Lambda\mapsto\textup{M}_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\Lambda
and its degree can be controlled (Lemma
9.6
). Finally the bijection
Y/L^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{I}/L^{\times}
is given by the first projection.

9.2.1
Proof of Proposition
9.2

We continue our notation. Let
\varphi\colon Z\to L
be a morphism. Write
\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
for the set of ring morphisms
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
such that
\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\textup{M}_{n}(D)
is a
Z
-algebra morphism. Here
L
and
\textup{M}_{n}(D)
are
Z
-algebras via
\varphi
and
Z\subset D\subseteq\textup{M}_{n}(D)
respectively.

The following decomposition is an analogue of
[
vKK19
, Lem 10.4]
:

Lemma 9.3
.

We have

\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))=\bigcup_{\varphi\in\textup{Hom}(Z,L)}\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})).

Proof.

Let
\rho\in\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
. We claim that
\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}(L)\subset\textup{M}_{n}(D)
contains
Z
. Define
K=Z\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}(L)\subset\textup{M}_{n}(D)
. Then
K
is a commutative finite étale
Z
-algebra. Thus
\dim_{Z}(K)\leq 2n
and

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(K)\leq 2n[Z:\mathbb{Q}]=g
. As
\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}(L)\subseteq K
is also of dimension
g
over
\mathbb{Q}
, we obtain
\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}(L)=K
, and hence
Z\subseteq\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}(L)
as claimed. Thus
\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}
induces some isomorphism of a subfield
Z^{\prime}\subset L
to
Z\subset\textup{M}_{n}(D)
. Define
\varphi=(\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Z^{\prime}})^{-1}
. Then
\rho\in\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
.
∎

As
|\textup{Hom}(Z,L)|\leq\frac{g}{2n}
, the proof of Proposition
9.2
is reduced to bounding the set

\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})

(9.6)

for a fixed morphism
\varphi\colon Z\to L
, where
{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
acts by conjugation. In the rest of the argument we assume that
\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
is nonempty. Define

\Gamma_{Z}=\varphi^{-1}(\Gamma),\quad A=\textup{M}_{n}(D)\otimes_{Z,\varphi}L\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Gamma_{A}=\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})\otimes_{\Gamma_{Z},\varphi}\Gamma

where
\Gamma_{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}_{D}
.
We write
\otimes_{\varphi}
as shorthand for
\otimes_{\Gamma_{Z},\varphi}
and
\otimes_{Z,\varphi}
. An application of
[
Jac89
, Thm 4.12]
with
n=2
and
r=n
gives isomorphisms
\phi\colon D\otimes_{\varphi}L\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{M}_{2}(L)
and
\textup{M}_{n}(D)\otimes_{\varphi}L\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{M}_{2n}(L)
of
L
-algebras. Then
\phi(\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{\varphi}\Gamma)\subset\textup{M}_{2}(L)
is an order of
\textup{M}_{2}(L)
.
We replace
\phi
by a suitable conjugate so that
\phi(\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{\varphi}\Gamma)\subseteq\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
. Write
\phi^{\prime}\colon A=\textup{M}_{n}(D)\otimes_{\varphi}L\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{M}_{2n}(L)
for the isomorphism induced by
\phi
.

The unique (up to isomorphism) simple
A
-module
S
is given by
L^{2n}
on which
A
acts by usual matrix multiplication (using
\phi^{\prime}
). Write
X
for the set of
\Gamma_{A}
-submodules
\Lambda\subset S
such that
\mathbb{Q}\Lambda=S
and
\Lambda
is of finite type as
\mathbb{Z}
-module.

We are now going to define a map

\psi\colon\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))\to X/L^{\times}.

(9.7)

Let
\rho\in\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
. Define the
\Gamma_{A}
-module
M_{\rho}=\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
given by
\Gamma_{A}\times M_{\rho}\to M_{\rho}
,
(d\otimes\gamma,m)\mapsto dm\rho(\gamma)
where on the right hand side
dm\rho(\gamma)
is the product in
\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
.We claim that
M_{\rho}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\cong S
as
A
-modules: As
A
is a simple ring, we have
M_{\rho}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\cong S^{r}
for some integer
r\geq 0
. We compute the dimensions

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(M_{\rho}\otimes\mathbb{Q})=4n^{2}[Z:\mathbb{Q}]=2ng=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(S),

and thus
r=1
. This proves the claim. Let
g\colon M_{\rho}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\overset{\sim}{\to}S
be an
A
-isomorphism. We define
\psi(\rho)=g(M_{\rho})\in X/L^{\times}.
If we had chosen a different
A
-isomorphism
g^{\prime}\colon M_{\rho}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\overset{\sim}{\to}S
, then
g^{\prime}(M_{\rho})=hg(M_{\rho})
for
h=g^{\prime}g^{-1}\in\textup{End}_{A}(S)^{\times}=L^{\times}
. In particular the construction does not depend on the choice of
g
and
\psi
is well-defined. This completes the definition of
\psi
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\tau\in{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
and
\rho\in\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
. Then
\psi(\tau\rho\tau^{-1})=\psi(\rho)
. In particular we obtain a map
\psi\colon\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})\to X/L^{\times}
.

Proof.

The map
k\colon M_{\rho}\to M_{\tau\rho\tau^{-1}}
defined by
m\mapsto m\tau^{-1}
is an
\Gamma_{A}
-isomorphism. Choose an isomorphism
g\colon M_{\rho}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\overset{\sim}{\to}S
. Let
g^{\prime}\colon M_{\tau\rho\tau^{-1}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\overset{\sim}{\to}S
be the composition

gk^{-1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. By the definition of
\psi
we have
\psi(\tau\rho\tau^{-1})=g^{\prime}(M_{\tau\rho\tau^{-1}})=gk^{-1}_{\mathbb{Q}}(M_{\tau\rho\tau^{-1}})=g(M_{\rho})=\psi(\rho)
inside
X/L^{\times}
, where we used that
k_{\mathbb{Q}}(M_{\rho})=M_{\tau\rho\tau^{-1}}
.
∎

Lemma 9.5
.

The map
\psi\colon\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})\to X/L^{\times}
is injective.

Proof.

Let
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))
and assume that
\psi(\rho)=\psi(\rho^{\prime})
. Then there exist
A
-isomorphisms
g\colon M_{\rho}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\overset{\sim}{\to}S
and
g^{\prime}\colon M_{\rho^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\overset{\sim}{\to}S
, such that
g(M_{\rho})=\lambda g^{\prime}(M_{\rho^{\prime}})\subset S
for some
\lambda\in L^{\times}
. After replacing
g^{\prime}
by
\lambda g^{\prime}
, we obtain
g(M_{\rho})=g^{\prime}(M_{\rho^{\prime}})\subset S
. Define the
\Gamma_{A}
-isomorphism
h=(g^{\prime,-1}g)\colon M_{\rho}\overset{\sim}{\to}M_{\rho^{\prime}}
. Then
h
is a morphism
\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})\to\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
such that for all
m\in\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
and all
d\otimes\gamma\in\Gamma_{A}
we have
h((d\otimes\gamma)m)=(d\otimes\gamma)h(m)
, so

h(dm\rho(\gamma))=dh(m)\rho^{\prime}(\gamma).

(9.8)

Taking
\gamma=1
, we get
h(dm)=dh(m)
and thus
h(d)=dh(1)
. Define
u=h(1)
, then
h(m)=mh(1)=mu
for all
m\in\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
. In particular (
9.8
) becomes
dm\rho(\gamma)u=dmu\rho^{\prime}(\gamma)
. Taking
d=m=1
we obtain
\rho(\gamma)u=u\rho^{\prime}(\gamma)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and hence
\rho^{\prime}=u^{-1}\rho u
(as
h
is invertible,
u=h(1)
is invertible as well). This completes the proof.
∎

Write
Y
for the set of
\textup{M}_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
-submodules
\Lambda
of
S=L^{2n}
such that
\Lambda
is of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
and such that
\mathbb{Q}\Lambda=S
. Consider the mapping
\beta\colon X\to Y
,
\Lambda\mapsto\textup{M}_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\Lambda
. Recall that
\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D)
is the finite Brauer discriminant and that
\delta=|\mathcal{O}_{L}/\Gamma|
.

Lemma 9.6
.

We have
\textnormal{deg}(\beta)\leq\delta^{16n^{3}g^{3}}N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))^{16n^{5}g^{2}}
.

Proof.

By definition
\Gamma_{A}=\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})\otimes_{\varphi}\Gamma
and
\phi^{\prime}\colon A=\textup{M}_{n}(D)\otimes_{\varphi}L\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{M}_{2n}(L)
is induced by the
L
-algebra isomorphism
\phi:D\otimes_{\varphi}L\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{M}_{2}(L)
with
\phi(\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{\varphi}\Gamma)\subseteq\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
. Define
R=\phi^{\prime}(\Gamma_{A})\subseteq\textup{M}_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
and
m=|\textup{M}_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{L})/R|=|\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})/\phi(\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{\varphi}\Gamma)|^{n^{2}}
. We have
m\textup{M}_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\subseteq R
and thus
\textup{M}_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\subseteq m^{-1}R
. If
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in X
satisfy
\beta(\Lambda)=\beta(\Lambda^{\prime})
, then
\Lambda\subseteq\textup{M}_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{L})\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq m^{-1}R\Lambda^{\prime}=m^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}
and, in the same way,
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq m^{-1}\Lambda
. Hence

\beta^{-1}(\beta(\Lambda))\subseteq\{\Lambda^{\prime}\in X\ |\ m\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq m^{-1}\Lambda\}\hookrightarrow\{\textnormal{subgroups of $\Lambda/m^{2}\Lambda$}\}

for all
\Lambda\in X
, where the injection is induced by
\Lambda^{\prime}\mapsto m\Lambda^{\prime}
and the subgroups in the image are generated by
\textnormal{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Lambda^{\prime})=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(S)=2ng
elements. Thus
\deg(\beta)
is at most
\sup_{\Lambda\in X}|\Lambda/m^{2}\Lambda|^{2gn}=m^{8n^{2}g^{2}},
where
m\leq\delta^{2gn}N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))^{2n^{3}}
by Lemma
9.7
below. ∎

Lemma 9.7
.

We have
|\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})/\phi(\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{\varphi}\Gamma)|\leq\delta^{\frac{2g}{n}}N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))^{2n}
.

Proof.

Define
R=\mathcal{O}_{Z,\varphi}
,
\Lambda_{0}=\phi(\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{R}\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma)
and
\Lambda=\phi(\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{R}\mathcal{O}_{L})
. The images of the orders
\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{\varphi}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{R}\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma
in
D\otimes_{\varphi}L
are the same and thus

|\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})/\phi(\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{\varphi}\Gamma)|=|\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})/\Lambda_{0}|=|\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})/\Lambda|\cdot|\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}|.

Tensoring the canonical morphisms
\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{L}\to\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma
with
\mathcal{O}_{D}
gives

\frac{\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{R}\mathcal{O}_{L}}{\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{R}\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{R}\left(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma\right).

As
\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\left(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma\right)\cong\left(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma\right)^{\frac{2g}{n}}
surjects onto
\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{R}\left(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma\right)
, we get

|\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}|\leq|\mathcal{O}_{L}/\varphi(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Gamma|^{\frac{2g}{n}}\leq\delta^{\frac{2g}{n}}.

We compute
|\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})/\Lambda|
. Write
I
for the
\mathcal{O}_{L}
-fractional ideal
[\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L}):\Lambda]_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}
(see e.g.
[
Voi21
,
\mathsection
9.6]
). We have
I^{2}=d(\Lambda/\mathcal{O}_{L})
by
[
Voi21
, 15.2.11,15.2.15]
. Moreover
[
Voi21
, Ex 15.8]
implies that
d(\Lambda/\mathcal{O}_{L})=d(\mathcal{O}_{D}/\mathcal{O}_{Z})\mathcal{O}_{L}
since
\Lambda\cong\mathcal{O}_{D}\otimes_{R}\mathcal{O}_{L}
, while
[
Voi21
, 15.4.7,15.5.5]
gives

d(\mathcal{O}_{D}/\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D)^{2}.

(9.9)

Thus
I^{2}=\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D)^{2}\mathcal{O}_{L}
. Taking norms we find
|\textup{M}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})/\Lambda|=N(I)=N(\textnormal{disc}_{Z}(D))^{[L:Z]}
where
[L:Z]=2n
by assumption. This implies the lemma.
∎

Write
p_{1}\colon L^{2n}\to L
for the first projection and consider the map
\varsigma\colon Y\to\mathcal{I}
,
\Lambda\mapsto p_{1}(\Lambda)
, where
\mathcal{I}
is the set of non-zero
\mathcal{O}_{L}
-fractional ideals, so that
h_{L}=|\mathcal{I}/L^{\times}|
.
We use the following version of Morita equivalence:

Lemma 9.8
.

The map
\varsigma\colon Y\to\mathcal{I}
is a bijection with inverse given by
I\mapsto I^{2n}
.

Proof.

If
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
\Lambda=I^{2n}
then
p_{1}(\Lambda)=I
. Conversely, let
\Lambda\in Y
and define
I=p_{1}(\Lambda)
. We claim
I^{2n}=\Lambda
. We first check
\Lambda\subseteq I^{2n}
. Let
v\in\Lambda
. Write
S_{1i}\in\textup{End}(\mathcal{O}_{L}^{2n})=\textup{M}_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
for the endomorphism
(x_{j})\mapsto(x_{\sigma(j)})
where
\sigma
is the permutation
(1i)\in\mathfrak{S}_{2n}
. Then
S_{1i}\Lambda=\Lambda
. In particular
v_{i}=p_{1}(S_{1i}v)\in p_{1}(\Lambda)=I
for all
i
. Consequently,
v\in I^{2n}
as well and indeed
\Lambda\subseteq I^{2n}
. Conversely, let
v\in I^{2n}
. For each
i
we have
v_{i}\in I
, and so
v_{i}=p_{1}(w_{i})
for some
w_{i}\in\Lambda
. Let
P_{i}\in\textup{End}(\mathcal{O}_{L}^{2n})
be the endomorphism
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n})\mapsto(0,\ldots,0,x_{i},0,\ldots,0)
. Define
w_{i}^{\prime}=P_{i}S_{1i}w_{i}
. Then
w_{i}^{\prime}=(0,\ldots,0,v_{i},0,\ldots,0)\in\Lambda
, and
v=\sum_{i=1}^{2n}w_{i}^{\prime}\in\Lambda
. This proves
\Lambda=I^{2n}
.
∎

For each
\lambda\in L^{\times}
and
\Lambda\in Y
we have
\varsigma(\lambda\Lambda)=p_{1}(\lambda\Lambda)=\lambda p_{1}(\Lambda)=\lambda\varsigma(\Lambda)
, and in particular we obtain an induced bijection
\varsigma:Y/L^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{I}/L^{\times}
. In conclusion we have

\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{O}_{L},\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{D})\overset{\psi}{\hookrightarrow}X/L^{\times}\overset{\beta}{\to}Y/L^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{I}/L^{\times}.

So Proposition
9.2
follows from (
9.6
) and the above lemmas.

9.3
The CM case

In this section we prove the bound for the number of conjugation classes of ring morphisms which we used in the proof of Theorem
9.1
in the CM case.

Let
L
be a totally real field of degree
g
, let
\Gamma
be an order of
L
of index
\delta
in
\mathcal{O}_{L}
, let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let
F
be a CM field of degree
2g/n
, and let
F^{+}\subset F
be the totally real field of degree
g/n
. The following result is an analogue of Proposition
9.2
in the CM case.

Proposition 9.9
.

We have

|\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})|\leq c(\delta D_{F})^{e}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}.

Here
c=(2g)^{15g^{3}}
,
e=5g^{3}
and
\Delta=D_{L}
. In the proof of Proposition
9.9
we obtain the sharper (but more complicated) bound
\tfrac{2g}{n}N^{g+1}h
with
N
and
h
defined in (
9.10
).

Strategy of proof.

We first decompose
\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
into
\varphi
-compatible morphisms for varying
\varphi\in\textup{Hom}(F^{+},L)
. Then, after applying
\otimes_{\varphi^{-1}(\Gamma)}\mathcal{O}_{F}
, the degree doubles and we can apply
[
vKK19
, Prop 10.3]
. This gives a bound in terms of the class number of the number field
M_{\varphi}=L\otimes_{\varphi,F^{+}}F
and the conductor of the order
\Gamma\otimes_{\varphi^{-1}(\Gamma)}\mathcal{O}_{F}
of
M_{\varphi}
. Finally we use Diophantine analysis to bound everything in terms of
F
and
\Gamma
.

9.3.1
Proof of Proposition
9.9

We continue our notation. For any
\varphi\in\textup{Hom}(F^{+},L)
we define
\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}))
to be the set of morphisms
\rho:\Gamma\to\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
such that
\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}(\varphi(x))=x
for all
x\in F^{+}
.

Lemma 9.10
.

We have
\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}))=\bigcup_{\varphi\in\textup{Hom}(F^{+},L)}\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})).

Proof.

As in Lemma
9.3
, the statement follows from the following claim: If
\rho\colon L\to\textup{M}_{n}(F)
is a morphism, then
F^{+}\subseteq\rho(L)
in
\textup{M}_{n}(F)
. Let
\alpha\in F
be a generator of
F/F^{+}
. Define the subalgebras
K^{+}=\rho(L)F^{+}
and
K=\rho(L)F
of
\textup{M}_{n}(F)
. As
\rho(L)
is totally real, we have
\alpha\in K
but
\alpha\notin K^{+}
and thus
K^{+}\neq K
. Note that
K
is a semisimple commutative
F
-subalgebra of
\textup{M}_{n}(F)
. Thus
\dim_{F}(K)\leq n
and this implies
[K:\mathbb{Q}]\leq n[F:\mathbb{Q}]
and
[K:L]\leq n[F:\mathbb{Q}]/[L:\mathbb{Q}]=2
. Note that
\dim_{L}(K^{+})\leq\dim_{L}(K)\leq 2
, as well. We have
K^{+}\subset K
and both algebras have dimension at most 2 over
L
. Since
K^{+}\neq K
the dimension of
K^{+}
over
L
must be 1. Thus
K^{+}=\rho(L)
, and hence
F^{+}\subseteq\rho(L)
as claimed.
∎

Let
\varphi\in\textup{Hom}(F^{+},L)
. Define
\Gamma_{\varphi}^{+}=\varphi^{-1}(\Gamma)\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{F^{+}}
and

\Gamma_{\varphi,F}=\Gamma\otimes_{\Gamma_{\varphi}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{F}.

Sending
\rho:\Gamma\to\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
to
\rho_{F}:\Gamma_{\varphi,F}\to\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
with
\rho_{F}(a\otimes b)=\rho(a)b
induces an injection

\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\hookrightarrow\textup{Hom}(\Gamma_{\varphi,F},\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}),\quad\rho\mapsto\rho_{F}.

Define
M_{\varphi}=L\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F^{+}}}\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Then

\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma_{\varphi,F}\overset{\sim}{\to}M_{\varphi}
, and
\Gamma_{\varphi,F}
identifies with an order of
M_{\varphi}
.
Note
M_{\varphi}
is a field, since
L
is totally real while
F
is totally imaginary. We have
[M_{\varphi}:\mathbb{Q}]=2g
.
Then
[
vKK19
, Prop 10.3, (10.4)]
give

|\textup{Hom}_{\varphi}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})|\leq 2N(\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi,F})^{g+1}h_{M_{\varphi}}

for
\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi,F}
the conductor of
\Gamma_{\varphi,F}
. (Here we obtain the factor
2
, since (the proof of)
[
vKK19
, Prop 10.3]
bounds compatible morphisms without the factor
t
and
\rho_{F}
is compatible with a morphism
F\to M_{\varphi}
that extends
\varphi\colon F^{+}\to L
.) Lenstra’s bound
[
Len92
, Thm 6.5]
and Lemma
9.11
show that
h_{M_{\varphi}}\leq h
, while Lemma
9.12
provides
N(\Gamma_{\varphi,F})\leq N
, where

N=(2\delta g)^{2g^{2}}\left(\tfrac{D_{F}}{D^{1/2}_{F^{+}}}\right)^{ng},\quad h=d\tfrac{(2g-1+\log d)^{2g-1}}{(2g-1)!},\quad d=(\tfrac{2}{\pi})^{g}D_{L}\left(\tfrac{D_{F}}{D_{F^{+}}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}.

(9.10)

Combining the above results gives
|\textup{Hom}(\Gamma,\textup{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}))/{\textup{GL}}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})|\leq\tfrac{2g}{n}N^{g+1}h
, which leads to the bound claimed in Proposition
9.9
. It remains to prove Lemmas
9.11
and
9.12
.

Lemma 9.11
.

The discriminant
D_{M_{\varphi}}
divides

D_{L}^{2}\left(\frac{D_{F}}{D_{F^{+}}^{2}}\right)^{n}
.

Proof.

Write
\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k}
for the discriminant ideal of an extension of fields
k^{\prime}/k
. From
\mathbb{Q}\subseteq F^{+}\hookrightarrow M_{\varphi}
we get
D_{M_{\varphi}}=D_{F^{+}}^{2n}N(\Delta_{M_{\varphi}/F^{+}})
, and
\Delta_{M_{\varphi}/F^{+}}
divides
\Delta_{L/F^{+}}^{2}\Delta_{F/F^{+}}^{n}
since
M_{\varphi}
is the
composite
of
L
and
F
over
F^{+}
. As
N(\Delta_{L/F^{+}})=D_{L}D_{F^{+}}^{-n}
and
N(\Delta_{F/F^{+}})=D_{F}D_{F^{+}}^{-2},
we find that
D_{M_{\varphi}}
divides
D^{2n}_{F^{+}}N(\Delta_{L/F^{+}})^{2}N(\Delta_{F/F^{+}})^{n}=D_{F^{+}}^{2n}D_{L}^{2}D_{F^{+}}^{-2n}D_{F}^{n}D_{F^{+}}^{-2n}
.
∎

Lemma 9.12
.

We have

N(\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi,F})\leq(2g\delta)^{2g^{2}}\left(\tfrac{D_{F}}{D^{1/2}_{F^{+}}}\right)^{ng}.

Proof.

It suffices to bound
i=|\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}/\Gamma_{\varphi,F}|
since
(i)\subseteq\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi,F}=\{a\in\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}\ |\ a\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}\subseteq\Gamma_{\varphi,F}\}
. Let
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
be a generator of
F/F^{+}
from Lemma
9.13
.
Write
f\in F^{+}[x]
for the minimal polynomial of
\alpha
over
F^{+}
. Then
\varphi(f)
is irreducible over
L
and
[
Voi21
, 15.2.15]
gives

(\Delta_{\varphi(f)})=[\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}:\mathcal{O}_{L}[\alpha]]^{2}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}\cdot\Delta_{M_{\varphi}/L},

where
\Delta_{g}
is the discriminant of a polynomial
g
and
\mathcal{O}_{L}[\alpha]
is the subring in
\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}
generated by
1\otimes\alpha
and
\mathcal{O}_{L}
.
Lemma
9.13
provides

N(\Delta_{f})\leq 4^{\frac{g}{n}}(\tfrac{g}{n}D_{F^{+}})^{\frac{3}{2}}N(\Delta_{F/F^{+}}),

and thus

|\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}/\mathcal{O}_{L}[\alpha]|^{2}\leq N(\Delta_{\varphi(f)})\leq\left(4^{\frac{g}{n}}(\tfrac{g}{n}D_{F^{+}})^{\frac{3}{2}}N(\Delta_{F/F^{+}})\right)^{[L:\varphi(F^{+})]}.

Define
R=\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes_{\Gamma_{\varphi}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{F}
. As
\mathcal{O}_{L}[\alpha]\subseteq R\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}
, we get

|\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}/R|\leq|\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}/\mathcal{O}_{L}[\alpha]|
and hence
i\leq|\mathcal{O}_{M_{\varphi}}/\mathcal{O}_{L}[\alpha]|\cdot|R/\Gamma_{\varphi,F}|
. The index of

\Gamma_{\varphi,F}=\Gamma\otimes_{\Gamma_{\varphi}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{F^{+}}
inside
R

is bounded by
|(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\Gamma)\otimes_{\Gamma_{\varphi}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{F^{+}}|\leq|(\mathcal{O}_{L}/\Gamma)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{O}_{F^{+}}|=\delta^{g/n}.
This implies the lemma.
∎

The proof of Lemma
9.12
uses a result on generators of quadratic field extensions which can be deduced quite directly from the proof of Minkowski’s theorem. We include here the result since we could not find a reference.

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
k
be a number field of degree
n

and let
k^{\prime}/k
be a quadratic extension with discriminant ideal
\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k}
. There exists
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{k^{\prime}}
such that
k^{\prime}=k(\alpha)
and such that

N(\Delta_{f})\leq 4^{n}m_{k}^{3}N(\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k})\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad|\mathcal{O}_{k^{\prime}}/\mathcal{O}_{k}[\alpha]|\leq 2^{n}m_{k}^{3/2}

for
\Delta_{f}\in\mathcal{O}_{k}
the discriminant of the minimal polynomial
f
of
\alpha
over
k
,
m_{k}=\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\left(\frac{4}{\pi}\right)^{s}D_{k}^{1/2}
and
s
the number of complex places of
k
. In particular
m_{k}\leq(nD_{k})^{1/2}
if
s=0
.

Proof.

Take
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{k^{\prime}}
with
\beta^{2}\in\mathcal{O}_{k}
and
k^{\prime}=k(\beta)
. By
[
Voi21
, 15.2.15]
we have

(4d)=[\mathcal{O}_{k^{\prime}}:\mathcal{O}_{k}[\beta]]^{2}_{\mathcal{O}_{k}}\cdot\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k},\quad d=\beta^{2}.

(9.11)

The proof of Minkowski’s theorem gives representatives
J_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{k}
,
i=1,\ldots,r
, of the class group of
k
with
N(J_{i})\leq m_{k}
. Choose
I\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{k}
such that
dI^{-2}
is a square-free ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{k}
. Write
M=dI^{-2}
. For some
i\in\{1,\dotsc,r\}
and some
x\in k
we have
I=J_{i}(x)\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{k}
, so

(dx^{-2})=J_{i}^{2}M\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{k}
and thus
dx^{-2}\in\mathcal{O}_{k}
.

We define
\alpha=\beta x^{-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{k^{\prime}}
. Thus
k^{\prime}=k(\alpha)
and
\alpha^{2}=dx^{-2}
. In particular if
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
is a prime ideal such that
\textnormal{ord}_{\mathfrak{p}}(dx^{-2})=1
, then
\mathfrak{p}
ramifies in
k^{\prime}/k
and
\mathfrak{p}|\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k}
. Write
dI^{-2}
as a product
AB
of ideals
A,B\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{k}
where
A
is a product of prime ideal divisors of
J_{i}
and
B
is coprime to
J_{i}
. As
AB=M
is square free,
B
is square free as well and any prime
\mathfrak{p}
dividing
B
divides
(dx^{-2})=J_{i}^{2}M
with multiplicity
1
, hence is ramified in
k^{\prime}/k
. Thus
B|\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k}
and
M=AB
divides
J_{i}\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k}
. Write
J_{i}\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k}=MC
with
C\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{k}
an ideal. Using
(dx^{-2})=J_{i}^{2}M
we find
J_{i}^{3}\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k}=C(dx^{-2})
. Thus
(dx^{-2})|J_{i}^{3}\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k}
and hence
N(dx^{-2})\leq m_{k}^{3}N(\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k})
. Then (
9.11
) gives
|\mathcal{O}_{k^{\prime}}/\mathcal{O}_{k}[\alpha]|^{2}=N(4dx^{-2})/N(\Delta_{k^{\prime}/k})\leq 4^{n}m_{k}^{3}.

∎

Section 10
Proof of the main results for integral points

In this section we combine the results obtained in the previous sections to prove Theorem
4.1
on integral points on coarse moduli schemes. Let
Y
be a variety over
\mathbb{Z}
, let
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
be nonempty open, and let
\mathcal{M}
be a Hilbert moduli stack. Suppose that
\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{M}^{I}
is associated to some
(L,I,I_{+})
, and write
g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
and
\Delta=D_{L}
. We shall see in Section
10.2
below that Theorem
4.1
is a consequence of the following result.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
and suppose that
Y
is a coarse moduli scheme over
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
of some arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
with branch locus
B=B_{\mathcal{P}}
.

(i)

Any point
P\in(Y\setminus B)(S)
satisfies
h_{\phi}(P)\leq c_{1}N_{S}^{e_{1}}
.

(ii)

The cardinality of
(Y\setminus B)(S)
is at most
c_{2}|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}N_{S}^{e_{2}}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}
.

Here
e_{1}=\max(24,5g)
and
c_{1}=(4n)^{(5n)^{4g}}
, while
e_{2}=5\cdot n^{8g}
and
c_{2}=n^{4^{9}gn^{12g}}
. Further
|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
is the maximal number of
\mathcal{P}
-level structures over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
, and
h_{\phi}
is the height on
Y(S)
defined in Section
3.5
. We also recall that for any nonempty open subscheme
T\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
we denote by
N_{T}
the product of all rational primes
p
not in
T
.

10.1
Proof of Theorem
10.1

We continue the notation introduce above. An outline of the principal ideas of the following proof of Theorem
10.1
can be found in the introduction.

Proof of Theorem
10.1
.

We suppose that
Y
,
\mathcal{P}
,
B
and
n
are as in Theorem
10.1
. To prove the theorem, we may and do assume that
U(S)
is not empty for
U=Y\setminus B
. Then we take
P\in U(S)
and we notice that
S
is a scheme over
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
since the coarse moduli scheme
Y
of
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
is a
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
-scheme. In what follows in this proof we work (in the category of stacks) over
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
, and to simplify notation we write
\mathcal{M}
for
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
.

Furthermore, to prove the statements of Theorem
10.1
, we may and do assume that
\mathcal{M}
equals
\mathcal{M}^{I}
. Indeed this follows from a formal computation using that
Y
is a coarse moduli scheme of the arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}\tau
on
\mathcal{M}^{I}
where
\tau:\mathcal{M}^{I}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}
is an inverse of the equivalence involved in the definition of the height
h_{\phi}
.

1.

In this first step we reduce via the covers constructed in Section
5.1
to the situation in which the moduli problem is representable. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the moduli problem on
\mathcal{M}
of principal level
n
-structures. As we work over
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
, the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}
is finite étale and surjective. Furthermore the moduli problem
\mathcal{Q}
is representable by a scheme
Z
since
n\geq 3
by assumption. Therefore we may apply the constructions and results in Section
5.1
with
B=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/n])
, the scheme
Z
and the morphism
Z\to\mathcal{M}
obtained by composing an equivalence
Z\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
with the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}
. Then Lemmas
5.1
and
5.2
together with the diagram displayed right before Lemma
5.2
give a connected Dedekind scheme
T
with a finite étale morphism
T\to S
and a morphism
P^{\prime}:T\to Y^{\prime}
fitting into a commutative diagram of noetherian schemes

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{Y.}

(10.1)

Here we recall that
Y^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
and that the morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
factors as
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y
where
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y
is the initial morphism of the coarse moduli scheme
Y
of
\mathcal{P}
used in the definition of the height
h_{\phi}
on
Y(S)
. Let
h_{\phi^{\prime}}
be the height on
Y^{\prime}(T)
defined right before (
5.11
). Evaluating both functions in (
5.11
) at
P^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}(T)
leads to

h_{\phi}(P)=h_{\phi^{\prime}}(P^{\prime})

(10.2)

since the height
h_{\phi}
is compatible with the dominant base change
T\to S
and the diagram (
10.1
) is commutative.
We write
(x,\alpha,\beta)
for the image of
P^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}(T)
under the equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
in (
5.1
). In particular
x=(A,\iota,\varphi)
is an object of
\mathcal{M}(T)
and
(\alpha,\beta)
lies in
\mathcal{P}(x)\times\mathcal{Q}(x)
. Then it follows from the definition of
h_{\phi^{\prime}}
that

h_{\phi^{\prime}}(P^{\prime})=h_{F}(A)

(10.3)

and we now proceed to bound the stable Faltings height
h_{F}(A)
of
A
. For this purpose we denote by
k
and
K
the function fields of
S
and
T
respectively. It holds that
k=\mathbb{Q}
since
S
is a nonempty open subscheme of
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
. Further, on using that
T
and
S
are connected Dedekind schemes with
T\to S
finite, we deduce that
K
is a number field and that
T
is an open subscheme of the spectrum of the ring of integers of
K
. Furthermore, the construction of
T
shows that we may and do assume that
K\subset\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
.

2.

In the following step we show that the abelian scheme
A
over
T
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies where
G=\textup{Aut}(\bar{k}/k)
. As
x=(A,\iota,\varphi)
is an object in
\mathcal{M}(T)
, the relative dimension of
A
over
T
equals the degree
g=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
and hence
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type since
\iota\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
embeds
L=\mathcal{O}\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
into
\textup{End}^{0}(A)
where we denote by
\mathcal{O}
the ring of integers of
L
. The reason why
A
has
G
-isogenies is as follows: The
\mathcal{O}
-abelian scheme
(A,\iota)
‘comes’ from a point in
Y(S)\subseteq Y(k)
and hence the isomorphism class of
(A,\iota)_{\bar{k}}
is stable under the action of the Galois group
G
assuring that there exist isomorphisms
\mu_{\sigma}:\sigma^{*}A_{\bar{k}}\overset{\sim}{\to}A_{\bar{k}}
with the desired properties. We now work out the details. As
K
is a finite extension of
k
, we may and do choose
\bar{k}
with
K\subset\bar{k}
. In particular this defines a morphism
\textnormal{Spec}(\bar{k})\to T
which induces the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{Y^{\prime}(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}(\bar{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y(\bar{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}(T)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}(\bar{k})]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})].}

(10.4)

Here the commutative diagram on the left hand side comes from the equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
in (
5.1
). The diagram on the right hand side is commutative, since
Y^{\prime}\to Y
factors as
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y
and since
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
is a morphism of categories over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
. Notice that
P^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}(T)
maps to the class of
(x,\alpha,\beta)
in
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}(T)]
which in turn maps to
[(x,\alpha)]
in
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})]
where we view
(x,\alpha)
inside
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})
via
k(T)=K\subset\bar{k}
.
We next show that the class
[(x,\alpha)]
is invariant under the
G
-action on
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})]
defined right before (
5.4
), that is

[(x,\alpha)]\in[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})]^{G}.

Denote by
P^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}
and
P_{\bar{k}}
the images of
P^{\prime}
and
P
under
Y^{\prime}(T)\to Y^{\prime}(\bar{k})
and
Y(S)\to Y(\bar{k})
respectively. On using that the diagram (
10.1
) commutes, we see that
P^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}
maps to the point
P_{\bar{k}}
in
Y(\bar{k})
.
In fact
P_{\bar{k}}
lies in
Y(\bar{k})^{G}
since
P
lies in
Y(S)\subseteq Y(k)
, and (
5.4
) gives that
Y(\bar{k})^{G}
identifies with
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})]^{G}
via
\pi^{-1}
. Hence in view of the commutative diagram (
10.4
) we see that the image
[(x,\alpha)]
of
P^{\prime}
in
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})]
actually lies in
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})]^{G}
. In other words
[(x,\alpha)]
is indeed invariant under the
G
-action. Then the description of
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})]^{G}
given right after (
5.4
) shows that for each
\sigma\in G
there exists an isomorphism

\mu_{\sigma}:\sigma^{*}(x,\alpha)\overset{\sim}{\to}(x,\alpha)

in the category
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})
. In particular
\mu_{\sigma}
is an isomorphism between the
\mathcal{O}
-abelian schemes
(\sigma^{*}A_{\bar{k}},\sigma^{*}(\iota_{\bar{k}}))
and
(A_{\bar{k}},\iota_{\bar{k}})
over
\bar{k}
, which implies that
\mu_{\sigma}\circ\sigma^{*}(l)=l\circ\mu_{\sigma}
for each
l
in
L=\mathcal{O}\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
viewed inside
\textup{End}^{0}(A_{\bar{k}})
via
\iota_{\bar{k}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
. Therefore we conclude that the abelian scheme
A
over
T
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies as desired.

3.

In this step we apply the effective Shafarevich conjecture for abelian varieties of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies established in Theorem
7.1
. To obtain better bounds, we would like to apply here the version of Conjecture (ES) in Proposition
7.13
. This requires to check that the following condition
(*)
is satisfied: The field extension
K/k
is normal, and the endomorphisms of
A_{\bar{k}}
and the isomorphisms
\mu_{\sigma}
are all defined over
K\subset\bar{k}
.

We first verify that
K/k
is a normal field extension. The reason why
K/k
is normal is as follows: It is the function field extension induced by
T\to S
which is essentially a base change of the Galois cover
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
defined by forgetting principal level
n
-structures. In what follows in this paragraph we work out the details. As
\textnormal{Spec}(K)\to\textnormal{Spec}(k)
is the generic fiber of
T\to S
, we observe that by Galois theory it suffices to show that the automorphism group
\textup{Aut}_{S}(T)
of the finite étale morphism
T\to S
acts transitively on
F_{s}(T)
where
F_{s}
is the fundamental functor associated to
s:\textnormal{Spec}(\bar{k})\to S
. Recall from (the proof of) Lemma
5.2
that
T
is a connected component of the noetherian scheme
S^{\prime}=S\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
which is finite étale over
S
. Then, on using that any automorphism of a scheme permutes its connected components, we see that we are in fact reduced to show that
\textup{Aut}_{S}(S^{\prime})
acts transitively on
F_{s}(S^{\prime})
. To this end we consider group homomorphisms

\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})\to\textup{Aut}_{Y}(Y^{\prime})\to\textup{Aut}_{S}(S^{\prime}).

(10.5)

The first morphism is obtained as follows: The group
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
acts on the product presheaf
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}
via (
5.3
) and then it acts on the
Y
-scheme
Y^{\prime}\to Y
by (
5.2
) and transport of structure using the equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
in (
5.1
) and the inverse
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y^{\prime}
defined right after (
5.1
). The second morphism is induced by the base change
S\to^{P}U\subseteq Y
. In view of (
10.5
) and the above reductions, we then see that we are reduced to show that
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
acts transitively on
Y^{\prime}_{u}(\bar{k})
where we denote by
Y^{\prime}_{u}(\bar{k})
the set of
\bar{k}
-rational points of the fiber
Y^{\prime}_{u}
of the morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
over the geometric point
u:\textnormal{Spec}(\bar{k})\to^{s}S\to^{P}U\subseteq Y
. To compute
Y^{\prime}_{u}(\bar{k})
, we view again our
(x,\alpha)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(T)
inside
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{k})
via
k(T)=K\subset\bar{k}
. On using that
Y^{\prime}\to Y
factors as
Y^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y
and (
5.1
) is a morphism of categories over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
, we deduce from (
10.1
) that
\pi(x,\alpha)=u
. Then the arguments surrounding (
5.8
) show that the equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
in (
5.1
) induces

Y^{\prime}_{u}(\bar{k})\overset{\sim}{\to}\{[(x,\alpha,\beta^{\prime})],\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}(x)\}.

(10.6)

Moreover, we observe that this identification is by construction (via transport of structure) compatible with the
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
-actions. Now, the key point is that the action of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
on the set
\mathcal{Q}(x)
is transitive. Indeed if
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
are in
\mathcal{Q}(x)
, then the automorphism
\beta^{-1}\beta^{\prime}
of the constant group scheme
(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})^{2}_{\bar{k}}
identifies with some
g\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
and thus we obtain that
g\cdot\beta^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}g^{-1}=\beta
. It follows that
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
acts transitively on the right hand side of (
10.6
) and hence on
Y^{\prime}_{u}(\bar{k})
since the identification in (
10.6
) is compatible with the
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
-actions. Then in view of the above reductions we conclude that
K/k
is indeed normal. Thus the first part of condition
(*)
is satisfied.

To verify the second part of condition
(*)
, we recall that
(x,\alpha,\beta)
lies in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}(T)
. The existence of the principal level
n
-structure
\beta\in\mathcal{Q}(x)
for
x=(A,\iota,\varphi)
implies that
K
equals the field of definition
K(A_{n})
of the
n
-torsion points of
A_{K}
. Then, on using that
n\geq 3
and that
K/k
is a normal extension, we see that the field
L
appearing in Lemma
7.6
has to coincide with our
K
. Thus Lemma
7.6
proves that condition
(*)
is satisfied.

Now, we may and do apply Proposition
7.13
with our abelian scheme
A
over
T
of relative dimension
g
which is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies by step 2. After recalling that
d=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
, we see that Propositions
7.13
and
7.14
lead to the bound

h_{F}(A)\leq(4gd)^{144g^{2}d}\textnormal{rad}(N_{S}D_{K})^{e},\quad e=\max(24,5g).

(10.7)

Here we used that the morphism
T\to S
is finite and thus integral, which implies that
T
has to be the spectrum of the integral closure of
\mathbb{Z}[1/N_{S}]
in
K
and hence for any finite place
v
of
K
with
v\notin T
the residue characteristic of
v
divides
N_{S}
.

4.

In the next step we control the discriminant
D_{K}
and degree
d
in terms of
S
,
g
and
n
. As
K
and
k=\mathbb{Q}
are the function fields of
T
and
S
respectively, Lemma
5.2
(iii) gives that
d\leq|\mathcal{Q}|_{\bar{k}}
. The number of principal level
n
-structures on an
\mathcal{O}
-abelian scheme over the algebraically closed field
\bar{k}
is bounded from above by the number of
\mathcal{O}
-module isomorphisms of
(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})^{2}
. Further the cardinality of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/n\mathcal{O})
is at most
n^{4g}
since
\mathcal{O}
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of rank
g
. Combining these observations leads to

d\leq|\mathcal{Q}|_{\bar{k}}\leq n^{4g}.

(10.8)

To bound the discriminant
D_{K}
, we use that the finite morphism
T\to S
is étale. This implies that the (corresponding function) field extension
K/\mathbb{Q}
is unramified over each rational prime in
S
. Therefore the divisibility statement

\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})\mid N_{S}

(10.9)

follows from Dedekind’s discriminant theorem. We are now ready to prove the height bound in statement (i), and this will be done in the following step.

5.

To prove statement (i), we combine (
10.2
) with (
10.3
) and we deduce that our point
P\in U(S)
satisfies
h_{\phi}(P)=h_{F}(A)
. Then the estimate for
h_{F}(A)
in (
10.7
) together with (
10.8
) and (
10.9
) proves the upper bound for
h_{\phi}(P)
claimed in (i).

6.

To bound the cardinality of
U(S)
we reduce again to the situation in which the moduli problem is representable. In steps 1-4 we showed that for each
P\in U(S)
there exists a morphism
P^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}(T)
fitting into a commutative diagram (
10.1
) where
T
is the spectrum of the integral closure of
\mathbb{Z}[1/N_{S}]
in a normal extension
K/\mathbb{Q}
with
[K:\mathbb{Q}]\leq n^{4g}
and
\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})\mid N_{S}
. Moreover, we showed that
P^{\prime}
lies in the subset

Y^{*}(T)\subseteq Y^{\prime}(T)

which consists of those points
Q\in Y^{\prime}(T)
such that the equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
in (
5.1
) sends
Q
to some object
(x,\alpha,\beta)
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}}
where
x=(A,\iota,\varphi)
with
A
an abelian scheme over
T
of relative dimension
g
such that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G
-isogenies satisfying condition
(*)
and
K=K(A_{n})
. Then we deduce

|U(S)|\leq\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}|Y^{*}(T)|

where
\mathcal{T}
is the set of schemes
T=\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}}[1/N_{S}])
with
K/\mathbb{Q}
a normal extension of degree at most
n^{4g}
and
\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})\mid N_{S}
. Here we used that
{\mathcal{O}_{K}}[1/N_{S}]
is the integral closure of
\mathbb{Z}[1/N_{S}]
in
K
and we applied the following: Given
T\to S
,
P^{\prime}:T\to Y^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}\to Y
, there is at most one
P:S\to Y
such that the diagram (
10.1
) commutes; indeed the uniqueness of
P:S\to Y
follows for example from our assumption that
Y
is separated since
T\to S
sends the generic point to the generic point. As
\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})
divides
N_{S}
, we see as in the proof of Theorem B (ii) that
D_{K}\leq N_{S}^{3l}l^{6l^{2}}
for
l=n^{4g}
and then (
7.20
) leads to

|\mathcal{T}|\leq l^{7l^{3}}N_{S}^{3l^{2}}.

Next, we take
T\in\mathcal{T}
and we bound
|Y^{*}(T)|
. Write
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q}
and
B=\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
. The equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
induces an isomorphism of presheaves
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}]
on
(\textnormal{Sch}/B)
,
where
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}]
sends a
B
-scheme
S
to the set of isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}(S)
. Notice that
[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}]
is the presheaf in
[
vKK19
, Lem 3.1]
with
(Sch)
replaced by
(\textnormal{Sch}/B)
. Then, as explained in
[
vKK19
,
\mathsection
3.2 and
\mathsection
6]
, composing
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}]
with the forgetful morphism gives a morphism
\phi^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to\underline{A}_{g}
of presheaves on
(\textnormal{Sch}/B)
which factors as

\phi^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to^{\phi_{\alpha}}M\to^{\phi_{\varphi}}\underline{M}\to^{\phi_{\iota}}\underline{A}_{g}.

Here the presheaves
M
,
\underline{M}
, and
\underline{A}_{g}
on
(\textnormal{Sch}/B)
are as in
[
vKK19
,
\mathsection
6]
and the morphisms
\phi_{\alpha}
,
\phi_{\varphi}
and
\phi_{\iota}
are induced by forgetting the
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
-level structure, the
I
-polarization and the
\mathcal{O}
-module structure respectively. Write
d_{\alpha}
,
d_{\varphi}
and
d_{\iota}
for the (set-theoretical) degrees of the maps
\phi_{\alpha}(T)
,
\phi_{\varphi}(T)
and
\phi_{\iota}(T)
respectively. The factorization of
\phi^{\prime}
shows

|Y^{*}(T)|\leq d_{\alpha}d_{\varphi}d_{\iota}\cdot|\phi^{\prime}(Y^{*}(T))|.

(10.10)

To compute the set
\phi^{\prime}(Y^{*}(T))
we take
Q\in Y^{*}(T)
. Let
(x,\alpha,\beta)
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}(T)
be the image of
Q
under the equivalence
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
and write
x=(A,\iota,\varphi)
. The isomorphism
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}]
sends
Q
to the isomorphism class
[(x,\alpha,\beta)]
and hence
\phi^{\prime}(Q)=[A]
. Thus the definition of the subset
Y^{*}(T)\subseteq Y^{\prime}(T)
assures that the isomorphism classes in
\phi^{\prime}(Y^{*}(T))
are generated by abelian schemes
A
over
T
of relative dimension
g
such that
A
is of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
-type with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-isogenies satisfying
K(A_{n})=K
. Moreover, our assumption
T\in\mathcal{T}
assures that
K/\mathbb{Q}
is normal with
[K:\mathbb{Q}]\leq n^{4g}=l
and that
\textnormal{rad}(D_{K})
divides
N_{S}
. Thus Proposition
7.15
gives

|\phi^{\prime}(Y^{*}(T))|\leq(9gl)^{(4g)^{5}l}N_{S}^{(4g)^{4}}.

To bound the set-theoretical degree
d_{\alpha}
of
\phi_{\alpha}(T)
we use that the Hilbert moduli schemes
Z\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
are both varieties over
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
. Hence, as in the proof of
[
vKK19
, Cor 4.2]
, we see that an application of
[
vKK19
, Lem 8.2]
with the (naive) extension of the presheaf
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to the Hilbert moduli stack over
\mathbb{Z}
gives
d_{\alpha}\leq|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|_{T}\leq|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Then the inequalities
|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}\leq|\mathcal{Q}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
and
|\mathcal{Q}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}\leq l
show

d_{\alpha}\leq l|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}.

It seems difficult to explicitly bound
d_{\iota}
only in terms of
N_{S}
,
g
and
L
. However (
10.10
) holds with
d_{\iota}
replaced by
d^{\prime}_{\iota}=\sup|\phi_{\iota}^{-1}([A])|
with the supremum taken over all
[A]\in\phi^{\prime}(Y^{*}(T))
, and
d^{\prime}_{\iota}\leq d_{\iota}
can be bounded as follows: For each
[A]\in\phi^{\prime}(Y^{*}(T))
we see as in the proof of
[
vKK19
, Cor 10.2]
that
|\phi_{\iota}^{-1}([A])|
coincides with the number of isomorphism classes of
\mathcal{O}
-module structures on
A
. Therefore Theorem
9.1
(i) together with the bounds for
h_{F}(A)
provided by Propositions
7.13
and
7.14
leads to

d^{\prime}_{\iota}\leq aN_{S}^{b}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}

for
a=(3g)^{(3g)^{11}}(4gl)^{37(2g)^{11}l}\leq(4n)^{(4g)^{12}n^{4g}}
and
b=12(2g)^{10}
. Here we used
T\in\mathcal{T}
which assures in particular that we can apply Propositions
7.13
and
7.14
. Indeed the isomorphism classes in
\phi^{\prime}(Y^{*}(T))
are generated by abelian schemes
A
over
T
with
K(A_{n})=K
normal over
\mathbb{Q}
since
T\in\mathcal{T}
, and hence condition
(*)
is satisfied by Lemma
7.6
.

We now put everything together. The above arguments give

|U(S)|\leq|\mathcal{T}|\cdot\sup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}d_{\alpha}d_{\varphi}d^{\prime}_{\iota}\cdot|\phi^{\prime}(Y^{*}(T))|.

Then, on combining the displayed bounds with the estimate for
d_{\varphi}
in Corollary
8.2
, we deduce an upper bound for
|(Y\setminus B)(S)|=|U(S)|
as claimed in Theorem
10.1
(ii). In the case when
g=1
and
n=3
we can take
c_{2}=2^{9^{9}}
in Theorem
10.1
(ii). Indeed this follows from the above arguments by computing the constant
a
instead of estimating it. ∎

10.2
Proof of Theorem
4.1

In this section we deduce our main result for integral points by applying the above established Theorem
10.1
. We continue our notation and we recall that
\mathcal{M}
is a Hilbert moduli stack,
Y
is a variety over
\mathbb{Z}
and
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
is a nonempty open subscheme.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

As in Theorem
4.1
, we let
Z\subseteq Y
be a closed subscheme and we suppose that there exists a nonempty open
T\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
Y_{T}
is a coarse moduli scheme of some arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
with branch locus
B=B_{\mathcal{P}}
contained in
Z_{T}
. To reduce to the situation treated in Theorem
10.1
, we take
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
and we define
S^{\prime}=S\cap T\cap\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/n])
. Then Theorem
4.1
follows from an application of Theorem
10.1
with
n=3
and the open subscheme
Y_{S^{\prime}}
of the coarse moduli scheme
Y_{T}\subseteq Y
, since all involved constructions are compatible with flat base change.

In what follows in this proof we give the details showing that indeed everything is compatible with the base change
S^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
. In a first step we prove that

(Y\setminus Z)(S)\subseteq(Y_{S^{\prime}}\setminus B_{S^{\prime}})(S^{\prime}).

(10.11)

The schemes
S^{\prime}
and
S
are nonempty open subschemes of the irreducible scheme
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
. Thus
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
is a dense open subscheme, which implies that
(Y\setminus Z)(S)\subseteq(Y\setminus Z)(S^{\prime})
since
Y\setminus Z
is (an open subscheme of
Y
which is) separated over
\mathbb{Z}
. Further it holds that
(Y\setminus Z)(S^{\prime})\cong(Y\setminus Z)_{S^{\prime}}(S^{\prime})
, since
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
are both open subschemes of the terminal object
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
in
(Sch)
. Then we deduce (
10.11
) by using our assumption
B\subseteq Z_{T}
which assures that
(Y\setminus Z)_{S^{\prime}}=Y_{S^{\prime}}\setminus Z_{S^{\prime}}
is an open subscheme of
Y_{S^{\prime}}\setminus B_{S^{\prime}}
.

Next, we check that
Y_{S^{\prime}}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
10.1
. Notice that
Y_{S^{\prime}}\cong(Y_{T})_{S^{\prime}}
and that
S^{\prime}\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/n])
is open. Then the discussions surrounding (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) imply that
Y_{S^{\prime}}
is a coarse moduli scheme over
\mathbb{Z}[1/n]
of some arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
which is essentially the restriction of
\mathcal{P}
to the open substack
\mathcal{M}_{S^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
; see (
10.13
) for explicit constructions.
In particular the variety
Y_{S^{\prime}}
over
\mathbb{Z}
, the open
S^{\prime}\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
, the arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
and the integer
n\geq 3
indeed satisfy all assumptions of Theorem
10.1
. Therefore an application of Theorem
10.1
with
Y=Y_{S^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
S=S^{\prime}
gives for each point
P\in(Y_{S^{\prime}}\setminus B_{S^{\prime}})(S^{\prime})
that

h_{\phi^{\prime}}(P)\leq k_{1}N_{S^{\prime}}^{e_{1}}\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad|(Y_{S^{\prime}}\setminus B_{S^{\prime}})(S^{\prime})|\leq k_{2}|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}N_{S^{\prime}}^{e_{2}}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{2g-1}

(10.12)

with the constants
e_{1}=\max(24,5g)
,
k_{1}=(4n)^{(5n)^{4g}}
and
e_{2}=5\cdot n^{8g}
,
k_{2}=n^{4^{9}gn^{12g}}
. Here we used that
B_{S^{\prime}}=B_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
which follows from (
10.13
) and (
5.10
), and
h_{\phi^{\prime}}
denotes the height on
Y_{S^{\prime}}
defined with respect to the initial morphism
\pi^{\prime}
in (
10.13
).

We claim that
h_{\phi^{\prime}}
restricts to the height
h_{\phi}
on
Y(S)
appearing in statement (i). To prove the claim, we consider the inclusion
Y(S)\subseteq Y_{T}(S^{\prime})
obtained by using (
10.11
) with
Z
empty and
Y_{S^{\prime}}\subseteq Y_{T}
. This inclusion factors as
Y(S)\subseteq Y_{T}(U)\subseteq Y_{T}(S^{\prime})
where
U=S\cap T
, and the height
h_{\phi}
on the coarse moduli scheme
Y_{T}=M_{\mathcal{P}}
is stable under the base change
S^{\prime}\to U
. Thus (
5.5
) together with (
10.13
) implies that
h_{\phi}(P)=h_{\phi^{\prime}}(P)
for each point
P\in Y(S)
viewed inside
Y_{S^{\prime}}(S^{\prime})
using (
10.11
) with
Z
empty. This proves our claim.

Now we deduce Theorem
4.1
: After taking
n=3
, we see that (
10.11
) and (
10.12
) lead to the bound in (i) and also to the bound in (ii) since (
10.13
) assures that
|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}=|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
. More precisely, we obtain here that Theorem
4.1
holds with
c_{1}=2^{7^{7g}}
,
c_{2}=3^{9^{9g}}
and
e_{2}=5\cdot 3^{8g}
.
To verify this in the case when
g=1
, we used that we can take
k_{2}=2^{9^{9}}
in (
10.12
) for
g=1
and
n=3
as explained at the end of the proof of Theorem
10.1
(ii).

To complete the proof, we give the construction of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime}
combining (
5.5
) with (
5.6
). The presheaf
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
is defined by setting
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}
over the open substack
\mathcal{M}_{S^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
and by setting
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(x)=\emptyset
for all objects
x
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
not in
\mathcal{M}_{S^{\prime}}
. Then we define the initial morphism
\pi^{\prime}:(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to Y_{S^{\prime}}
as the composition of the morphisms

(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\to}(\mathcal{M}_{S^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to^{\pi_{S^{\prime}}}Y_{S^{\prime}}.

(10.13)

Here
\pi_{S^{\prime}}:(\mathcal{M}_{S^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to Y_{S^{\prime}}
is a base change (see (
5.5
)) of the initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y_{T}
used in the definition of
h_{\phi}
, and
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\to}(\mathcal{M}_{S^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
is the isomorphism of categories over
(\textnormal{Sch}/\mathbb{Z}[1/n])
given by the identity functor where we also write
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for the restriction of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to the open substack
\mathcal{M}_{S^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
. In fact
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is automatically an arithmetic moduli problem on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/n]}
, since
(\mathcal{M}_{S^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
identifies with
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}}S^{\prime}
as explained right after (
5.5
) and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a separated finite type DM stack over
\mathbb{Z}
by assumption.
∎

We now explain that the finiteness statement provided by Theorem
4.1
is essentially due to Faltings
[
Fal83
]
. Recall that
Z\subseteq Y
is closed and
T\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
is nonemtpy open such that
Y_{T}
is a coarse moduli scheme of some arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
with
B_{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq Z_{T}
. Suppose that
|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}<\infty
. Then the finiteness result

|(Y\setminus Z)(S)|<\infty

(10.14)

can be proved as follows. As in the proof of Theorem
4.1
, we reduce to the situation treated in Theorem
10.1
. Then the arguments of Theorem
10.1
give a finite finite set
\mathcal{T}
of schemes
T
, with
T
an open subscheme of
\textnormal{Spec}({\mathcal{O}_{K}})
for some number field
K
, such that

|(Y\setminus Z)(S)|\leq\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}|Y^{\prime}(T)|.

Here
Y^{\prime}
is a moduli scheme of a moduli problem
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{M}
, which satisfies
|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}<\infty
since
|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}<\infty
by assumption. Now, we see that (
10.14
) follows directly from the finiteness of
Y^{\prime}(T)
obtained in
[
vKK19
, Prop 12.1]
which ultimately relies on Faltings’ finiteness result
[
Fal83
, Thm 6]
. Furthermore, as the latter two results hold for any number field
F
, we expect that the same strategy allows to deduce the finiteness statement (
10.14
) more generally for any open subscheme
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
; we leave this for the future.

10.3
Proof of the corollaries

In this section we prove the corollaries. We continue our notation. Recall that
\mathcal{M}
is a Hilbert moduli stack and that
S\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
is nonempty open. Let
Y(2)
be the coarse moduli scheme over
\mathbb{Z}[1/2]
of the moduli problem
\mathcal{P}(2)
of principal level 2-structures on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Z}[1/2]}
. We first deduce Corollary
4.2
from Theorem
4.1
and Corollary
5.6
.

Proof of Corollary
4.2
.

Let
Y
be a variety over
\mathbb{Z}
. Suppose that there exists a nonempty open
T\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
Y_{T}\cong Y(2)_{T}
. We write
\mathcal{P}
for
\mathcal{P}(2)
. As in (
10.8
), we find that
|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}\leq 2^{4g}
. Further, Corollary
5.6
gives that the branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq Y(2)
is empty. Thus an application of Theorem
4.1
with
Y_{T}
and
Z=\emptyset
implies Corollary
4.2
.

We now give some details explaining how to formally apply here Theorem
4.1
. After identifying
Y_{T}
with the coarse moduli scheme
Y(2)_{T}
over
T
using (
5.5
), we extend
Y_{T}=Y(2)_{T}
to a coarse moduli scheme over
\mathbb{Z}
via
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
defined in (
5.6
). Then
Y_{T}
is a coarse moduli scheme of the arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{M}
such that on
Y_{T}(S\cap T)
the height
h_{\phi}
defined with respect to an initial morphism
\pi:(\mathcal{M}_{T})_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y(2)_{T}
equals the height defined with respect to the initial morphism
\pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to Y_{T}
in (
5.6
). Further it holds
|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}=|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
, while (
5.10
) implies
B_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}=(B_{\mathcal{P}})_{T}
and hence
B_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\subset Y_{T}
is empty.

Finally, to see that we can indeed take in Corollary
4.2
the same constants as in Theorem
4.1
, we used that Theorem
4.1
holds in fact with
c_{1}=2^{7^{7g}}
,
c_{2}=3^{9^{9g}}
and
e_{2}=5\cdot 3^{8g}
as explained in its proof. Therefore we can also take the same constants in the remark below Corollary
4.2
concerning the case
T=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2\Delta}])
. ∎

Section 11
Integral points on the Clebsch–Klein surfaces

In this section we study integral points on the Clebsch–Klein surfaces. After discussing various aspects of the motivating Diophantine equations, we show that the Clebsch–Klein surfaces are coarse Hilbert moduli schemes over
\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}]
and we compare the height
h_{\phi}
with the Weil height. Then we deduce from Theorem
4.1
explicit bounds for the Weil height and the number of integral points on the Clebsch–Klein surfaces.

11.1
Main results

To explain the main results of this section, we continue the notation of
\mathsection
1.1.1
. Recall that
S
is an arbitrary finite set of rational primes, that
\mathbb{Z}_{S}=\mathbb{Z}[1/N_{S}]
for
N_{S}=\prod_{p\in S}p
and that
h
denotes the usual absolute logarithmic Weil height (
[
BG06
, p.16]
).

11.1.1
Diophantine equations

The study of integral points on the Clebsch–Klein surfaces is motivated by the Diophantine equations (
1.1
) and (
1.3
). We now discuss various aspects of these equations; see also
\mathsection
11.1.3
for a discussion of two Diophantine results based on completely different methods.

Icosahedron equation.

We first consider (
1.3
). Let
\Sigma
be the set of
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{5}
such that
\gcd(x_{i})=1
and such that for all rational primes
p\notin S
the image of
x
in
(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{5}
is not a scalar multiple of a standard basis vector of
(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{5}
. Recall that (
1.3
) is given by

\sigma_{4}(x)=0=\sigma_{2}(x),\quad x\in\Sigma.

(
1.3
)

Here
\sigma_{n}
denotes the
n
-th elementary symmetric polynomial for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Notice that (
1.3
) has infinitely many solutions if one removes in the definition of
\Sigma
any of the two extra assumptions on
x
. Indeed the equations
\sigma_{2}=0
and
\sigma_{4}=0
are homogeneous, and they define the projective scheme
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
which has infinitely many
\mathbb{Q}
-points by (
11.3
).

For any solution
x
of (
1.3
), we define the logarithmic Weil height
h(x)=\max_{i}\log|x_{i}|.
A main goal of this section is to deduce from Theorem
4.1
the following result.

Corollary 11.1
.

Any solution
x
of (
1.3
) satisfies
h(x)\leq cN_{S}^{24}
and the number of solutions of (
1.3
) is at most
(cN_{S})^{e}
, where
e=10^{12}
and
c=10^{e}
.

The explicit height bound in Corollary
11.1
implies that for any given set
S
one can in principle determine all solutions of (
1.3
) as follows: For each of the finitely many
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{5}
with
h(x)\leq cN_{S}^{24}
, check whether
x
lies in
\Sigma
and satisfies (
1.3
). Furthermore, for small sets
S
