this allows to completely solve (
1.3
) in practice when combined with an efficient sieve for the
x\in\Sigma
which satisfy (
1.3
). Such efficient sieves which can deal with initial height bounds of the size of Corollary
11.1
were developed for many Diophantine equations via Diophantine approximation techniques, see for example Baker–Davenport
[
BD69
]
, de Weger
[
dW87
]
, K.-Matschke
[
vKM16
]
, Gherga–Siksek
[
GS22
]
and the references therein. However, new ideas are required to construct an efficient sieve for (
1.3
).

Cubic diagonal equation.

We next discuss equation (
1.1
). This equation is contained in (
1.3
) from a geometric point of view, see Lemma
11.4
. If one replaces
\mathbb{Z}^{\times}_{S}
by
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
, then

x_{1}^{3}+x_{2}^{3}+x_{3}^{3}+x_{4}^{3}=1=x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4},\quad x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times},

(
1.1
)

has infinitely many solutions. Indeed the solutions of (
1.1
) with
x_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
correspond to the
\mathbb{Q}
-points of the variety
U
over
\mathbb{Z}
defined in (
1.6
) and
U
has infinitely many
\mathbb{Q}
-points, see Section
11.7
. For each solution
x=(x_{i})
of (
1.1
), we denote by
h(x)
the logarithmic Weil height of
x\in\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{4}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
. We deduce from Theorem
4.1
the following result.

Corollary 11.2
.

Any solution
x=(x_{i})
of (
1.1
) satisfies
h(x)\leq cN_{S}^{24}
and the number of solutions of (
1.1
) is at most
(cN_{S})^{e}
, where
e=10^{12}
and
c=10^{e}
.

Here again, the explicit height bound implies that for any given
S
one can in principle determine all solutions of (
1.1
) and that for small
S
one can completely solve (
1.1
) in practice when combining with an efficient sieve. We are currently trying to develop such an efficient sieve for (
1.1
) via Diophantine approximation techniques. In fact Diophantine approximation techniques give that the number
n
of solutions of (
1.1
) satisfies

n\leq b,\quad b=\exp\bigl(24^{12}(4|S|+1)\bigl).

This is a very special case of the result of Evertse–Schlickewei–Schmidt
[
ESS02
]
for the generalized unit equation. We point out that
b
does not depend on the size of the primes in
S
and that
n\leq b
is substantially stronger than our estimate for
n
in Corollary
11.2
; indeed
b\leq(3N_{S})^{4\cdot 24^{12}}
and for each real
\epsilon>0
the prime number theorem gives
b\ll_{\epsilon}N_{S}^{\epsilon}
.

11.1.2
Clebsch–Klein surfaces and coarse Hilbert moduli schemes

The solutions of the Diophantine equations (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) correspond to
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
-points on the Clebsch–Klein surfaces. We next discuss these surfaces and we state Theorem
11.3
which gives that these surfaces are coarse Hilbert moduli schemes over
\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}]
.

Icosahedron surface.

To relate the solutions of (
1.3
) to points on a coarse Hilbert moduli scheme, let
Z\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the union of (the images of) the five
\mathbb{Z}
-points obtained by permuting the coordinates of
(1,0,\dotsc,0)
and consider the relative surface
Y
over
\mathbb{Z}
:

Y=X\setminus Z,\quad X\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}:\,\sigma_{2}=0=\sigma_{4}.

(
1.2
)

The rational surfaces
Y_{\mathbb{C}}\subset X_{\mathbb{C}}
can be obtained via Klein’s construction involving the Icosahedron; see
[
Kle73
,
Hir76
]
.
The solutions of the Diophantine equation (
1.3
) correspond (modulo
\pm 1
) to
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
-points of
Y
by Lemma
11.15
.
Hirzebruch
[
Hir76
]
showed that
Y_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hilbert modular variety of the principal level 2 subgroup of the Hilbert modular group of the field
L=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
. Moreover, building on the work of Hirzebruch
[
Hir76
]
, we shall obtain the following theorem which is the main result of this section: Let
\mathcal{M}
be the Hilbert moduli stack associated to
L=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
and define
B=\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}]
.

Theorem 11.3
.

The scheme
Y_{B}
is a coarse moduli scheme of an arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
with the following properties.

(i)

The branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}}
is empty and
|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}\leq 16
.

(ii)

Any
P\in Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
satisfies
h(P)\leq 2h_{\phi}(P)+8^{8}\log(h_{\phi}(P)+8)
.

Here the restriction of
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{M}_{B}\subset\mathcal{M}
is the moduli problem of principal level 2-structures as in
[
Rap78
, 1.21]
; we shall recall in (
11.6
) the definition of
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}_{B}
. Further
|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
is the maximal number of
\mathcal{P}
-level structures over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
as in (
3.1
),
h
is the logarithmic Weil height on
Y\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
h_{\phi}
is the height on
Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\cong Y_{B}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
defined in (
3.2
) with respect to the initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y_{B}
given in (
11.31
).

We now discuss some aspects of Theorem
11.3
. For any algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic not in
\{2,3,5\}
, it provides a natural moduli interpretation of
Y(k)=Y_{B}(k)
:

\pi^{-1}:Y(k)\overset{\sim}{\to}[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(k)].

(11.1)

Concerning the shape of the height bound in (ii): It is linear in
h_{\phi}
which is best possible and the factor
2
in front of
h_{\phi}
might be optimal, but the constants
8^{8}
and
8
can be improved up to a certain extent; see the discussions at the end of Section
11.5
.

Cubic diagonal surface.

The Icosahedron surface is related to the cubic diagonal surface which was first studied by Clebsch
[
Cle71
]
. Recall that
V
is the closed subscheme

V\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}:\sum z_{i}=0=\sum z_{i}^{3},\quad\textnormal{and}\quad U=V\setminus\cup V_{+}(z_{i})

(
1.6
)

with the union taken over the five coordinate functions
z_{i}
of
\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\textnormal{Proj}\bigl(\mathbb{Z}[z_{i}]\bigl)
. The solutions of (
1.1
) correspond to
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
-points of
U
by Lemma
11.16
. Thus the following lemma shows that solving (
1.1
) is equivalent to solving a special case of (
1.3
).

Lemma 11.4
.

There exists an open immersion
\varphi:U\hookrightarrow Y
over
\mathbb{Z}[1/3]
such that

\tfrac{1}{4}h(P)\leq h(\varphi(P))\leq 4h(P),\quad P\in U(\mathbb{Q}).

Here
h
denotes the logarithmic Weil height on
U\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
Y\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Now, as the open immersion
\varphi:U\hookrightarrow Y
is flat, we can view
U
as a coarse moduli scheme via Theorem
11.3
which gives that the open substack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}=U\times_{Y}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
admits a coarse moduli space
\pi_{U}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to U
. In particular we obtain a natural moduli interpretation of
U(k)
:

\pi_{U}^{-1}:U(k)\overset{\sim}{\to}[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}(k)]

(11.2)

for any algebraically closed field
k
whose characteristic does not lie in
\{2,3,5\}
. As
\pi
is étale by Theorem
11.3
(i), its base change
\pi_{U}
remains étale and hence the branch locus of
\pi_{U}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to U
is again empty. In the proof of Lemma
11.4
given in Section
11.7
, we shall explicitly construct the open immersion
\varphi:U\hookrightarrow Y
over
\mathbb{Z}[1/3]
.

Terminology.

The literature contains various names for (surfaces which are birational to) the surfaces defined in (
1.2
) and (
1.6
) such as for example Clebsch diagonal (cubic) surface, Clebsch surface, Klein’s icosahedron surface or Clebsch–Klein surface. In what follows we shall refer by Clebsch–Klein surfaces to the surfaces defined in (
1.2
) and (
1.6
).

11.1.3
Rational points and degeneration of
S
-integral points

To complement our discussion of Diophantine aspects of the Clebsch–Klein surfaces, we now mention in addition two important Diophantine results. They both directly follow from general theorems for smooth cubic surfaces based on completely different methods.

Rational points.

The varieties
Y
and
U
both have infinitely many
\mathbb{Q}
-points. To give a stronger statement, we define
Y^{0}=Y\setminus\cup_{i}V_{+}(x_{i})
for
x_{i}
the
i
-th coordinate function on
\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\textnormal{Proj}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{i}])
and we take
W\in\{U,Y^{0}\}
. Then for any
b\in\mathbb{R}
with
b\to\infty
it holds

|\{P\in W(\mathbb{Q}),h(P)\leq\log b\}|\gg b^{\nu}(\log b)^{\rho-1},\quad\nu=\begin{cases}1&\textnormal{if }W=U,\\
1/4&\textnormal{if }W=Y^{0}.\end{cases}

(11.3)

Here
\rho
is the Picard rank of the smooth projective surface
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
and the implied constant depends only on
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
. In
\mathsection
11.7.2
we shall deduce (
11.3
) by combining Lemma
11.4
with a result of Slater–Swinnerton-Dyer
[
SSD98
]
in which they established for certain smooth cubic surfaces the lower bound predicted by Manin’s conjecture (
[
FMT89
]
). In fact the lower bound in (
11.3
) is optimal for
W=U
if Manin’s conjecture holds, see
\mathsection
11.7.2
.

Degeneration of
S
-integral points.

Let
K
be a number field and let
S
be a finite set of places of
K
. On using Diophantine approximation techniques, Corvaja, Levin and Zannier (
[
CZ04
,
Zan05
,
CZ06
,
Lev09
,
CLZ09
,
CZ10
]
) established fundamental Diophantine results proving in particular the degeneration of
S
-integral points for large classes of varieties over
K
. For example, consider the divisor
D=V_{+}(z_{i})\cup V_{+}(z_{j})
of
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
with
i\neq j
. Then, as explained in
\mathsection
11.7.3
, the general result of Corvaja–Zannier
[
CZ10
, Thm 1]
for smooth cubic surfaces proves that the set of
S
-integral points of

V_{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus D

(11.4)

is not Zariski dense. Furthermore, it follows from Corvaja–Zannier
[
CZ10
, Thm 9]
that this degeneration result is essentially optimal; see
\mathsection
11.7.3
. We point out that even in the special case when
K=\mathbb{Q}
, proving the degeneration of
S
-integral points of
V_{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus D
is out of reach for our approach which can only deal with the much smaller variety
U_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset V_{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus D
. Here the main issue is that the moduli interpretation of the points in
V\setminus U
is substantially more complicated and it is not clear to us how to exploit it.

One could try to prove new degeneration results for
S
-integral points on surfaces by combining the moduli formalism with the powerful techniques developed by Corvaja, Levin and Zannier. To this end we now discuss an open problem for Hilbert modular surfaces.
Let
L
be a totally real quadratic field, let
\Gamma
be a congruence subgroup of
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
, and let
Y
be the canonical model over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
of the complex Hilbert modular surface
\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma
. We denote by
X
the Baily–Borel compactification of
Y
. The singular locus
X^{s}
of
X
is finite and
X\setminus Y\subseteq X^{s}
. Let
\pi:\tilde{X}\to X
be a minimal desingularization. We write
E
for the exceptional divisor of
\pi
, and we let
D
be a divisor of
\tilde{X}
with
\textnormal{supp}(D)\subseteq E
.

Problem.

Let
S
be a finite set of places of a number field
K
over which everything is defined. Prove or disprove that the set of
S
-integral points of
\tilde{X}\setminus D
is Zariski dense.

In the simplest case when
\textnormal{supp}(D)=E
, we expect that the set of
S
-integral points of
\tilde{X}\setminus D
is finite and thus degenerate. This follows for example from Theorem
4.1
(ii) for
K=\mathbb{Q}
and it should follow from the discussion below (
10.14
) for all
K
, since
\tilde{X}\setminus E\cong Y\setminus Y^{s}
and
Y
is a coarse Hilbert moduli scheme whose branch locus is precisely the singular locus
Y^{s}
of
Y
. On the other hand, the problem seems to be more complicated when
D\neq E
. A difficulty arises from the fact that the divisor
D
is never big, since
\textnormal{codim}(X,\pi(D))\geq 2
and
\pi:\tilde{X}\to X
is birational. However
\tilde{X}
and
D
have other useful geometric properties which one could try to combine with the techniques of Corvaja, Levin and Zannier in order to study the problem. For instance Hirzebruch
[
Hir73
]
explicitly determined the divisor
E
, while Hirzebruch–van de Ven–Zagier
[
Hir73
,
HVdV74
,
HZ77
]
and others classified the surfaces
\tilde{X}
in the sense of Enriques–Kodaira; see van der Geer
[
vdG88
, VII]
.

11.1.4
Outline of the section

In Section
11.2
we introduce some notation and we give in Proposition
11.5
a moduli interpretation of
X
and
Y
over
\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}]
. Then we prove Proposition
11.5
in Sections
11.3
and
11.4
. More precisely, in Section
11.3
we study the Hodge bundle
\omega
on the toroidal compactification of Rapoport
[
Rap78
]
and we show in Proposition
11.6
that
\omega^{\otimes 2}
is generated by its global sections. For this we first collect various results: We prove in
\mathsection
11.3.1
that
\omega^{\otimes 2}
descends to a line bundle on the coarse moduli space, we define and study in
\mathsection
11.3.2
integral models of modular curves, and we determine in
\mathsection
11.3.3
the Fourier expansion of the Eisenstein series
E_{i}
corresponding to the cusps. This allows us to compute in
\mathsection
11.3.4
the divisors of the Eisenstein series
E_{i}
in terms of integral models of modular curves and then we complete in
\mathsection
11.3.5
the proof of Proposition
11.6
. In Section
11.4
we deduce Proposition
11.5
by combining Proposition
11.6
with Hirzebruch’s work
[
Hir76
]
and the construction of the minimal compactification due to Faltings–Chai
[
FC90
,
Cha90
]
.

In Section
11.5
we study heights and we prove the height bound in Theorem
11.3
(ii). Here an important part of the proof is the construction of a Theta height on
Y
which we relate in Lemma
11.14
to the Weil height on
Y\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. In Section
11.6
we complete the proof of Theorem
11.3
and we deduce the Corollary from the introduction.

In Section
11.7
we first collect basic results for the Clebsch–Klein surfaces and the Diophantine equations (
1.1
) and (
1.3
). Then we deduce in
\mathsection
11.7.1
the Corollaries
11.1
and
11.2
which explicitly bound the height and the number of solutions of (
1.1
) and (
1.3
). We conclude by discussing a lower bound for the rational points of bounded height (
\mathsection
11.7.2
) and a degeneration result for
S
-integral points (
\mathsection
11.7.3
).

11.2
Moduli interpretations for
X
and
Y

Let
X
and
Y
be as in (
1.2
), and let
\mathcal{M}
be the Hilbert moduli stack associated to the real quadratic field
L=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
with discriminant
\Delta=5
. We denote by
\mathcal{O}
the ring of integers of
L
. Throughout Sections
11.2
,
11.3
and
11.4
, we shall work over
B=\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}]
and we write
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{B}
,
X=X_{B}
and
Y=Y_{B}
to simplify notation.

The stack
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

We define
\mathcal{M}_{2}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
where
\mathcal{P}
is the moduli problem on
\mathcal{M}
of principal level
2
-structures satisfying the condition (
11.6
) of
[
Rap78
, 1.21]
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is a separated finite type DM-stack over
B

with a coarse moduli space
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to M_{2}
. In fact
M_{2}
is a scheme (
[
Con05
, Thm 3.1]
) since
\mathcal{M}_{2}
admits a finite étale scheme cover.

Moduli interpretation.

Faltings–Chai
[
FC90
,
Cha90
]
constructed the minimal compactification
{M_{2}^{*}}
of
M_{2}
. In particular
{M_{2}^{*}}
is a normal scheme containing
M_{2}
as an open subscheme. The following result gives a moduli interpretation for the points of
X
and
Y.

Proposition 11.5
.

There is an isomorphism
M_{2}^{*}\overset{\sim}{\to}X
which restricts to
M_{2}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y.

Hirzebruch
[
Hir76
]
constructed the isomorphism
M_{2}^{*}\overset{\sim}{\to}X
over
\mathbb{C}
by using the five Eisenstein series
E_{i}
corresponding to the five cusps of the Hilbert modular variety
M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. Proposition
11.5
shows in particular that
Y
is a coarse moduli scheme over
B
of the arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
; we shall explicitly construct in (
11.21
) an initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to Y
using the five Eisenstein series
E_{i}
. In the following Sections
11.3
and
11.4
, we give a proof of Proposition
11.5
via the strategy outlined in
\mathsection
1.4.1
.

11.3
The square of the Hodge bundle

We continue our notation and terminology. Rapoport
[
Rap78
]
constructed a smooth toroidal compactification
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
. In this section we study the square
\omega^{\otimes 2}
of the Hodge bundle
\omega
on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
defined by Chai in
[
Cha90
, 4.1]
. We denote by
s_{i}
the five global sections of
\omega^{2}
corresponding to the five Eisenstein series
E_{i}
defined in
\mathsection
11.3.3
.

Proposition 11.6
.

The invertible sheaf
\omega^{\otimes 2}
on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
is globally generated by the
s_{i}
.

Hirzebruch
[
Hir76
]
essentially proved this statement over the generic fiber, while the method of Faltings–Chai
[
FC90
,
Cha90
]
gives that a positive power of
\omega
is generated by its global sections. The proof of Proposition
11.6
given in
\mathsection
11.3.5
crucially relies on the results and arguments of Hirzebruch and Faltings–Chai, see
\mathsection
1.4.2
for the principal ideas.

Geometric properties.

We now introduce some notation and we review geometric properties of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
,
M_{2}
,
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
and
{M_{2}^{*}}
which we shall use in the proofs below. The stack
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is smooth over
B
since the smooth stack in
[
Rap78
, 1.22]
identifies over
B
with
\mathcal{M}_{2}
. Then Corollary
5.6
implies that
M_{2}
is also smooth over
B
.
The following statements are (direct consequences of) fundamental results in
[
Cha90
,
\mathsection
3.6 and
\mathsection
4.3]
: The minimal compactification
{M_{2}^{*}}
is a normal scheme which is projective and finite type over
B
,
\mathcal{M}_{2}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
and
M_{2}\subset{M_{2}^{*}}
are dense open, there exists a canonical surjective morphism

\bar{\pi}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to{M_{2}^{*}}

(11.5)

induced by the fact that a positive power of the Hodge bundle
\omega
on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
is generated by its global sections, the restriction of
\bar{\pi}
to
\mathcal{M}_{2}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
is the initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to M_{2}
, and
\bar{\pi}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{2})={M_{2}^{*}}\setminus M_{2}
. In particular
M_{2}
is fiberwise dense in
{M_{2}^{*}}
since
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is fiberwise dense in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
by construction. As
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to M_{2}
is a universal homeomorphism and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
has irreducible fibers by
[
Rap78
, 6.2]
, the fibers of
M_{2}
, and thus of
{M_{2}^{*}}
, are irreducible. This implies that
M_{2}
, and hence
{M_{2}^{*}}
, is irreducible since
M_{2}
is flat and of finite presentation over
B
. Further
M_{2}
and
{M_{2}^{*}}
have both dimension 3 with all fibers of dimension 2, since the generic fiber of
M_{2}
has dimension 2 by
[
Rap78
, p.269]
. We also mention that Lan’s book
[
Lan13
]
on compactifications of PEL-type Shimura varieties contains a detailed construction of the toroidal and minimal compactifications of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

The moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
.

For several computations in this section it will be useful to recall the definition of the moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
of principal level 2-structures satisfying the condition in
[
Rap78
, 1.21]
. Let
x=(A,\iota,\varphi)
be in
\mathcal{M}(S)
for some
B
-scheme
S
. Then
\mathcal{P}(x)
is the set of all principal level 2-structures
\alpha:(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})^{2}_{S}\to A_{2}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,A^{\vee})^{\textnormal{sym}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\wedge_{\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O}}^{2}A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{-1}\otimes\alpha^{-1}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\wedge_{\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O}}^{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})_{S}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})_{S}}

(11.6)

commutes. Here
\mathfrak{d}^{-1}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-dual of
\mathcal{O}
and we view
\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,A^{\vee})^{\textnormal{sym}}
as a sheaf for the étale topology on
(\textnormal{Sch}/S)
. As explained in
[
Rap78
, p.266]
the top horizontal morphism is an isomorphism induced by the Weil pairing of
A
which is
\mathcal{O}
-compatible. The bottom horizontal morphism comes from the isomorphism
\wedge_{\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O}}^{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})^{2}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O}
given by the determinant and the right vertical morphism comes from
\mu_{2}\overset{\sim}{\to}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})_{S}
.

11.3.1
Descending powers of
\omega

We continue our notation. In what follows we also denote by
\omega
the restriction of
\omega
to
\mathcal{M}_{2}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
. The method of Faltings–Chai
[
FC90
]
gives that a positive power
\omega^{\otimes k}
of
\omega
descends along the initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to M_{2}
to an invertible sheaf on
M_{2}
, see
[
Cha90
, Main Thm]
. To show that one can take here
k=2
, we use a different strategy which follows Olsson’s proof of
[
Ols12
, Prop 6.1]
and which exploits that we can explicitly compute all the geometric automorphism groups of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

Lemma 11.7
.

The canonical (adjunction) morphism
\pi^{*}\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}\overset{\sim}{\to}\omega^{\otimes 2}
is an isomorphism of sheaves on
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, and
\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}
is an invertible sheaf on
M_{2}
.

Proof.

On using the arguments in the proof of Corollary
5.6
which combine Serre’s lemma with Lemma
5.3
, we compute that the geometric automorphism groups of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
are all given by
\{\pm 1\}
. In particular the order of each geometric automorphism group of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is invertible in our base
B=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}])
. Hence
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is a tame stack. Then
[
Ols12
, Prop 6.1]
gives that the pullback functor
\pi^{*}
induces an equivalence of categories between invertible sheaves on
M_{2}
and invertible sheaves
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathcal{M}_{2}
with the following property: For every geometric point
x:\textnormal{Spec}(k)\to\mathcal{M}_{2}
the action of the automorphism group scheme
G_{x}
on
x^{*}\mathcal{L}
is trivial. We claim that
\omega^{\otimes 2}
has this property. Any non-trivial automorphism
g
in
G_{x}
has order two,
and
x^{*}\omega
is a rank 1 representation of
G_{x}
.
Thus we obtain that
gv\otimes gw=g^{2}v\otimes w=v\otimes w
for sections
v,w
of
x^{*}\omega
. This shows that
G_{x}
acts trivially on
x^{*}\omega\otimes x^{*}\omega\cong x^{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}
, proving the claim. Now we can apply with
\mathcal{L}=\omega^{\otimes 2}
the arguments of the part of the proof of
[
Ols12
, Prop 6.1]
showing that
\pi^{*}
is essentially surjective. These arguments directly give that
\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}
is an invertible sheaf on
M_{2}
and that the adjunction morphism
\pi^{*}\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}\to\omega^{\otimes 2}
is an isomorphism. This completes the proof of Lemma
11.7
.
∎

The method of Faltings–Chai also gives that a positive power of
\omega
on the toroidal compactification
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
descends along the morphism
\bar{\pi}
in (
11.5
) to an invertible sheaf on the minimal compactification
{M_{2}^{*}}
. More precisely, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
\omega^{\otimes k}
is generated by its global sections we obtain that
\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes k}
is an invertible sheaf on
{M_{2}^{*}}
with

\bar{\pi}^{*}\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes k}\cong\omega^{\otimes k}.

(11.7)

To see this one can proceed as follows: By construction
\bar{\pi}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to{M_{2}^{*}}
is the canonical morphism which comes from
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to\mathbb{P}^{m}_{B}
via Stein factorization
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to^{\bar{\pi}}{M_{2}^{*}}\to^{\varphi}\mathbb{P}^{m}_{B}
, where
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to\mathbb{P}^{m}_{B}
is induced by the assumption that
\omega^{\otimes k}
is generated by its global sections. Then (
11.7
) follows from the isomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{{M_{2}^{*}}}\cong\bar{\pi}_{*}\mathcal{O}_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}}
and the projection formula which identifies
\mathcal{L}\cong(\mathcal{L}\otimes\bar{\pi}_{*}\mathcal{O}_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}})
with
\bar{\pi}_{*}\bar{\pi}^{*}\mathcal{L}\cong\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes k}
for
\mathcal{L}=\varphi^{*}\mathcal{O}(1)
.

As the line bundle
\omega^{\otimes k}
is generated by its global sections for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
by
[
Cha90
, Main Thm]
, we obtain (
11.7
) for this
k
. This is used in the proof of Proposition
11.6
given below. After completing the proof of Proposition
11.6
, we can come back and deduce that (
11.7
) holds in fact with the explicit
k=2
by Proposition
11.6
.

11.3.2
Modular curves inside
M_{2}

We continue our notation. In this section we define and study modular curves inside
M_{2}
. After giving the construction, we establish some geometric properties which we will use in
\mathsection
11.3.4
below to compute the divisors associated to certain Eisenstein series.

Modular morphism.

To define the modular curves
gC\subset M_{2}
, we consider the separated finite type DM-stack
\mathcal{E}_{2}
over
B
whose objects
(E,\alpha)
over any
B
-scheme
S
are elliptic curves
E
over
S
with a symplectic level 2-structure
\alpha:(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2}_{S}\overset{\sim}{\to}E_{2}
as in
[
FC90
, p.121]
. By tensoring with
\mathcal{O}
, we then define a morphism of stacks over
B
,

\phi:\mathcal{E}_{2}\to\mathcal{M}_{2},\quad(E,\alpha)\mapsto(E,\alpha)\otimes\mathcal{O}=(A,\iota,\varphi,\beta),

(11.8)

which lies over the modular morphism
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{M}
,
E\mapsto(A,\iota,\varphi)
, constructed by Bruinier–Burgos–Kühn
[
BBK07
, Prop 5.12]
for the stack
\mathcal{E}
over
B
of elliptic curves. In (
11.8
) the abelian scheme
A=E\otimes\mathcal{O}
over
S
represents the presheaf
T\mapsto E(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{O}
on
(\textnormal{Sch}/S)
, and
\iota:\mathcal{O}\hookrightarrow\textup{End}(A)
is induced by
\mathcal{O}
-multiplication on
\mathcal{O}
. Further, as
A^{\vee}=E\otimes\mathfrak{d}^{-1}
represents the presheaf
T\mapsto E(T)\otimes\mathfrak{d}^{-1}
on
(\textnormal{Sch}/S)
, the polarization
\varphi
is given by:

\varphi:\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\to\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,A^{\vee})^{\textnormal{sym}},\quad\varphi(\lambda)(e\otimes\alpha)=e\otimes\lambda\alpha,

for
e\otimes\alpha
in
A(T)=E(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{O}
and
\mathfrak{d}^{-1}
the
\mathbb{Z}
-dual of
\mathcal{O}
.
The symplectic
\alpha:(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2}_{S}\overset{\sim}{\to}E_{2}
is compatible with the determinant
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
and the Weil pairing composed with
\mu_{2}\overset{\sim}{\to}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})_{S}
. Thus, on using results in
[
AK18
,
\mathsection
1]
, we compute that

\beta=\alpha\otimes\mathcal{O}:(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})^{2}_{S}\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{O}\overset{\sim}{\to}E_{2}\otimes\mathcal{O}=A_{2}

is a principal level 2-structure on
(A,\iota,\varphi)
with (
11.6
). Furthermore
(E,\alpha)\mapsto(A,\iota,\varphi,\beta)
is functorial and
\phi:\mathcal{E}_{2}\to\mathcal{M}_{2}
is a morphism of stacks over
B
. Indeed
E\mapsto(A,\iota,\varphi)
is functorial since it defines the functor
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{M}
, and if
f:(E,\alpha)\to(E^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
is a morphism in
\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
then
\alpha^{\prime}=f|_{E_{2}}\alpha
and thus
\phi(f)=f\otimes\mathcal{O}
satisfies
\beta^{\prime}=\phi(f)|_{A_{2}}\beta
.

Modular curves
gC
.

Let
g\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
. We denote by
gC\subset M_{2}
the scheme-theoretic image of the morphism
\pi\tau_{g}\phi:\mathcal{E}_{2}\to M_{2}
, where the automorphism
\tau_{g}
of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is as in (
5.2
) and
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to M_{2}
is an initial morphism. Write
C
for
gC
if
g=\textnormal{id}
. We next use results of Bruinier–Burgos–Kühn
[
BBK07
]
and Yang
[
Yan10
]
to establish some geometric properties of
gC\subset M_{2}
which are applied in Lemma
11.12
below.

Lemma 11.8
.

The following statements hold.

(i)

The morphism
\phi:\mathcal{E}_{2}\to\mathcal{M}_{2}
is proper.

(ii)

The curve
C(\mathbb{C})
is an irreducible component of
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
.

(iii)

Let
g,h
in
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
. If
gC\cap hC
is nonempty, then
h^{-1}g
lies in
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
.

Here we view
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
as a subgroup of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
via
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\otimes\mathcal{O}\cong\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O}
, and
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
is the usual modular curve inside
M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. Such modular curves were studied among others by Hirzebruch, Zagier and van der Geer (see
[
Hir73
,
HZ76
,
vdGZ77
]
); notice that
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
can be defined analogously as in
[
vdG88
, Def V.1.3]
with
M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
in place of
X_{\Gamma}
. In what follows we shall make identifications via GAGA without mentioning it explicitly.

Proof of Lemma
11.8
.

We first prove (i). As already observed by Yang in his proof of
[
Yan10
, Lem 2.2]
, it follows from Bruinier–Burgos–Kühn
[
BBK07
, Prop 5.14]
that the modular morphism
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{M}
is proper. This morphism fits into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}}

of algebraic stacks in which the vertical morphisms are the forgetful morphisms. As the forgetful morphisms are finite and
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{M}
is proper, the composition
\mathcal{E}_{2}\to\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{M}
is proper and
\mathcal{M}_{2}\to\mathcal{M}
is separated. Thus
\phi:\mathcal{E}_{2}\to\mathcal{M}_{2}
is proper as claimed in (i).

We next show (ii). To relate
C(\mathbb{C})
with
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
, choose a coarse moduli space
\mathcal{M}\to M
and consider the image
V\subset M(\mathbb{C})
of the curve
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})\subset M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
under the quotient map

p_{\mathbb{C}}:M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)\to\mathbb{H}^{2}/\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})\cong M(\mathbb{C})

(11.9)

where
\Gamma(2)
denotes the kernel of the natural projection
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})\to\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
. Here the algebraic space
M
is in fact a scheme over
B
and the quotient map
p_{\mathbb{C}}
comes from the base change to
\mathbb{C}
of a morphism
p:M_{2}\to M
of
B
-schemes, which is induced by the initial property of
\pi
and which fits into a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M.}

We now combine the displayed diagrams: Bruinier–Burgos–Kühn
[
BBK07
, Prop 5.12]
showed that
V
is the image of
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{C})
under the composition
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})\to M(\mathbb{C})
and hence
C=\pi\phi(\mathcal{E}_{2})
satisfies
C(\mathbb{C})\subset p_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}(V)
. On using that
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})\cong W/\Gamma(2)
and
V\cong W/\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
for some
W\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}
which is stable under the
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
-action, we obtain

p_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}(V)=F_{1}(\mathbb{C}).

(11.10)

As the morphism
\pi\phi:\mathcal{E}_{2}\to M_{2}
is universally closed by (i) and
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is irreducible, we see that
C(\mathbb{C})
is irreducible and closed inside
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
. Notice that
C(\mathbb{C})
can not be of dimension zero. Thus
C(\mathbb{C})
is an irreducible component of the curve
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
as claimed in (ii).

To prove (iii) we take
g,h
in
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
and we suppose that
gC\cap hC
is nonempty. After possibly replacing
g
by
h^{-1}g
, we may and do assume that
h=\textnormal{id}
. Then
gC(k)\cap C(k)
is nonempty for some algebraically closed field
k
. We next construct an elliptic curve
E
over
k
with symplectic level 2-structures
\alpha,\alpha^{*}
such that

g\cdot(\alpha\otimes\mathcal{O})=\alpha^{*}\otimes\mathcal{O}.

(11.11)

As
gC(k)\cap C(k)
is nonempty and
gC
is the scheme theoretic image of the morphism
\pi\tau_{g}\phi:\mathcal{E}_{2}\to M_{2}
which is closed by (i), we obtain
(E,\alpha)
and
(E^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{E}_{2}(k)
such that
\tau_{g}\phi(E,\alpha)
and
\phi(E^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
are isomorphic in
\mathcal{M}_{2}(k)
. Write
(x,g\cdot(\alpha\otimes\mathcal{O}))
for
\tau_{g}\phi(E,\alpha)
and
(x^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O})
for
\phi(E^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
. Yang showed in
[
Yan10
, Lem 2.2]
that
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{M}
induces a bijection

\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}(k)}(E,E^{\prime})\overset{\sim}{\to}\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}(k)}(x,x^{\prime}).

Thus, after applying isomorphisms in
\mathcal{E}_{2}(k)
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}(k)
, we obtain a symplectic level 2-structure
\alpha^{*}
of
E
satisfying (
11.11
). Now, it follows from (
11.11
) that
g=u\otimes\mathcal{O}
with
u=(\alpha^{*})^{-1}\alpha
. Hence
g
lies in the image of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
inside
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\otimes\mathcal{O})\cong\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
as claimed in (iii). This completes the proof of the lemma.∎

In view of (
11.10
), the group
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})\cong\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})/\Gamma(2)
acts on
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
and then on the set of irreducible components of
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
. In what follows, the action of
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
on (the set of irreducible components of)
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
will refer to this action.

Components of
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
.

We showed in Lemma
11.8
(ii) that
C(\mathbb{C})
is an irreducible component of
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
. To describe all irreducible components of
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
in terms of
C
, write
G
for
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
and let
H\subset G
be the stabilizer of
C(\mathbb{C})
under the action of
G
on the set of irreducible components of
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
. It is known (
[
vdG88
, p.189]
) that

F_{1}(\mathbb{C})=\cup F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}

(11.12)

has 10 irreducible components
F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}
which are labelled so that the closure of
F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}
in
{M_{2}^{*}}(\mathbb{C})
does not intersect after desingularization the two cusps
i
and
j
of the minimal desingularization of
{M_{2}^{*}}(\mathbb{C})
. Here we label the five cusps of
{M_{2}^{*}}(\mathbb{C})
by
0,\dotsc,4
following Hirzebruch
[
Hir76
]
. The next result computes
F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}
in terms of
C
.

Lemma 11.9
.

For each
ij
there exists
g_{ij}\in G
such that
F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}=(g_{ij}C)_{\mathbb{C}}
. In particular,
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})=\cup(gC)_{\mathbb{C}}
with the disjoint union taken over all left cosets
g\in G/H
.

Proof.

Let
p_{\mathbb{C}}:M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\to M(\mathbb{C})
be the quotient map induced by
\Gamma(2)\subset\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
as in (
11.9
). The Hirzebruch–Zagier divisor
V=p_{\mathbb{C}}(F_{1}(\mathbb{C}))
in
M(\mathbb{C})
is irreducible, and the arguments before (
11.10
) show that the irreducible component
C(\mathbb{C})
of
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
satisfies
V=p_{\mathbb{C}}(C(\mathbb{C}))
. Then we deduce that
G
acts transitively on the set of irreducible components of
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
. Hence for each
ij
there exists
g_{ij}\in G
such that

F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}=g_{ij}(C(\mathbb{C}))=(g_{ij}C)_{\mathbb{C}}.

Here the second equality uses that the identification
M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
is compatible with the actions of
G\cong\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})/\Gamma(2)
, where
G\subset\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
acts on
M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
via
\pi:[\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})]\overset{\sim}{\to}M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and (
5.3
) while
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})/\Gamma(2)
acts on
\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
as usual. This proves the first statement, which implies the second statement since
H
is the stabilizer of
C(\mathbb{C})
. ∎

This description of the connected components of
F_{1}(\mathbb{C})
is crucial for our computation of the divisors of certain Eisenstein series in Lemma
11.12
below.

11.3.3
Eisenstein series

We continue our notation. Let
\Gamma(2)
be the kernel of the projection
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})\to\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
. In this section we discuss the Fourier expansion of the Eisenstein series of weight two for
\Gamma(2)
corresponding to the cusps of
\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
. In particular we compute the constant terms.

Definition of
E_{i}
.

Following Hirzebruch
[
Hir76
]
we label the five cusps of
\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
by
0,\dotsc,4
with the cusp
\infty
labelled by 0. The group
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
acts transitively on these five cusps. For any
i\in\{0,\dotsc,4\}
choose
A_{i}\in\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
which sends the cusp
i
to
\infty
. Hirzebruch works in
[
Hir76
, p.164]
with five Eisenstein series
E_{i}
of weight two for
\Gamma(2)
, defined by

E_{0}=\sum\phantom{}^{{}^{\prime}}\tfrac{1}{N(m\tau+n)^{2}},\quad E_{i}=E_{0}|_{A_{i}},

(11.13)

such that
E_{i}
vanishes at all cusps except the
i
-th.
Here the restricted sum is taken via Hecke’s summation over all coprime
(m,n)\in\mathcal{O}^{2}
reducing to
(0,1)
in
(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})^{2}
, where the restriction
\phantom{}{}^{{}^{\prime}}
means we sum modulo the subgroup of
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
reducing to
1
in
\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O}
. Further we denote by
|_{A_{i}}
the usual slash operator and by
N(\cdot)
the usual norm, see (
11.14
).

Fourier expansion.

Klingen
[
Kli62
]
used analytic methods to establish formulas for the Fourier coefficients of Eisenstein series of weight
k\geq 2
for
\Gamma(2)
. Therein the constant term
a_{0}
is determined by the value at
k
of certain zeta functions, and for
E_{i}
one can reduce the computation of
a_{0}
to the result
\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})}(-1)=\tfrac{1}{30}
of Siegel
[
Sie69
]
and Zagier
[
Zag76
]
. Then computing the quantities in Klingen’s formulas leads to the following:

Lemma 11.10
.

The coefficients of the Fourier expansion of
E_{i}
at the cusp
j
lie in
\mathbb{Z}
and are coprime. The constant term
a_{0}
satisfies
a_{0}=3
when
j=i
and
a_{0}=0
otherwise.

The computations required to deduce this lemma from
[
Kli62
]
are all basic and straight forward. However, we include them here since we are not aware of precise references. We begin by recalling the slash operator
|_{A}
and the norm
N(\cdot)
. They are defined by

f|_{A}(\tau)=\tfrac{1}{N(c\tau+d)^{2}}f(A\tau)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad N(u\tau+v)=(u\tau_{1}+v)(u^{\prime}\tau_{2}+v^{\prime})

(11.14)

for any Hilbert modular form
f
of weight two for
\Gamma(2)
, any
A\in\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
given by
\begin{pmatrix}a\ b\\
c\ d\end{pmatrix}
, any
(u,v)\in\mathcal{O}^{2}
and any
\tau\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Here
x^{\prime}
denotes the conjugate of
x\in\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
.

Relation to
G_{2}
and
G_{2}^{*}
.

To apply Klingen’s results, we first relate
E_{0}
to the Eisenstein series
G_{2}
and
G_{2}^{*}
defined in
[
Kli62
, (12) and
\mathsection
3]
with
k=2
,
\mathfrak{a}=\mathcal{O}
,
\mathfrak{n}=2\mathcal{O}
and
(a_{1},a_{2})=(0,1)
. The ray class group modulo
2\mathcal{O}
is trivial. Hence
[
Kli62
, (20)]
leads to

wE_{0}=G_{2}^{*}=\tfrac{1}{\zeta_{2}(2)}G_{2},\quad w=4.

(11.15)

Here
\zeta_{2}(2)=\sum\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{O}/I|^{2}}
with the sum taken over all ideals
I\subseteq\mathcal{O}
coprime to
2\mathcal{O}
, and
w
is the index of
\{x\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{+};\,\bar{x}=1\}
inside
\{x\in\mathcal{O}^{\times};\,\bar{x}=1\}
where we denote by
\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{+}
the totally positive units of
\mathcal{O}
and we write
\bar{x}
for the image of
x\in\mathcal{O}
inside
\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O}
.

Constant term and
\zeta_{2}(2)
.

We next compute the constant term
a_{0}
of the Fourier expansion of
E_{0}
at
\infty
and the value
\zeta_{2}(2)
. The index of
\{x\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{+};\,\bar{x}=1\}
inside
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
is given by
|(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})^{\times}|w
. Then, on using the formula
[
Kli62
, (14)]
for the constant term
\Sigma_{1}
of the Fourier expansion of
G_{2}
at
\infty
, we find that
\Sigma_{1}=3w\zeta_{2}(2)
. Thus (
11.15
) proves

a_{0}=3,\quad\textnormal{while}\quad\zeta_{2}(2)=\tfrac{\pi^{4}}{\sqrt{5}}\cdot\tfrac{1}{40}

(11.16)

follows from
\zeta_{L}(-1)=\tfrac{1}{30}
in
[
Sie69
,
Zag76
]
where
\zeta_{L}
is the Dedekind zeta function of
L=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
. Here we used the Euler product formula which gives
\zeta_{2}(2)=\tfrac{15}{16}\zeta_{L}(2)
and we applied the functional equation which relates
\zeta_{L}(-1)
with
\zeta_{L}(2)
.

Proof of Lemma
11.10
.

Let
i\in\{0,\dotsc,4\}
be a cusp. By construction the Eisenstein series
E_{i}
is of the form
E_{i}=E_{0}|_{A_{i}}
where
A_{i}\in\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
sends the cusp
i
to
\infty
. Thus the constant term
a_{0}
of the Fourier expansion of
E_{i}
at the cusp
j
has the following properties: It equals the constant term of the Fourier expansion of
E_{0}
at
\infty
if
j=i
, and it satisfies
a_{0}=0
otherwise. Hence (
11.16
) proves the statements about the constant term.

We now deal with the higher Fourier coefficients of
E_{i}
. Denote by
G_{2,i}
the Eisenstein series defined by Klingen in
[
Kli62
, (12)]
with
k=2
,
\mathfrak{a}=\mathcal{O}
,
\mathfrak{n}=2\mathcal{O}
and
(a_{1},a_{2})=A_{i}^{t}(0,1)
where
A_{i}^{t}
is the transpose of
A_{i}
. On using (
11.15
) and (
11.16
) we compute

E_{i}=E_{0}|_{A_{i}}=\tfrac{\sqrt{5}}{\pi^{4}}\cdot 10G_{2,i}.

(11.17)

In
[
Kli62
, p.182]
, Klingen obtains a formula for the higher Fourier expansion
\Sigma_{2}
of
G_{2,i}
at the cusp
\infty
and he observes that
e^{2\pi i(a_{2}\nu+a^{\prime}_{2}\nu^{\prime})}
is a 4-th root of unity for all
\nu\in\tfrac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}\mathcal{O}
. These results and (
11.17
) imply that all higher Fourier coefficients of
E_{i}
lie in
\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-1}]
, and then they all lie in
\mathbb{Z}
since the terms involving
\sqrt{-1}
cancel out by symmetry.

Finally, we show that the Fourier coefficients are coprime. For this it suffices to find a Fourier coefficient
a_{\nu}
with
3\nmid a_{\nu}
, since
a_{0}=3
and
E_{i}=E_{0}|_{A_{i}}
. A set of representatives of
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
modulo
\{x\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{+};\,\bar{x}=1\}
is given by
\{\pm\epsilon^{i};\,i=0,\dotsc,5\}
. Then, on using again Klingen’s formula for
\Sigma_{2}
, we compute that the totally positive element
\nu=\epsilon/\sqrt{5}
of the module
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}
satisfies
a_{\nu}=-8
which is not divisible by 3. This completes the proof.
∎

In particular, Lemma
11.10
assures that the Hilbert modular form
E_{i}
of weight two for
\Gamma(2)
has all Fourier coefficients in
B=\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}]
. Thus the
q
-expansion principle of Rapoport
[
Rap78
]
and Faltings–Chai
[
FC90
,
Cha90
]
gives that
E_{i}
corresponds to a global section of the square of the Hodge bundle
\omega^{\otimes 2}
on the toroidal compactification
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
.

11.3.4
Divisors of the Eisenstein series

We continue our notation. Lemma
11.7
gives that
\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}
is an invertible sheaf on the regular scheme
M_{2}
, where
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to M_{2}
is an initial morphism and
\omega
is the Hodge bundle. In this section we study the divisors
D_{i}
on
M_{2}
associated to the five global sections of
\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}
corresponding to the five Eisenstein series
E_{i}
defined in
\mathsection
11.3.3
.

To compute the divisors
D_{i}
, we first show that they are horizontal. The following lemma relies on the computation of the constant term of
E_{i}
in Lemma
11.10
and fundamental results for
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
and
{M_{2}^{*}}
proven by Rapoport
[
Rap78
]
and Faltings–Chai
[
FC90
,
Cha90
]
.

Lemma 11.11
.

Each prime divisor
Z
of
D_{i}
is the closure of its generic fiber
Z_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Proof.

Let
Z
be a prime divisor of
D_{i}
. The idea of the proof is as follows: We first reduce the problem to showing that the closure
Z^{*}
of
Z
in
{M_{2}^{*}}
is a horizontal divisor. Then we prove that
Z^{*}
is a horizontal divisor by combining the
q
-expansion principle with the computation of the constant term of the Eisenstein series
E_{i}
in Lemma
11.10
.

We first introduce some notation and make a reduction. Let
s_{i}
be the global section of
\omega^{\otimes 2}
on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
corresponding to the Eisenstein series
E_{i}
. We also denote by
s_{i}
its restriction to
\mathcal{M}_{2}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
. Write
s=s_{i}
and
D=D_{i}
. For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we obtain that
kD
is the divisor on
M_{2}
associated to the global section of
\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes k}
defined by
s^{\otimes k}
; indeed
\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2k}\cong(\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes k}
by Lemma
11.7
and the projection formula.
To prove Lemma
11.11
, we thus may replace
s
by
s^{\otimes k}
and
D
by
kD
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Moreover
[
Cha90
, Main Thm (iii)]
and (
11.7
) show that we may and do this for some
k
such that
\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2k}
is an invertible sheaf on
{M_{2}^{*}}
with
\bar{\pi}^{*}\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2k}\cong\omega^{\otimes 2k}
for
\bar{\pi}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to{M_{2}^{*}}
as in (
11.5
). We now can consider the divisor
D^{*}
on the normal scheme
{M_{2}^{*}}
associated to the global section of
\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2k}
defined by
s
.

Let
Z^{*}
be the closure in
{M_{2}^{*}}
of our given prime divisor
Z
of
D
. As
\pi
is the restriction of
\bar{\pi}
to
\mathcal{M}_{2}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
and
\bar{\pi}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{2})={M_{2}^{*}}\setminus M_{2}
, we see that
\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2k}
is the restriction of
\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2k}
to
M_{2}\subset{M_{2}^{*}}
. It follows that
D
is the restriction of
D^{*}
to
M_{2}\subset{M_{2}^{*}}
. In particular
Z^{*}
is a prime divisor of
D^{*}
. Moreover the claim (1) below gives that
Z^{*}
is a horizontal divisor. Thus the generic point
z
of
Z^{*}
lies in the generic fiber
Z^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}
of
Z^{*}
and
Z^{*}
is the closure of
Z^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}
in
{M_{2}^{*}}
. In fact
z
also lies in
Z
since
Z=M_{2}\cap Z^{*}
is nonempty open in the irreducible
Z^{*}
. Hence
Z_{\mathbb{Q}}=Z\cap Z^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}
contains
z
and thus the closure of
Z_{\mathbb{Q}}
in
M_{2}
is
Z^{*}\cap M_{2}=Z
. This proves that
Z
is the closure of its generic fiber as claimed in Lemma
11.11
.

It remains to show the claim (1) that any prime divisor of
D^{*}
is horizontal. Each prime divisor of
D^{*}
is either horizontal or vertical, since
{M_{2}^{*}}\to B
is closed and
B
is a connected Dedekind scheme. To show that
D^{*}
has no vertical prime divisors, we take a closed point
p\in B
. The fiber
M^{*}_{2,p}
of
{M_{2}^{*}}
over
p
is irreducible. Hence any vertical prime divisor
Z^{*}
of
D^{*}
over
p
equals
M^{*}_{2,p}
, since
Z^{*}\subseteq M^{*}_{2,p}
is irreducible closed with
\dim Z^{*}=\dim{M_{2}^{*}}-1
equal to
2=\dim M^{*}_{2,p}.

This implies that the global section of
\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2k}
defining
D^{*}
vanishes on
Z^{*}=M^{*}_{2,p}
. As
s
identifies with the pullback of this section under
\bar{\pi}
, we deduce that
s
vanishes on the whole fiber
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2,p}
of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
over
p
. Then the
q
-expansion principle assures that the
q
-expansion of
s
at each cusp of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is zero modulo
p
. But at the cusp
i
of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
corresponding to
E_{i}
, Lemma
11.10
gives that the constant term of the
q
-expansion of
s=s_{i}^{\otimes k}
is
3^{k}
which is nonzero modulo
p
. This shows that
D^{*}
can indeed not have vertical prime divisors, which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Next, we combine the above result with Lemmas
11.8
,
11.9
and
11.10
to compute the divisors
D_{i}
in terms of the modular curves
gC
constructed in
\mathsection
11.3.2
.

Lemma 11.12
.

The following statements hold.

(i)

The support of
D_{i}
is the union of four disjoint
gC
with
g\in\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
.

(ii)

The intersection
\cap_{i}D_{i}
is empty.

Proof.

To prove (i) we first determine the support of
D_{i}
over
\mathbb{Q}
. In Lemma
11.10
we computed the constant term of the Fourier expansion of our Eisenstein series
E_{i}
in each cusp
j
of
M_{2}^{*}(\mathbb{C})
: It is nonzero if and only if
j=i
. Thus our
E_{i}
is up to a constant factor the Eisenstein series
E_{i+1}
in
[
vdG88
, p.190]
. Then
[
vdG88
, p.190]
implies

\textnormal{supp}D_{i,\mathbb{C}}=\cup F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}

(11.18)

since by construction the Eisenstein series
E_{i}
corresponds to the global section of
\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}
defining the divisor
D_{i}
. Here the union is taken over the four cusps
j\neq i
and we recall from (
11.12
) that the curve

F_{1}(\mathbb{C})=\cup F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}

has 10 irreducible components
F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Lemma
11.9
gives
g_{ij}\in\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
such that
F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}=(g_{ij}C)_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then (
11.18
) leads to

\textnormal{supp}D_{i,\mathbb{Q}}=\cup F^{ij}_{\mathbb{Q}},\quad F^{ij}=g_{ij}C

(11.19)

with the union taken over the four cusps
j\neq i
. We now compute the closure inside
M_{2}
. As the moduli stack
\mathcal{E}_{2}
of elliptic curves with symplectic level 2-structures is irreducible and
F^{ij}=g_{ij}C
is the scheme-theoretic image of
\pi\tau_{g_{ij}}\phi:\mathcal{E}_{2}\to M_{2}
, we obtain that
F^{ij}=g_{ij}C
is an integral closed subscheme of
M_{2}
with generic fiber
F^{ij}_{\mathbb{Q}}
nonempty. Hence
F^{ij}
is the closure of
F^{ij}_{\mathbb{Q}}
in
M_{2}
. Further, the closure of
\textnormal{supp}D_{i,\mathbb{Q}}
in
M_{2}
is given by
\textnormal{supp}D_{i}
since each prime divisor of
D_{i}
is the closure of its generic fiber by Lemma
11.11
. Thus (
11.19
) shows

\textnormal{supp}D_{i}=\cup F^{ij}

(11.20)

with the union taken over the four cusps
j\neq i
. Next, we determine the intersection
F^{ij}\cap F^{kl}
for arbitrary cusps
i,j,k,l
. We claim that either
F^{ij}\cap F^{kl}
is empty or
F^{ij}=F^{kl}
. To prove this claim, we assume that
F^{ij}\cap F^{kl}
is nonempty. Then Lemma
11.8
gives that
g=g_{kl}^{-1}g_{ij}
lies in the image of
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
in
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
via
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\otimes\mathcal{O}\cong\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O}
. It follows that
gC=C
, since
C
is the scheme-theoretic image of the morphism
\pi\phi:\mathcal{E}_{2}\to M_{2}
and
\phi
is induced by tensoring with
\mathcal{O}
. This implies that
F^{ij}=g_{ij}C=g_{kl}C=F^{kl}
as claimed. We now consider the four subschemes
F^{ij}\subset M_{2}
appearing in (
11.20
). They are pairwise distinct since their base changes
F^{ij}_{\mathbb{C}}
are pairwise distinct by (
11.12
), and thus the claim below (
11.20
) assures that these four
F^{ij}
are disjoint. This completes the proof of (i).

It remains to prove (ii). Suppose that (ii) does not hold, that is
\cap D_{i}
is nonempty. Then (
11.20
) gives that
\cap_{i}\cup F^{ij}
is nonempty and hence there are
j_{i}\neq i
such that
\cap_{i}F^{ij_{i}}
is nonempty. But, as the five
F^{ij_{i}}
can not all be equal by (
11.12
), the claim below (
11.20
) shows that there exist at least two disjoint
F^{ij_{i}}\subset M_{2}
which implies that
\cap_{i}F^{ij_{i}}
is empty. This contradiction proves (ii) and completes the proof of Lemma
11.12
.
∎

In the proof of Lemma
11.12
(i) we obtain in addition a description in (
11.20
) of those four
g\in\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
with
gC
a prime divisor of
D_{i}
. The description is in terms of the intersection behaviour of the cusp resolutions in the minimal desingularization of
M_{2}^{*}(\mathbb{C})
. This is crucial for the proof of (ii) and might be of independent interest.

11.3.5
Proof of Proposition
11.6

We continue our notation. Recall that
\bar{\pi}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to{M_{2}^{*}}
is the canonical morphism from (
11.5
) and that
s_{i}
is the global section of
\omega^{\otimes 2}
corresponding to the Eisenstein series
E_{i}
defined in
\mathsection
11.3.3
where
\omega
denotes the Hodge bundle on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
. In the following proof of Proposition
11.6
, we combine Lemmas
11.10
and
11.12
with fundamental results for
{M_{2}^{*}}
obtained by Rapoport
[
Rap78
]
and Faltings–Chai
[
FC90
,
Cha90
]
.

Proof of Proposition
11.6
.

We first show that the sections
s_{i}
have no common zeroes on the boundary
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{2}
. It follows for example from
[
Cha90
, Main Thm]
that
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{2}
has five connected components which we label such that
\bar{\pi}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to{M_{2}^{*}}
sends the component
i
to the cusp
i
of
M_{2}^{*}
, where the five cusps of
{M_{2}^{*}}
are labelled by
0,\dotsc,4
. The section
s_{i}
corresponds to the Eisenstein series
E_{i}
, and Lemma
11.10
gives that the constant term of the Fourier expansion of
E_{i}
at the cusp
i
of
M_{2}^{*}
is invertible in
B
. Then the
q
-expansion principle implies that
s_{i}
does not vanish in the connected component
i
of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{2}
. Thus the sections
s_{i}
have no common zeroes on the boundary
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{2}
as desired.

Further, it is a rather direct consequence of Lemma
11.12
(ii) that the sections
s_{i}
have no common zeroes in
\mathcal{M}_{2}
. The details are as follows. Let
p:X\to\mathcal{M}_{2}
be an étale scheme cover of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
. To show that the sections
s_{i}
generate
\omega^{\otimes 2}
over
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, it suffices to prove that the intersection

\cap_{i}\textnormal{div}(p^{*}s_{i})
is empty. Corollary
5.6
implies that the initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to M_{2}
is étale and hence
q=p\pi
is étale. Then we compute

\textnormal{div}(p^{*}s_{i})=\textnormal{div}(q^{*}t_{i})=q^{*}D_{i}

where
D_{i}
is the divisor associated to the global section
t_{i}
of
\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}
defined by
s_{i}
. Here we identified
\pi^{*}t_{i}
with
s_{i}
via the adjunction isomorphism
\pi^{*}\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}\overset{\sim}{\to}\omega^{\otimes 2}
in Lemma
11.7
. Now, as the intersection
\cap_{i}D_{i}
is empty by Lemma
11.12
(ii), we deduce that
\cap_{i}\textnormal{div}(p^{*}s_{i})=\cap_{i}q^{*}D_{i}
is empty as desired. This completes the proof of Proposition
11.6
.
∎

11.4
Proof of Proposition
11.5

We continue the notation and terminology of
\mathsection
11.2
and
\mathsection
11.3
. Recall that
{M_{2}^{*}}
denotes the minimal compactification of the coarse moduli scheme
M_{2}
. In this section we use the above results to construct the isomorphisms
{M_{2}^{*}}\overset{\sim}{\to}X
and
M_{2}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y
in Proposition
11.5
.

Proof of Proposition
11.5
.

As in the statement of Proposition
11.5
, we consider the objects over the base scheme
B=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}])
. The following proof consists of four steps.

1.

We first construct a morphism
{M_{2}^{*}}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
using the Hodge bundle
\omega
on the toroidal compactification
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
. Proposition
11.6
gives that
\omega^{\otimes 2}
is globally generated by the five global sections
s_{i}
corresponding to the five Eisenstein series
E_{i}
defined in
\mathsection
11.3.3
. Hence these sections
s_{i}
define a morphism
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}

which is automatically proper since
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
is proper over
B
. Then Stein factorization decomposes
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
into two morphisms:

{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to^{\bar{\pi}}{M_{2}^{*}}\to^{f}\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B},

where the canonical morphism
\mathcal{O}_{{M_{2}^{*}}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\bar{\pi}_{*}\mathcal{O}_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}}
is an isomorphism and the morphism
f
is finite. Here we used the construction of
{M_{2}^{*}}
which gives moreover that the restriction of
\bar{\pi}
to
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is an initial morphism
\mathcal{M}_{2}\to M_{2}
, see
[
Cha90
, Main Thm (vi)]
.

2.

Next, we prove that the scheme theoretic image
X^{\prime}
of
f
equals
X
. The projection formula together with
\omega^{\otimes 2}\cong\bar{\pi}^{*}f^{*}\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\bar{\pi}_{*}\mathcal{O}_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{{M_{2}^{*}}}
implies that
\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}\cong f^{*}\mathcal{O}(1)
. In particular
\bar{\pi}_{*}\omega^{\otimes 2}
is an invertible sheaf on
{M_{2}^{*}}
which is globally generated by the five sections defined by the
s_{i}
. The section
s_{i}
corresponds to the Eisenstein series
E_{i}
which coincides up to a constant factor with the Eisenstein series
E_{i+1}
in van der Geer
[
vdG88
, p.191]
. Thus the arguments in
[
vdG88
, p.190-191]
, which can be modified so that they work over
\mathbb{Q}
as explained by Shepherd-Barron–Taylor
[
SBT97
, p.289]
,
show that

f_{\mathbb{Q}}:{M^{*}_{2,\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Q}}

is a closed immersion with scheme theoretic image
X_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Hence
X^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}}
equals
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
inside
\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The schemes
X^{\prime}
and
X
are both integral closed subschemes of
\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
. Indeed the scheme-theoretic image
X^{\prime}
of the finite morphism
f:{M_{2}^{*}}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
has this property since
{M_{2}^{*}}
is integral,
while a computation (using Macaulay2) with the defining equations of
X
gives that
X
is integral as well. Furthermore
X^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
and
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
have the same generic point, since
X^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}}=X_{\mathbb{Q}}
inside
\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
and since the generic points of
X^{\prime}
and
X
are the generic points of
X^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
respectively. It follows that
X^{\prime}=X
as desired.

3.

We now apply Zariski’s main theorem to deduce that
f:{M_{2}^{*}}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
induces an isomorphism on its image. The noetherian schemes
{M_{2}^{*}}
and
X
are both integral, and a computation (using Macaulay2) with the defining equations of
X
shows that
X
is normal. As
X
is the scheme-theoretic image of
f
by step 2, we obtain that
f
induces a morphism

f:{M_{2}^{*}}\to X.

This is a birational morphism of integral schemes, since it extends
f_{\mathbb{Q}}:M^{*}_{2,\mathbb{Q}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
which is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-schemes as explained in step 2. Then, as
X
is normal, we can apply Zariski’s main theorem to deduce that
\mathcal{O}_{X}\overset{\sim}{\to}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{{M_{2}^{*}}}
. This proves that the finite morphism
f:{M_{2}^{*}}\to X
has to be an isomorphism.

4.

It remains to show that
f:{M_{2}^{*}}\overset{\sim}{\to}X
restricts to an isomorphism
M_{2}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y
. Write
V
for the boundary
{M_{2}^{*}}\setminus M_{2}
, and recall that
Z
is the union of the images of the five
B
-points of
X
obtained by permuting the coordinates of
(1,0,0,0,0)
. We now prove that

f(V)=Z.

It follows from
[
vdG88
, p.190-191]
and
[
SBT97
, p.289]
that
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
is given by five
\mathbb{Q}
-points of
{M_{2}^{*}}
and
f(V_{\mathbb{Q}})=Z_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Further,
Z
is the closure of
Z_{\mathbb{Q}}
in
X
and
[
Cha90
, Main Thm (vi)]
implies that
V
is the closure of
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
in
{M_{2}^{*}}
.
As
f
is a homeomorphism, we then deduce that
f(V)=\overline{f(V_{\mathbb{Q}})}=Z
.
Thus the isomorphism
f:{M_{2}^{*}}\overset{\sim}{\to}X
restricts to an isomorphism
M_{2}={M_{2}^{*}}\setminus V\overset{\sim}{\to}X\setminus Z=Y
as desired. This completes the proof of Proposition
11.5
. ∎

Proposition
11.5
implies that
Y
is a coarse Hilbert moduli scheme over
B
of the moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}_{2}
defined in (
11.6
). For what follows it will be crucial to specify an initial morphism
\mathcal{M}_{2}\to Y
. In the above proof of Proposition
11.5
, we constructed in step 1 the morphisms
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to^{\bar{\pi}}{M_{2}^{*}}\to^{f}\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
and we showed in step 4 that the restriction to
\mathcal{M}_{2}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
of the composition
f\bar{\pi}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}_{B}
defines an initial morphism

\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to Y.

(11.21)

Indeed
f
restricts to an isomorphism
M_{2}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y
by step 4 and the restriction of
\bar{\pi}
to
\mathcal{M}_{2}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}}
is an initial morphism
\mathcal{M}_{2}\to M_{2}
by
[
Cha90
, Main Thm (vi)]
. By construction
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to Y
is given by the five global sections of
\omega^{\otimes 2}
on
\mathcal{M}_{2}
corresponding via the
q
-expansion principle to the five Eisenstein series
E_{i}
for
\Gamma(2)
of weight 2 defined in
\mathsection
11.3.3
.

11.5
Heights

Let
Y
be as in (
1.2
) and define
B=\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}]
. Proposition
11.5
gives that
Y_{B}
is a coarse moduli scheme over
B
of the arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}_{B}
of principal level 2-structures with (
11.6
). The goal of this section is to prove the following result which explicitly bounds the logarithmic Weil height
h
on
Y\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
in terms of the height
h_{\phi}
.

Proposition 11.13
.

Any
P\in Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
satisfies
h(P)\leq 2h_{\phi}(P)+8^{8}\log(h_{\phi}+8)
.

Here
h_{\phi}
is the height on
Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\cong Y_{B}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
defined with respect to the initial morphism
\pi:\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y_{B}
in (
11.21
), and recall that the logarithmic Weil height
h
on projective space is normalized as in
[
BG06
, p.16]
. We shall discuss the bound at the end of Section
11.5
.

In the remaining of this section we prove Proposition
11.13
as follows. We first relate the height
h
to a Theta height
h_{\theta}
on
Y
by using the work of Hirzebruch
[
Hir76
]
and computations for Hilbert theta functions due to Götzky
[
G2̈8
]
, Gundlach
[
Gun63
]
and Lauter–Naehrig–Yang
[
LNY16
]
. Then we bound
h_{\theta}
in terms of
h_{\phi}
via the explicit height comparison of Pazuki
[
Paz12
]
which is based on ideas of Bost–David. Throughout this section we work over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
and we write
Y
for
Y_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
and
\mathbb{P}^{n}
for
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
where
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

Theta height on
Y
.

We can define a Theta height on
Y
by pulling back a Theta height on the coarse moduli space
A_{2,2}
over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
of principally polarized abelian varieties with a symplectic level two structure
[
FC90
, p.19]
. More precisely, we define the Theta height

h_{\theta}=\theta^{*}h:\ Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathbb{R}

where
\theta:Y\to\mathbb{P}^{9}
is the composition of the following morphisms: The morphism
Y\to A_{2,2}
induced on coarse moduli schemes by forgetting
\iota:\mathcal{O}\to\textup{End}(A)
and the morphism
A_{2,2}\to\mathbb{P}^{9}
defined by the fourth powers of the even Theta functions
\theta_{m}=\theta_{m}(\tau,0)
as in
[
vdG88
, p.217]
. The heights
h_{\theta}
and
h
on
Y
are closely related.

Lemma 11.14
.

Any
P\in Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
satisfies
h(P)\leq h_{\theta}(P).

Proof.

Let
x_{i}
be the pullback along
Y\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{4}
of the
i
-th coordinate function on
\mathbb{P}^{4}
, and let
z_{j}
be the pullback along
\theta:Y\to\mathbb{P}^{9}
of the
j
-th coordinate function on
\mathbb{P}^{9}
. Lemma
11.14
is a direct consequence of the following claim: On
Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
it holds

x_{i}^{4}=\pm\prod_{j\in J_{i}}z_{j}

(11.22)

where
J_{i}\subset\{0,\dotsc,9\}
with
|J_{i}|=4
. To prove this claim, we need to introduce some notation. Let
\mathbb{H}_{2}
be the Siegel upper half space of genus 2 on which the symplectic group
\textnormal{Sp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
acts as usual and write
\mathbb{H}=\{z\in\mathbb{C},\textnormal{im}(z)>0\}
. We denote by
\Gamma_{2}
the kernel of the projection
\textnormal{Sp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\to\textnormal{Sp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
and we denote by
\Gamma(2)
the kernel of the projection
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})\to\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
. Then we consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y(\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{M}_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{C}})]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\epsilon}}
\textstyle{A_{2,2}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{2,2}(\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{A}_{2,2}(\bar{\mathbb{C}})]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbb{H}_{2}/\Gamma_{2}}

(11.23)

which is obtained as follows:
The map
\phi_{\epsilon}
is the modular morphism of Lauter–Yang
[
LNY16
, p.153]
with
\epsilon=\tfrac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
. The two bijections in the middle come from the coarse moduli spaces
\pi_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to Y
and
\mathcal{A}_{2,2}\to A_{2,2}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{2}=(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}})_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2,2}
is the moduli stack over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
of principally polarized abelian schemes with a symplectic level two structure as in
[
FC90
, p.19]
. The two bijections on the right come from the usual uniformization of complex abelian varieties (with real multiplications), where
[\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})]\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
factors through the inverse of the map
\phi_{0}:\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{2}
defined in
[
LNY16
, p.153]
.

We now use the diagram to compute
x_{i}
in terms of the
z_{j}
. The discussion right after (
11.21
) provides that
\pi_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to Y
is given by the five global sections of the square
\omega^{\otimes 2}
of the Hodge bundle on
\mathcal{M}_{2}
corresponding via the
q
-expansion principle to the five Eisenstein series
E_{i}
for
\Gamma(2)
of weight 2 which are defined in
\mathsection
11.3.3
. Then the inverse
\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)\overset{\sim}{\to}Y(\mathbb{C})
of the composition of the isomorphisms
Y(\mathbb{C})\overset{\sim}{\to}[\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})]\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
in the diagram (
11.23
) is given by the five Eisenstein series
E_{i}
. Moreover, it holds

Y_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\textnormal{Proj}\bigl(\mathbb{C}[E_{i}]/(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{4})\bigl)\setminus Z_{\mathbb{C}},\quad{\textnormal{and}}\quad E_{0}=\theta_{01}\theta_{02}\theta_{03}\theta_{04}

(11.24)

by Hirzebruch’s computation in
[
Hir76
, p.166]
. Here
Z_{\mathbb{C}}
is given by the five permutations of
(1,0,\dotsc,0)
and
\theta_{ij}
is the theta function (viewed on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
) of Götzky
[
G2̈8
, (19)]
and Gundlach
[
Gun63
, p.233]
. We next express the
\theta_{ij}
, and then all Eisenstein series
E_{i}
, in terms of the Hilbert theta functions
\theta_{a,b}
for some
(a,b)\in(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})^{2}
, where we write
\theta_{a,b}
for the function
\theta_{a,b}^{\epsilon}
of Lauter–Naehrig–Yang
[
LNY16
, (2.5)]
defined with respect to the fundamental unit
\epsilon=\tfrac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
and where
\mathcal{O}
denotes the ring of integers of
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
. In the table displayed in
[
Gun63
, p.232]
, Gundlach computed all pairs
(\alpha,\beta)
which are relevant for the definition of the functions
\theta_{ij}
. For any pair
(\alpha,\beta)
in this table, it holds that
\alpha\beta\in\{0,1,-1\}
and thus the field trace
t=\textnormal{Tr}(\tfrac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{5}}\tfrac{ab}{4})
is zero for
(a,b)=(\alpha,\beta/\epsilon)
.
Then unwinding the definitions of the functions
\theta_{ij}
and
\theta_{a,b}
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
leads to

\theta_{ij}=e^{-2\pi it}\theta_{a,b}=\theta_{a,b}

(11.25)

for some
(a,b)\in(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})^{2}
. The group
\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})
acts transitively on the set of cusps of
\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
and recall from (
11.13
) that the Eisenstein series
E_{i}=E_{0}|_{A_{i}}
is obtained from
E_{0}
via the slash operator for some
A_{i}\in\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
. Thus, on combining (
11.24
) and (
11.25
) with the transformation formula for
\theta_{ij}^{8}
established by Götzky
[
G2̈8
, Lem 6]
, we obtain

E_{i}^{4}=\pm\prod\theta_{a,b}^{4}.

(11.26)

Here the product is taken over four distinct pairs
(a,b)\in(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})^{2}
. These four pairs are all even in the sense of
[
LNY16
, p.154]
since
E_{i}\neq 0
. For any even
(a,b)\in(\mathcal{O}/2\mathcal{O})^{2}
, Lauter–Naehrig–Yang
[
LNY16
,
\mathsection
2.5,
\mathsection
3]
showed that each Hilbert theta function
\theta_{a,b}
is (up to a sign) the pullback along
\phi_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)\to\mathbb{H}_{2}/\Gamma_{2}
of an even Theta function
\theta_{m}
. Thus the commutative diagram (
11.23
) gives that
\theta_{a,b}^{4}
identifies on
Y(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
with the pullback
z_{j}=\theta^{*}t_{j}
of a coordinate function
t_{j}
on
\mathbb{P}^{9}
along the composition
\theta:Y\to A_{2,2}\to\mathbb{P}^{9}
.
Then (
11.23
) and (
11.26
) imply the claim (
11.22
) since
x_{i}
identifies on
Y(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
with
E_{i}
by (
11.24
). This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

One can determine the sign of
E_{i}^{4}=\pm\prod\theta_{a,b}^{4}
in (
11.26
), and thus of
x_{i}^{4}=\pm\prod_{j\in J_{i}}z_{j}
in (
11.22
), by going into the proof of
[
G2̈8
, Lem 6]
and computing therein the sign in the transformation formula for five explicit
A_{i}\in\textnormal{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
defining the five
E_{i}=E_{0}|_{A_{i}}
.

Proof of Proposition
11.13
.

To prove an explicit height bound, we need to compare our normalizations of the heights with other normalizations. Recall from Section
3.4
that
h_{\phi}:Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathbb{R}
is the pullback of
h_{F}:\underline{A}_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathbb{R}
along the forgetful map

\phi_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}:Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\overset{\sim}{\to}^{\pi^{-1}}[\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})]\to\underline{A}_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})

(11.27)

of the coarse Hilbert moduli scheme
Y
defined right after (
3.2
), where
h_{F}
is the stable Faltings height with the metric normalized as in Faltings
[
Fal83
, p.354]
. Further, we recall that the logarithmic Weil height
h
on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
is defined as usual in Diophantine approximation with respect to the infinity norm at the infinite places (
[
BG06
, p.16]
). It holds

h_{F}=h^{\prime}_{F}-\log\pi,\quad h\leq h^{\prime}\leq h+\tfrac{1}{2}\log(n+1),

(11.28)

where
h_{F}^{\prime}
denotes the stable Faltings height with the metric normalized as in Deligne
[
Del85
, p.27]
and where
h^{\prime}
denotes the logarithmic Weil height on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
defined with respect to the usual Euclidean or Hermitian
l^{2}
norm at the infinite places.

We now deduce Proposition
11.13
by combining Lemma
11.14
with the explicit height comparison of Pazuki
[
Paz12
, Cor 1.3.2]
which is based on ideas of Bost–David.

Proof of Proposition
11.13
.

To prove the statement, it suffices to bound the Theta height
h_{\theta}
on
Y
since
h_{\theta}\geq h
by Lemma
11.14
. We first relate
h_{\theta}
to another Theta height
h^{\prime}_{\theta}
on
Y
which is the pullback of the Theta height defined in Pazuki
[
Paz12
]
. More precisely, applying
[
Paz12
, Def 2.6]
with
g=2
and
r=2
gives a height
h_{\Theta}:A_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathbb{R}
on the coarse moduli scheme
A_{2}
over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
of principally polarized abelian surfaces. Then we define

h^{\prime}_{\theta}=f^{*}h_{\Theta}:Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathbb{R}

where
f:Y\to A_{2}
is the composition of the morphism
Y\to A_{2,2}
induced on coarse moduli schemes by forgetting
\iota:\mathcal{O}\to\textup{End}(A)
and the morphism
A_{2,2}\to A_{2}
induced on coarse moduli schemes by forgetting the symplectic level 2-structure. The Theta height
h_{\Theta}
is determined by the 10 even Theta functions
\theta_{m}
, since any Theta function
\theta_{m}
is nonzero if and only if the Theta characteristic
m
is even. Thus (the proof of) (
11.28
) leads to

h_{\theta}\leq 4h^{\prime}_{\theta}\leq 4h_{\theta}+2\log 10.

(11.29)

We next compare the heights
h^{\prime}_{\theta}
and
h_{\phi}
on
Y
. It follows from (
11.27
) that the map
\phi_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}:Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\to\underline{A}_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
factors as
Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\to A_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\to\underline{A}_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
where
A_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\to\underline{A}_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
is induced by forgetting the polarization. Then, on combining (
11.28
) and (
11.29
) with the bound for
|h_{\Theta}-\tfrac{1}{2}h_{F}^{\prime}|
in Pazuki
[
Paz12
, Cor 1.3.2]
, we deduce

4h^{\prime}_{\theta}\leq 2h_{\phi}+8^{8}\log(h_{\phi}+8).

(11.30)

To simplify the shape of this estimate, we used here in addition Bost’s explicit lower bound
h^{\prime}_{F}\geq-\log(2\pi)
in
[
Bos96a
]
. Finally for any
P\in Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
the inequalities (
11.29
) and (
11.30
) give an upper bound for
h_{\theta}(P)\geq h(P)
as claimed in Proposition
11.13
. ∎

We now discuss the bounds. The constants
8^{8}
and
8
appearing in (
11.30
) and Proposition
11.13
crucially depend on the value at
g=2
and
r=2
of the function
C_{2}(g,r)
in Pazuki’s general result
[
Paz12
, Cor 1.3]
. We simplified both constants
8^{8}
and
8
and thus they can be improved up to a certain extent. However, the factor 2 in front of
h_{\phi}
is optimal in (
11.30
) by the height comparison
[
Paz12
, Cor 1.3]
and hence the factor 2 might also be optimal in Proposition
11.13
in view of the relation
x_{i}^{4}=\pm\prod z_{j}
in (
11.22
).

The bound in Proposition
11.13
is linear in
h_{\phi}
which is best possible in the sense that for any exponent
e<1
the bound
h\ll h^{e}_{\phi}
can not hold on
Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
. Indeed on combining
[
Paz12
, Cor 1.3]
and (
11.29
) with the relation
x_{i}^{4}=\pm\prod z_{j}
in (
11.22
), we see that such a bound already fails on the subset
Y^{0}(\mathbb{Q})\subset Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
which is an infinite set by (
11.3
), where
Y^{0}=Y\setminus\cup V_{+}(x_{i})
for
x_{i}
the
i
-th coordinate function on
\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\textnormal{Proj}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{i}])
.

11.6
Proof of Theorem
11.3
and the Corollary

In this section we first prove Theorem
11.3
by combining Propositions
11.5
and
11.13
. Then we deduce from Theorems
4.1
and
11.3
the Corollary stated in the introduction.

We continue the notation of Section
11.1
. Let
Y
be the variety over
\mathbb{Z}
defined in (
1.2
). We denote by
h
the logarithmic Weil height on
Y\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we write
\mathcal{M}
for the Hilbert moduli stack associated to
L=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
and we define
B=\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{30}]
.

Proof of Theorem
11.3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the moduli problem on
\mathcal{M}_{B}
of principal level 2-structures satisfying the condition (
11.6
). Proposition
11.5
gives that
Y_{B}
is a coarse moduli scheme over
B
of
\mathcal{P}
. Let
\pi:(\mathcal{M}_{B})_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y_{B}
be the initial morphism given in (
11.21
) and let
h_{\phi}
be the height on
Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\cong Y_{B}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
defined with respect to
\pi
. We claim that

B_{\mathcal{P}}=\emptyset,\quad|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}\leq 16\ \textnormal{ and }\ h(P)\leq 2h_{\phi}(P)+8^{8}\log(h_{\phi}(P)+8),\,P\in Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}).

Indeed on using exactly the same arguments as in the proof of Corollary
5.6
, we deduce from Proposition
5.5
that the branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}}
is empty. Further, as in (
10.8
) we find that the maximal number of level
\mathcal{P}
-structures over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
is bounded by
|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}\leq 16
, while the height bound is provided by Proposition
11.13
. Now, the claim implies Theorem
11.3
after extending
Y_{B}
to a coarse moduli scheme over
\mathbb{Z}
. The details are as follows: We extend
Y_{B}
to a coarse moduli scheme over
\mathbb{Z}
via the naive extension
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{M}
defined right above (
5.6
) and we use the
initial morphism
\pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to Y_{B}
given by the composition

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\to}(\mathcal{M}_{B})_{\mathcal{P}}\to^{\pi}Y_{B}.

(11.31)

Here
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\to}(\mathcal{M}_{B})_{\mathcal{P}}
is induced by the identity. Thus the branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
equals
B_{\mathcal{P}}
, the height defined with respect to
\pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\to Y_{B}
coincides on
Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\cong Y_{B}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
with the height
h_{\phi}
defined with respect to
\pi:(\mathcal{M}_{B})_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y_{B}
, and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is an arithmetic moduli problem on
\mathcal{M}
since
\mathcal{P}
is an arithmetic moduli problem on
\mathcal{M}_{B}
. We also notice that
|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}=|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Hence the claim proves that Theorem
11.3
holds with
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\pi=\pi^{\prime}
.
∎

We now deduce from Theorems
4.1
and
11.3
the Corollary stated in the introduction.

Proof of Corollary.

Let
S
be a finite set of rational primes. Theorem
11.3
gives that
Y_{B}
is a coarse moduli scheme of an arithmetic moduli problem
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{M}
with
|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}\leq 16
such that the branch locus
B_{\mathcal{P}}
is empty and such that each
P\in Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
satisfies

h(P)\leq 2h_{\phi}(P)+8^{8}\log(h_{\phi}(P)+8).

Here
h_{\phi}
is the height on
Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\cong Y_{B}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
defined in (
3.2
) with respect to the initial morphism
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y_{B}
given in (
11.21
). Base change properties of the height
h_{\phi}
assure that
h_{\phi}
coincides on
Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\subset Y(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
with the height
h_{\phi}
on
Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
obtained by applying the definition at the end of Section
3.4
with
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}\to Y_{B}
; notice that
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})-S=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
is a nonempty open subscheme of
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then an application of Theorem
4.1
with
T=B
,
S=\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
and
Z=\emptyset
gives that any
P\in Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
satisfies

h_{\phi}(P)\leq c_{1}N_{U}^{24},\quad\textnormal{and}\quad|Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})|\leq c_{2}|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}N_{U}^{e_{2}}\Delta\log(3\Delta)^{3}.

Here the constants are
c_{1}=7^{7^{14}}
,
c_{2}=9^{9^{18}}
and
e_{2}=4^{16}
, while
\Delta=5
and
N_{U}=\prod_{p\in S^{\prime}}p
for
S^{\prime}=S\cup\{2,3,5\}
. As
|\mathcal{P}|_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}\leq 16
and
N_{U}\leq 30N_{S}
, the displayed bounds show that the Corollary holds with
e=10^{12}
and
c=10^{e}
as claimed. This completes the proof. ∎

11.7
Diophantine equations and the Clebsch–Klein surfaces
