In this section we first collect basic results for the Clebsch–Klein surfaces and the Diophantine equations (
1.1
) and (
1.3
). Then we combine these results with the Corollary stated in the introduction to deduce Corollaries
11.1
and
11.2
giving explicit bounds for the solutions of (
1.1
) and (
1.3
). Finally, we discuss lower bounds for rational points of bounded height (
\mathsection
11.7.2
) and the degeneration of
S
-integral points (
\mathsection
11.7.3
).

We continue the notation introduced in Section
11.1
. Let
Y
be the variety over
\mathbb{Z}
defined in (
1.2
) and let
U
be the variety over
\mathbb{Z}
defined in (
1.6
). For any finite set
S
of rational primes, we recall that
\mathbb{Z}_{S}=\mathbb{Z}[1/N_{S}]
where
N_{S}=\prod_{p\in S}p
. We first state basic lemmas relating the solutions of (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) with the
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
-points of
Y
and
U
respectively. Sending
x
to
-x
defines an action of the group
\{\pm 1\}
on the set of solutions of (
1.3
).

Lemma 11.15
.

The set of solutions of (
1.3
) modulo
\{\pm 1\}
identifies with
Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
.

Here the identification comes from the usual description of
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
-points on projective space. The identification in the following lemma also comes from this description.

Lemma 11.16
.

The set of solutions of (
1.1
) identifies with
U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
.

We write
\mathbb{P}^{4}
for
\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. In Section
11.1
we defined the logarithmic Weil height
h
on the sets of solutions of (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) and also on the subsets
Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
and
U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
of
\mathbb{P}^{4}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Lemma 11.17
.

The identifications in Lemmas
11.15
and
11.16
are compatible with
h
.

In the remaining of this section we prove inter alia the above lemmas and we explicitly construct the open immersion
U\hookrightarrow Y
over
\mathbb{Z}[1/3]
appearing in Lemma
11.4
. The proofs use standard computations. In particular we shall use the usual identification

\mathbb{P}^{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}.

(11.32)

Here the group
\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
acts diagonally on the set
\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}
of
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{5}
with
\gcd(x_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}

and the bijection (
11.32
) comes from the standard description of
\mathbb{P}^{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
in terms of isomorphism classes of surjections
\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{5}\to\mathbb{Z}_{S}
using that
\textnormal{Pic}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})=0
. We first prove Lemma
11.15
.

Proof of Lemma
11.15
.

Let
P
a
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
-point of
\mathbb{P}^{4}
corresponding via (
11.32
) to some
[x]
in
\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
, and let
Q
be the
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
-point of
\mathbb{P}^{4}
given by
e_{1}=(1,0,\dotsc,0)
. For any closed point
p\in\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
, the images in
\mathbb{P}^{4}
of the sections
P
and
Q
have empty intersection in the fiber
\mathbb{P}^{4}_{p}
if and only if the image of
x
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{5}
does not lie in the line
L_{p}=\mathbb{F}_{p}\cdot e_{1}
where
\mathbb{F}_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{S}/p\mathbb{Z}_{S}
. It follows that the identification (
11.32
) restricts to a bijection

Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{Y}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}

for
\Sigma_{Y}
the set of
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{5}
with
\gcd(x_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
such that
\sigma_{2}(x)=0=\sigma_{4}(x)
and such that for all rational primes
p\notin S
the image of
x
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{5}
does not lie in any of the five permutations of the line
L_{p}
. Let
\Sigma_{\sigma}
be the set of
x\in\Sigma
with
\sigma_{2}(x)=0=\sigma_{4}(x)
for
\Sigma
as in (
1.3
). Multiplying with the denominators of the
x_{i}
and dividing by the
\gcd
defines a bijection

\Sigma_{Y}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{\sigma}/\{\pm 1\}

(11.33)

which composed with
Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{Y}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
gives an identification
Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{\sigma}/\{\pm 1\}
as desired. The remaining of this proof contains the details of the definition of the bijection (
11.33
). Suppose that
x=(x_{i})
lies in
\Sigma_{Y}
. As the
x_{i}
are in
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
with
\gcd(x_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
, there exists
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
which is unique up to
\pm 1
such that
y=ux
lies in
\mathbb{Z}^{5}
with
\gcd(y_{i})=1
. The scalar multiple
y=ux
of
x
satisfies again the homogeneous equations
\sigma_{2}=0=\sigma_{4}
. Moreover, if
p\notin S
is a rational prime then
\textnormal{ord}_{p}(u)=0
and thus our assumption
x\in\Sigma_{Y}
assures that the image of
y
in
(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{5}\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}^{5}
is not a scalar multiple of a standard basis vector of
(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{5}
. Hence
y
lies in
\Sigma_{\sigma}
. On using that
u
is unique up to
\pm 1
, we see that
x\mapsto y
defines an injective map
\Sigma_{Y}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}\to\Sigma_{\sigma}/\{\pm 1\}
which is surjective since
\Sigma_{\sigma}\subset\Sigma_{Y}
. Now, we define (
11.33
) by
x\mapsto y
. This completes the proof of Lemma
11.15
.
∎

We now deduce Lemma
11.16
from the identification
\mathbb{P}^{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
in (
11.32
).

Proof of Lemma
11.16
.

Recall that
U=V\setminus\cup V_{+}(z_{i})
for
V\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
as in (
1.6
).
We first compute the image of
U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
under the identification (
11.32
). Let
P
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
-point of
\mathbb{P}^{4}=\textnormal{Proj}(\mathbb{Z}[z_{i}])
corresponding via (
11.32
) to
[x]
in
\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
. Then
P
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
-point of
\mathbb{P}^{4}\setminus V_{+}(z_{i})
if and only if
x_{i}
lies in
\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
. This implies that (
11.32
) restricts to a bijection

U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{U}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}

where
\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
acts diagonally on the set
\Sigma_{U}
of
x\in(\mathbb{Z}^{\times}_{S})^{5}
satisfying
\sum x_{i}=0=\sum x_{i}^{3}
.
Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the set of solutions of (
1.1
). To identify
\Sigma_{U}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
with
\Sigma^{\prime}
, we take
x\in\Sigma_{U}
and we define
y_{i}=-x_{i}/x_{4}
for
i\leq 3
. As
\sum y_{i}=1=\sum y_{i}^{3}
, we see that
y=(y_{i})
lies in
\Sigma^{\prime}
. It follows that
x\mapsto y
defines a bijection
\Sigma_{U}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma^{\prime}
which composed with
U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{U}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
gives an identification
U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma^{\prime}
as desired. This completes the proof of Lemma
11.16
.
∎

Next, we show that the two identifications constructed in the above proofs are compatible with
h
. For each
[x]\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
we define
h([x])
as the logarithmic Weil height of
x\in\mathbb{P}^{4}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
. Then the identification
\mathbb{P}^{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
in (
11.32
) is compatible with
h
.

Proof of Lemma
11.17
.

In the proof of Lemma
11.15
we identified (modulo
\pm 1
) the set of solutions
\Sigma_{\sigma}
of (
1.3
) with
Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
via the inverse
\Sigma_{\sigma}/\{\pm 1\}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
of the composition

Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{Y}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{\sigma}/\{\pm 1\}.

Here
Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{Y}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
is compatible with
h
since it is the restriction of (
11.32
), while
\Sigma_{Y}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{\sigma}/\{\pm 1\}
is induced by
x\mapsto y
which is compatible with
h
since
x
and
y=ux
are equal in
\mathbb{P}^{4}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
and since
h(x)=\max_{i}\log|x_{i}|
for any
x\in\mathbb{P}^{4}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
with
x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\gcd(x_{i})=1
. Thus the identification
\Sigma_{\sigma}/\{\pm 1\}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
is compatible with
h
as desired.

We now go into the proof of Lemma
11.16
. Therein we identified the set
\Sigma^{\prime}
of solutions of (
1.1
) with
U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
via the inverse
\Sigma^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
of the composition

U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{U}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma^{\prime}.

Here again, the bijection
U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma_{U}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}
is compatible with
h
since it is the restriction of (
11.32
). The bijection
\Sigma_{U}/\mathbb{Z}_{S}^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\to}\Sigma^{\prime}
is induced by
x\mapsto y
which is compatible with
h
by the following computation: As
x
and
y^{\prime}=(y,-1)
are equal in
\mathbb{P}^{4}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
, we obtain that
h(x)=h(y^{\prime})=h((y,1))=h(y)
. Hence the identification
\Sigma^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
is compatible with
h
as desired. This completes the proof of Lemma
11.17
.
∎

Finally we construct the open immersion
\varphi:U\hookrightarrow Y
over
\mathbb{Z}[1/3]
of Lemma
11.4
.

Proof of Lemma
11.4
.

Consider the open subscheme
Y^{0}=Y\setminus\cup V_{+}(x_{i})
of
Y
. Any isomorphism
U\overset{\sim}{\to}Y^{0}
induces an open immersion
U\hookrightarrow Y
. To construct an isomorphism
U\overset{\sim}{\to}Y^{0}
over
\mathbb{Z}[1/3]
, we first explicitly describe the schemes
U=V\setminus\cup V_{+}(z_{i})
and
Y^{0}
inside
\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. On using that
U=D_{+}(\prod z_{i})\subset V
,
we see that
t_{i}=z_{i}/z_{0}
satisfies

U=\textnormal{Spec}\bigl(\mathbb{Z}[t_{i},t_{i}^{-1}]/(u,v)\bigl)\subset\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}.

(11.34)

Here
u=\sigma_{1}(t_{i})=\sum t_{i}
and
v=\sum t_{i}^{3}
with both sums taken over all
i\in\{0,\dotsc,4\}
. Analogously, on using that
Y^{0}=D_{+}(\prod x_{i})\subset Y
, we deduce that
s_{i}=x_{i}/x_{0}
satisfies

Y^{0}=\textnormal{Spec}\left(\mathbb{Z}[s_{i},s_{i}^{-1}]/\bigl(\sigma_{2}(s_{i}),\sigma_{4}(s_{i})\bigl)\right)\subset\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}.

In the remaining of this proof, we work over
R=\mathbb{Z}[1/3]
and we use the descriptions of
U\subset\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
Y^{0}\subset\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
to show that
s_{i}\mapsto 1/t_{i}
defines an isomorphism of
R
-schemes:

\varphi:U_{R}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y^{0}_{R}.

Let
I
be the ideal of
A=R[s_{i},s_{i}^{-1}]
generated by
\sigma_{2}(s_{i}),\sigma_{4}(s_{i})
and let
J
be the ideal of
B=R[t_{i},t_{i}^{-1}]
generated by
u,v
. To see that
s_{i}\mapsto 1/t_{i}
defines an isomorphism
A/I\overset{\sim}{\to}B/J
of
R
-algebras, we recall the Newton identity
3\sigma_{3}=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{1}p_{2}+p_{3}
for
p_{n}=\sum y_{i}^{n}
and we observe that
\tau\sigma_{2}(s_{i})=\sigma_{3}(t_{i})
and
\tau\sigma_{4}(s_{i})=u
where
s_{i}=1/t_{i}
and
\tau=\prod t_{i}
. Thus
s_{i}\mapsto 1/t_{i}
sends
I
inside
J
since
3
is invertible in
R
, and
t_{i}\mapsto 1/s_{i}
sends
J
inside
I
. It follows that
s_{i}\mapsto 1/t_{i}
indeed defines an isomorphism
A/I\overset{\sim}{\to}B/J
of
R
-algebras, and hence an isomorphism
\varphi:U_{R}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y^{0}_{R}
of
R
-schemes, whose inverse is given by
t_{i}\mapsto 1/s_{i}
.

It remains to prove the height inequalities for any point
P\in U(\mathbb{Q})
. We view
P
inside
\mathbb{P}^{4}(\mathbb{Q})
via
U\hookrightarrow V\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{4}
. Then the description of
U
in (
11.34
) shows that
P\in\mathbb{P}^{4}(\mathbb{Q})
is defined by some
x\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}
with
x_{1}=1
and
x_{i}\neq 0
. There exists
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{5}
with
\gcd(a_{i})=1
and
a_{i}\neq 0
such that
x_{i}=a_{i}/a_{0}
. It holds that
h(P)=\log\max_{i}|a_{i}|
and then we compute

h(\varphi(P))\leq\max_{i}\log|a_{i}^{-1}\prod a_{j}|\leq 4h(P).

To verify the converse height inequality, we now use the displayed description of
Y^{0}
. Then an application of the above arguments with
\varphi^{-1}:Y^{0}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\to}U_{R}
and
Q=\varphi(P)
shows that
h(P)=h(\varphi^{-1}(Q))
is at most
4h(Q)=4h(\varphi(P))
. Thus the height inequalities hold for any
P\in U(\mathbb{Q})
as desired. This completes the proof of Lemma
11.4
.
∎

11.7.1
Proof of Corollaries
11.1
and
11.2

We continue the notation of the previous section. For any finite set
S
of rational primes, the (proof of the) Corollary stated in the introduction gives that each
P\in Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
satisfies

4h(P)\leq c(\tfrac{1}{3}N_{S})^{24}\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad|Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})|\leq(\tfrac{c}{3}N_{S})^{e}

(11.35)

for
e=10^{12}
and
c=10^{e}
. We now deduce Corollaries
11.1
and
11.2
by combining the results in (
11.35
) with the basic lemmas collected in the previous section.

Proof of Corollary
11.1
.

In Lemma
11.15
we identified (modulo
\pm 1
) the set of solutions of (
1.3
) with the set
Y(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
, and Lemma
11.17
assures that this identification is compatible with the Weil height
h
. Thus (
11.35
) proves Corollary
11.1
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
11.2
.

Recall that
\Sigma^{\prime}
denotes the set of solutions of (
1.1
) and define
T=S\cup\{3\}
. Lemma
11.17
gives that the identification
\Sigma^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
constructed in Lemma
11.16
is compatible with the Weil height
h
. Then we deduce

|\Sigma^{\prime}|=|U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})|\leq|Y(\mathbb{Z}_{T})|\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\sup_{x\in\Sigma^{\prime}}h(x)=\sup_{P\in U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})}h(P)\leq 4\sup_{Q\in Y(\mathbb{Z}_{T})}h(Q).

Here we exploited that
U(\mathbb{Z}_{S})\subseteq U(\mathbb{Z}_{T})=U_{\mathbb{Z}[1/3]}(\mathbb{Z}_{T})
which follows since
U
is separated over the terminal object
\mathbb{Z}
and
3
is invertible in the dense open
\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}_{T})\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}_{S})
, and we used the open immersion
\varphi:U_{\mathbb{Z}[1/3]}\to Y_{\mathbb{Z}[1/3]}
of Lemma
11.4
with
h(P)\leq 4h(\varphi(P))
for all
P\in U(\mathbb{Q})
. An application of (
11.35
) with
S=T
gives explicit bounds for
|Y(\mathbb{Z}_{T})|
and
\sup_{Q\in Y(\mathbb{Z}_{T})}h(Q)
in terms of
N_{T}
. As
N_{T}\leq 3N_{S}
, these explicit bounds combined with the displayed inequalities show that Corollary
11.2
holds with
e=10^{12}
and
c=10^{e}
.
∎

11.7.2
Lower bounds for the number of rational points of bounded height

On using a secant-tangent process, Slater–Swinnerton-Dyer
[
SSD98
]
established for certain smooth cubic surfaces the lower bound predicted by Manin’s conjecture (
[
FMT89
]
) on rational points of bounded height. In this section we give the basic computations which show that their result directly implies the lower bounds in (
11.3
).

We continue our notation. Let
U\subset V
be as in (
1.6
) and let
\rho
be the Picard rank of the smooth projective surface
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
. We first consider the
\mathbb{Q}
-points of
U
of bounded height.

Claim: For any
b\in\mathbb{R}
with
b\to\infty
it holds

|\{P\in U(\mathbb{Q}),h(P)\leq\log b\}|\gg b(\log b)^{\rho-1}.

Here the implied constant depends only on
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The claim has the following two consequences. Firstly, it proves the lower bounds in (
11.3
) when combined with the isomorphism
U_{\mathbb{Q}}\overset{\sim}{\to}Y^{0}_{\mathbb{Q}}
constructed in the proof of Lemma
11.4
for
Y^{0}=Y\setminus\cup_{i}V_{+}(x_{i})
.
Secondly, the claim and (the proofs of) Lemmas
11.16
and
11.17
show for each
b\in\mathbb{R}
with
b\to\infty
that

|\{x\in\mathcal{S},h(x)\leq\log b\}|\gg b(\log b)^{\rho-1},\ \mathcal{S}=\{x\in(\mathbb{Q}^{\times})^{4},\sum x_{i}^{3}=1=\sum x_{i}\}.

(11.36)

Here for each
x\in\mathcal{S}
we denote by
h(x)
the logarithmic Weil height of
x\in\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
and the implied constant depends again only on
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The following proof shows that the lower bounds in the claim and in (
11.36
) are both optimal if Manin’s conjecture holds for the
\mathbb{Q}
-points of the smooth cubic surface defined by
\sum x_{i}^{3}-(\sum x_{i})^{3}
inside
\mathbb{P}^{3}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Proof of the Claim.

To identify
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
with a smooth cubic surface in
\mathbb{P}^{3}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, we consider
f=\sum x_{i}^{3}-(\sum x_{i})^{3}
in
A=\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dotsc,x_{4}]
and
p_{3}=\sum z_{i}^{3}
in
B=\mathbb{Z}[z_{0},\dotsc,z_{4}]
. Then
x_{i}\mapsto z_{i}
defines an isomorphism of rings
A/(f)\overset{\sim}{\to}B/(\sigma_{1},p_{3})
with inverse given by
z_{i}\mapsto x_{i}
for
i\geq 1
and
z_{0}\mapsto-\sum x_{i}
. This induces an isomorphism of varieties over
\mathbb{Z}
:

\tau:V^{\prime}=\textnormal{Proj}\bigl(B/(f)\bigl)\overset{\sim}{\to}V,\quad h(P)\leq h(\tau(P))\leq h(P)+\log 8,\ P\in V^{\prime}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}),

(11.37)

where
h
denotes the logarithmic Weil height on
V^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
V\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. We now work over
\mathbb{Q}
. To simplify notation we write
V
for
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
V^{\prime}
for
V^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. As
V^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is a smooth cubic surface with two rational skew lines, it satisfies all assumptions in
[
SSD98
, Thm 1]
of Slater–Swinnerton-Dyer. Thus for each
b\in\mathbb{R}
with
b\to\infty
their result gives

|\{P\in U^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q}),h(P)\leq\log b\}|\gg b(\log b)^{\rho-1},

(11.38)

where
U^{\prime}\subset V^{\prime}
is obtained by removing all the lines from
V^{\prime}
and where the implied constant depends only on
V\cong V^{\prime}
. To relate
U^{\prime}
with
U
, we consider the 15 lines
\Delta_{n}
on
V
given by the permutations of the line
z_{0}+z_{1}=0
,
z_{2}+z_{3}=0
,
z_{4}=0
and we consider for
\epsilon=\tfrac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
the 12 lines
L_{i}
on
V
given by the permutations (fixing
z_{0}
) of the line

z_{1}+\epsilon z_{2}+z_{3}=0,\ \epsilon z_{1}+z_{2}+z_{4}=0,\ -\epsilon(z_{1}+z_{2})+z_{0}=0.

Notice that
\tau^{-1}(L_{i})
and
\tau^{-1}(\Delta_{n})
are both lines inside
V^{\prime}
, the lines
L_{i}
have no
\mathbb{Q}
-points since
\epsilon
does not lie in
\mathbb{Q}
, and the variety
V^{\prime}
contains exactly 27 lines since it is a smooth cubic surface. Then, on using the Newton identity for
p_{3}
, we compute

U=V\setminus(\cup\Delta_{n})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\tau:U^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})\overset{\sim}{\to}(U\setminus\cup L_{i})(\mathbb{Q})=U(\mathbb{Q}).

(11.39)

Thus (
11.37
) and (
11.38
) prove the claim. Furthermore, if Manin’s conjecture holds for
V^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})
, then the lower bound (
11.38
) is optimal and hence the claim is optimal as well; indeed (
11.37
) assures that the bijection
\tau^{-1}:U(\mathbb{Q})=(U\setminus\cup L_{i})(\mathbb{Q})\overset{\sim}{\to}U^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})
injects the set
\{P\in U(\mathbb{Q}),h(P)\leq\log b\}
into
\{P\in U^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q}),h(P)\leq\log b\}
for all
b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
∎

11.7.3
Degeneration of
S
-integral points

Corvaja–Zannier
[
CZ04
,
CZ06
,
CZ10
]
proved in particular the degeneration of
S
-integral points on smooth cubic surfaces minus two completely reducible hyperplane sections. In this section we give the basic computations which show that their result directly implies the degeneration of
S
-integral points on the affine variety defined in (
11.4
).

We continue our notation. Denote by
V=V_{\mathbb{Q}}
the projective variety over
\mathbb{Q}
defined by (
1.6
), and for
i\neq j
consider the divisor
D=V_{+}(z_{i})\cup V_{+}(z_{j})
of
V
. We again identify
V
with the smooth cubic surface
V^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}_{\mathbb{Q}}
defined by (
11.37
). After restricting the inverse
\tau^{-1}:V\overset{\sim}{\to}V^{\prime}
of the isomorphism in (
11.37
) to the open subvariety
V\setminus D
, we obtain

\tau^{-1}:V\setminus D\overset{\sim}{\to}V^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime},\quad D^{\prime}=\tau^{-1}(D).

To determine the divisor
D^{\prime}
of
V^{\prime}
, we denote by
\{\Delta_{n}\}
the 15 lines defined in
\mathsection
11.7.2
. On using the same arguments as in (
11.39
), we compute that
V_{+}(z_{i})
is the union of the three lines in
\{\Delta_{n}\}
with
z_{i}=0
. Then
D=\cup\Delta_{n}
is the union of six lines
\Delta_{n}
and we find that

D^{\prime}=\cup\tau^{-1}(\Delta_{n})

consists of six lines
\tau^{-1}(\Delta_{n})
lying in two planes such that no three of them intersect. Hence an application of Corvaja–Zannier
[
CZ10
, Thm 1]
with
V^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime}
gives the following: For any number field
K
and each finite set
S
of places of
K
, the set of
S
-integral points of
V^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}
, and thus of
V\setminus D
, is not Zariski dense. Moreover
[
CZ10
, Thm 9]
of Corvaja–Zannier shows inter alia that this statement is optimal in the following sense: For any line
\Delta_{n}
inside
D
define
D_{0}=D\setminus\Delta_{n}
and
D^{\prime}_{0}=D^{\prime}\setminus\tau^{-1}(\Delta_{n})
. Then there exist
K
and
S
such that the set of
S
-integral points of
V^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}_{0}
, and thus of
V\setminus D_{0}
, is in fact Zariski dense.

rafaelvonkanel@gmail.com
